# Performance Monitor Darling — Headless Edition

Darling is the headless, centralized edition of Performance Monitor: a 24/7 service (a Windows service, or Linux with systemd or Docker Compose) that collects from your SQL Servers into a central PostgreSQL (optionally TimescaleDB) store, plus a detached desktop viewer that reads that store. No desktop app has to stay open for collection to happen, and every viewer seat reads the same central data.

**Running an install day to day?** [Running Darling day to day](../docs/darling/admin.md) has the steps to add or remove a server, change a password, rotate a token, pause collection, upgrade and uninstall.

It runs the **same monitoring brain as the Lite edition** — one shared codebase, two storage engines:

- `PerformanceMonitor.Collectors` owns every collector definition (more than 40 for SQL Server and more than 25 for PostgreSQL): the exact query sent to monitored servers, the result-row mappings, the delta rules, the default cadences and retention horizons, and the ignored-wait-types list. Lite writes those rows to DuckDB; Darling writes the same rows to PostgreSQL via binary COPY. Each definition declares which engine it targets, and a collector never runs against the other one — see [PostgreSQL targets](#postgresql-targets).
- `PerformanceMonitor.Alerting` owns the shared alert engine — the same thresholds, edge-trigger gates, cooldowns, and dedup fingerprints Lite uses.
- The analysis/recommendations pipeline (the same inference engine behind both apps' Recommendations tabs and the `analyze_server` MCP tool) runs on a schedule inside the service.

A collector, alert, or analysis change lands once in the shared libraries and both editions get it. A Darling install monitoring a server even derives the **same `server_id`** Lite would for that server, because the identity rule (`host[:database][:RO]`, hashed) is shared too.

---

## When to Choose Darling vs. Lite

| | **Lite** | **Darling** |
|---|---|---|
| Collection runs | While the desktop app is open (or in the tray) | 24/7 as a Windows service, or on Linux with systemd or Docker Compose |
| Data lives | Locally per seat (DuckDB + Parquet) | Centrally (PostgreSQL / TimescaleDB) |
| Execution plans | Not stored (fetched live when you view a query) | Captured and stored, TOAST-compressed (`capturePlans`, default on) |
| Viewers | The app is the viewer | Any number of viewer seats read the central store |
| Setup | Download and run | On Windows, extract the release zip and run `install-darling.ps1` (on Linux, see [Run on Linux](../docs/darling-quick-start.md#run-on-linux-docker-compose-or-systemd)). The shipped default is the bundled store (managed mode), so you provision no database. To use your own PostgreSQL, provision it and set its connection string. |
| Best for | Quick triage, consultants, a handful of servers | Always-on team monitoring, larger estates, one shared store |
| Configuration | Settings UI | `darling.json` seeds the first start. After that, the Viewer edits servers, alert settings and notification routes in the store. The web dashboard's Admin page edits a server in place and the agent channel of a notification route, for a sign-in that can make changes. Its Alert Settings tab is read-only. |

Nothing is installed on the monitored SQL Servers by either edition beyond two lightweight Extended Events ring-buffer sessions and, when it is unset, a one-time `blocked process threshold` bootstrap (see [What the Service Does on Monitored Servers](../docs/darling/monitored-servers.md#what-the-service-does-on-monitored-servers)).

## Configuration Reference

All sections except `postgres` and `servers` are optional — omit a section (or any key) to get the defaults listed here. Defaults deliberately mirror a fresh Lite install.

### Where each setting lives: first-run seed or file only

`darling.json` does two jobs. Some keys are read from the file at every start. Others are only a **first-run seed**. The first time the service starts against a store with no settings, it copies them into the store. After that the store holds the only copy that counts, and the service replaces the file's value with the store's on every load. If you change a seeded key in the file and restart, nothing changes. Each seeded section is copied once, only while its own store table is empty.

| Keys | Where they live | Where to change them after the first start |
|---|---|---|
| `alerts` (every key), `analysis` (`enabled`, `intervalMinutes`, `notificationsEnabled`, `notifySeverity`) | First-run seed | The Viewer's Settings window, or the MCP tool `update_alert_settings` |
| `smtp` (including `emailSendResolutions`), and `webhooks` (the Teams, Slack, generic and PagerDuty keys, including `slackSendResolutions`, `teamsSendResolutions` and `genericSendResolutions`) | First-run seed | The Viewer's Settings > Notifications, or the MCP tool `update_notification_channels`. The send-resolutions switches are also set by `update_alert_settings` |
| `capturePlans`, `queryStoreBackfillEnabled`, `queryStoreTextBudgetMb` (default `64`), `queryStoreFetchTopN` (default `100`), `maxConcurrentSweeps` (default `4`) | First-run seed | The Viewer's Settings window, or the MCP tool `update_service_flags` |
| `planContentRetentionDays` (default `21`), `composeStatementTimeoutSeconds` (default `60`), `planXmlCompression` (default `"gzip"`) | First-run seed | Neither the Viewer nor MCP edits them. Change the column in `config.config_service` (`plan_content_retention_days`, `compose_statement_timeout_seconds`, `plan_xml_compression`). The statement timeout reaches the `viewer` and `mcp` roles on the next service start |
| `mcp.enabled`, `mcp.port`, `web.enabled`, `web.port` | First-run seed | The Viewer's Settings window, live and with no restart. On a box with no Viewer, `--enable-mcp` and `--enable-web` |
| `servers` | First-run seed, copied once while the server registry is empty. A server added to the file later is ignored | The Viewer, the web dashboard's Admin page, or the MCP tools `add_servers` and `edit_server`. The one exception is the `remediation*` and `hypotheticalIndex*` login keys, which the service syncs from the file at every start |
| `analysis.uncorroboratedRoute` | Both. The store's value wins when it is set. Otherwise the file's value is used | The store value, from the same places as the rest of `analysis`. Clear it to hand the choice back to the file |
| `postgres`, `collectSchemaChangeEvents`, `rawChunkIntervalReconcileEnabled` (default `true`), `procedureStatsPlanCycleInterval`, `queryStatsDeferredPlanFetch`, `procedureStatsDeferredPlanFetch`, `allowedAwsRoles`, `peers`, `heartbeat`, `reads`, `forcePlanBot`, `analyzer`, and every other key in `mcp` and `web` (including `mcp.network` and `web.network`) | File only | Edit `darling.json` and restart the service |

### postgres

Two mutually exclusive modes — setting `managed: true` together with `connectionString` (or with either login setting below) is a validation error:

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `managed` | `false` | `true` runs the bundled PostgreSQL + TimescaleDB (Windows only; see [Managed Bundled PostgreSQL](#managed-bundled-postgresql)). The connection string is derived, never configured. |
| `port` | `5641` | Managed mode only: the loopback port the bundled server listens on. Deliberately uncommon so it coexists with any PostgreSQL (5432) already on the machine. |
| `dataDirectory` | *(null)* | Managed mode only: the cluster's data directory. `null` means `%ProgramData%\PerformanceMonitorDarling\pg`. |
| `connectAs` | `"admin"` | Managed mode only: which least-privilege role the Viewer connects as — `"admin"` (reads everything + manages mute rules and dismisses alerts) or `"viewer"` (read-only; those write actions are hidden/disabled). See [Security & Least-Privilege Roles](#security--least-privilege-roles). Ignored in bring-your-own mode (the connection string picks the role). |
| `connectionString` | *(required unless managed)* | Npgsql connection string for a store you provision yourself, e.g. `Host=localhost;Port=5432;Username=darling;Password=...;Database=darling`. You own that cluster's settings: if it has TimescaleDB, size its [background workers](#background-workers-sizing-an-unmanaged-store-and-what-happens-if-you-dont) — managed mode does this for you, this mode does not. |
| `webConnectionString` | *(unset)* | Bring-your-own only: the web dashboard's store login, normally the `viewer` role [`provision-roles.sql`](#security--least-privilege-roles) creates, e.g. `Host=localhost;Port=5432;Username=viewer;Password=...;Database=darling`. Same forms as `connectionString` (a literal, or an `env:NAME` / `file:/path` reference), resolved when the dashboard starts, so an unreadable reference keeps the dashboard down with the reason logged rather than stopping collection. **Unset, the dashboard connects as the owner** (`connectionString`) and loses the `viewer` role's secret-column carve, narrow write grants and `statement_timeout` backstop; the service warns at startup. Not needed on the compose distribution's own store, where the service provisions the role itself, and a validation error in managed mode ([#3914](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3914)). |
| `mcpConnectionString` | *(unset)* | The same for the MCP server: normally the `mcp` role `provision-roles.sql` creates; unset, MCP connects as the owner, with the same warning. |

### servers (array, at least one entry)

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `name` | `""` | Display name; falls back to `host` |
| `host` | *(required)* | Server/instance to monitor |
| `engine` | `"sqlserver"` | `"sqlserver"` or `"postgres"` (`postgresql` / `pg` / `aurora-postgresql` also accepted). Configuration rather than something probed, because it decides which driver builds the connection string before there is a connection to ask. An omitted or unrecognized value means SQL Server, so every existing `darling.json` keeps its exact present behaviour — see [PostgreSQL targets](#postgresql-targets) |
| `port` | *(driver default)* | PostgreSQL targets on a non-default port. SQL Server carries its port in the host as `host,1433` instead, and that convention is left alone |
| `database` | *(none)* | Azure SQL Database only: the one database this entry monitors (also part of the server's storage identity). PostgreSQL targets connect to the maintenance database and read cluster-wide catalogs |
| `auth` | `"integrated"` | `"integrated"`, `"sql"`, `"serviceprincipal"` or `"managedidentity"` |
| `username` | *(none)* | Required for `"sql"` (the login) and `"serviceprincipal"` (the application or client id). For `"managedidentity"`, only a user-assigned identity sets it (its client id) |
| `encryptedPassword` | *(none)* | DPAPI blob from `--encrypt-password` (preferred). For `"serviceprincipal"` it holds the client secret |
| `password` | *(none)* | A literal (dev only, warned on every use) or an `env:NAME` / `file:/path` reference (#1804) — references are the supported non-Windows shape and are not warned |
| `readOnlyIntent` | `false` | Route to a readable AG secondary (`ApplicationIntent=ReadOnly`) |
| `queryStoreReplica` | *(none)* | SQL Server only: a readable AG secondary (`host` or `host,port`) or a listener name that Darling reads Query Store from, instead of the server itself, so the primary carries none of that read. Empty (the default) reads the primary and changes nothing. Azure SQL Database never reads a replica. The replica host gets the same login and password as the server, so the replica must accept them. Changing it counts as a change of how the server is reached, so the web dashboard, the MCP `edit_server` tool and the desktop viewer ask for the password again. Collection health shows the newest Query Store run's `read_source` (for example `replica 12, primary 2`) beside the collector, only when a run has one |
| `trustServerCertificate` | `false` | |
| `encryptMode` | `"Mandatory"` | `Mandatory` / `Strict` / `Optional`; unknown values fail closed to `Mandatory` |
| `multiSubnetFailover` | `false` | |
| `excludedDatabases` | `[]` | Databases excluded from collection |
| `monthlyCostUsd` | `0` | The server's monthly cost in USD. Every cost figure on the FinOps tab is proportional math on this number. `0` hides the cost columns and cards |
| `alertDeliveryModeOverride` | *(none)* | `"Summary"` or `"PerEvent"` for this server's deadlock and blocking alerts. With none, the server follows `alerts.deliveryMode` |
| `awsRoleArn` | *(none)* | PostgreSQL targets on Amazon RDS or Aurora only: the IAM role ARN (such as `arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/darling-monitor`) Darling assumes for this server's AWS calls (the host check, CPU and host memory from Performance Insights, and the log reads behind plan capture, deadlocks and log events). With none, the process's own AWS credentials are used. The role must be allowed: a role named in `servers[]` is allowed by being here, and the web, MCP, desktop viewer and `--add-server` can set only roles listed in [`allowedAwsRoles`](#allowedawsroles-array-optional). See the [runbook](../docs/postgres-first-target-runbook.md) for the trust policy |
| `awsExternalId` | *(none)* | Optional, needs `awsRoleArn`: the external ID sent when the role is assumed, to match the trust policy's `sts:ExternalId` condition. 2 to 1224 characters. Never returned by the web or MCP and never written to a log |
| `remediationUsername` | *(none)* | **Optional second credential, for operator-initiated remediation (#2138) and the Level 3 server actions an agent can run (#5632).** The credential above stays read-only forever and is never used for a write. Omit both remediation keys — the default — and a server you add has no remediation login. For a server that is already registered, leaving the keys out changes nothing: the store keeps the login it has. Both halves are required; one alone counts as unarmed and the service says so at connect. Always SQL auth when set, so there is no `remediationAuth` key: an integrated remediation identity would be the service account, which is the monitoring identity |
| `remediationEncryptedPassword` | *(none)* | The remediation credential's secret: an `env:NAME` / `file:/path` reference, or a DPAPI blob from `--encrypt-password`. **Only a reference works everywhere.** A DPAPI blob still works for operator-initiated remediation (#2138), but the agent server actions (plan force, plan eviction and session kill) accept only an `env:` or `file:` reference and refuse a blob; `--validate-config` and the service log at start warn about a blob. There is deliberately **no plaintext variant** of this one — a wrong monitoring password fails a read, a wrong remediation password fails a write against a production server. Unlike the other per-server keys, **both remediation keys are re-read from darling.json on every start**: an entry with both keys sets or replaces that registered server's remediation login. Anything else leaves the login alone. An entry with neither key never clears a login that was set in the store, and **removing the keys does not disarm a server**. To stop agent actions on a server, turn off its action switches, or turn off agent changes service-wide; the switches cover agent actions only, not operator remediation from the Viewer. To remove a server's remediation login entirely, so neither agents nor operators can use it, first take both keys out of darling.json (an entry with both keys would set the login again on the next start), then run this as the store owner: `UPDATE config_monitored_servers SET remediation_username = NULL, remediation_encrypted_password = NULL, modified_at = now() AT TIME ZONE 'UTC' WHERE name = 'alpha-sql-01';`. An entry with only one key is logged and changes nothing. A server that is not in darling.json is never touched. The login is written only to the registered server that pairs with the entry by id (else by name, among rows no other entry claimed by id), and only when that server's host, port, engine and other connection settings are the entry's: otherwise it is skipped and the log names the server. A password that is none of an `env:`/`file:` reference, a sealed value or a DPAPI blob is not written (the log gives its shape, never the value). Services that share one store should declare the same logins, because each one copies its own darling.json when it starts. The log says when a login is set or changed |
| `hypotheticalIndexUsername` | *(none)* | **PostgreSQL targets only. The login that the "Test an index" check runs under (#6170).** The check never runs under the monitoring login. With no login set, the check is refused and the message says what to set up. See [Testing an index on PostgreSQL](#testing-an-index-on-postgresql) |
| `hypotheticalIndexEncryptedPassword` | *(none)* | The password of that login, as an `env:NAME` or `file:/path` reference. Darling reads it on its own host when the check runs. A stored password is refused, and so is a reference to one of Darling's own configuration files or secrets (unless this same reference is in darling.json for this server). The login needs a SCRAM-SHA-256 password: the check signs in with SCRAM-SHA-256 only, as the remediation login does |

**Least privilege for the remediation credential.** Grant `ALTER` on each database you intend to force a plan in — that is what `sp_query_store_force_plan` / `sp_query_store_unforce_plan` need. `ALTER SERVER STATE` is needed *only* to evict a plan (`evict_cached_plan`, #5632), because that is what a targeted `DBCC FREEPROCCACHE(plan_handle)` requires, and it is the expensive grant. It is genuinely optional: without it an eviction is refused as `evict_permission_denied`, a named reason and never silent, and nothing is run. The service does not guess per platform: before it evicts (and on a dry run) it asks the server with a read-only `HAS_PERMS_BY_NAME` check, which answers for the credential's effective permissions however the grant arrived. Azure SQL Database has no plan handle eviction at all, which no grant changes, and that is refused as its own reason, `evict_unsupported_on_platform`, rather than as a permission problem. An eviction has no undo, and it also needs that server's `evict_plan` switch; no server has it on after an upgrade.

**`ALTER ANY CONNECTION` is a strong grant, and it is needed only to kill (`kill_session`, #5632).** It lets the remediation login kill ANY session on that server, not only the one an agent names, so grant it only on a server where you want an agent to be able to kill. Darling checks that the session id and its sign-in time still match what the agent read from `get_blocking` before it runs `KILL`, and refuses with `kill_permission_denied` (a named reason, nothing run) when the login lacks the grant. A kill also needs that server's `kill_session` switch, which is off after an upgrade. A person turns it on the way they turn on `evict_plan`: the store owner sets `enabled` to true for that server and the action `kill_session` in `config.config_server_actions`. There is no switch screen for it today. [Agent dispatch](../docs/darling-agent-dispatch.md) gives the statement. **Azure SQL Database (General Purpose and Hyperscale).** A session there is reached only from inside its own database, so `kill_session` takes `database_name` (the `database_name` of the `get_blocking` row) and connects to that database, never to `master`, read-write. The database must be the server entry's database or one Darling collected a blocking row from in the last day, and not one the entry excludes; anything else is refused before a connection opens (`database_required`, `database_excluded`, `database_not_monitored`). The remediation login needs a user in each database it may kill in, either a contained user or a user for its login, with exactly these grants: `CREATE USER [remediation] WITH PASSWORD = '...'; GRANT VIEW DATABASE STATE TO [remediation]; GRANT KILL DATABASE CONNECTION TO [remediation];`. A missing grant answers `view_state_permission_denied` or `kill_permission_denied` and names the grant. One batch checks the engine, that the connection is in the named database (`wrong_database`), that the database is read-write there (`not_primary`), the session id and its `login_time` (`session_reused`), that the session's `database_id` is this database (`session_in_other_database`), and refuses system sessions and any session of the monitoring or remediation login (`tool_login_session`). On Hyperscale the kill reaches only the primary: a session on a named replica or a read-only secondary answers `session_gone` (not found on the primary) and nothing elsewhere is touched. A serverless database that is paused or resuming is retried on the open twice and then answers `database_unavailable` with nothing sent; an Azure transient error after the batch was sent answers `outcome_unknown`, and the batch is never sent again.

### Testing an index on PostgreSQL

The server tab can test an index on a PostgreSQL server. It plans one stored statement twice, with and without an index that does not exist. The index is a hypothetical one from the `hypopg` extension. Nothing is built.

Planning a statement can run a function. PostgreSQL runs a function while it plans when the function is marked `IMMUTABLE` and its arguments are constants, and also when it is marked `STABLE` and its arguments are constants, because the planner calls it to estimate how many rows a condition matches. The function runs under the login that plans. So the check does not use the monitoring login, which usually holds `pg_monitor` or `pg_read_all_stats`. It uses its own login.

Set up the login the way you set up the remediation login:

1. Create a login on the server. Give it no `pg_monitor`, `pg_read_all_stats`, `pg_read_all_data` or superuser role.
2. Give it `CONNECT` on the database.
3. Give it `USAGE` on each schema that holds a table you want to test, and on the schema `hypopg` is installed in.
4. Give it `SELECT` on each table that the statements read. PostgreSQL checks table privileges when it starts an `EXPLAIN`, so a table the login cannot read stops the check with `permission denied`.
5. Give it a SCRAM-SHA-256 password (`password_encryption = 'scram-sha-256'` when you set it), and a `scram-sha-256` line in `pg_hba.conf`. The check signs in with SCRAM-SHA-256 only. A server that asks for a password in clear text, MD5, or no password at all is refused before the password is sent. Unless the server entry verifies the server's certificate in full (encryption not `Optional` and `Trust server certificate` off), the check also needs SCRAM channel binding, so TLS must be on at the server (`ssl = on`).
6. Set `hypotheticalIndexUsername` to the login name and `hypotheticalIndexEncryptedPassword` to an `env:NAME` or `file:/path` reference to its password.

**For a server that is already in the store**, there are two ways. If you set BOTH keys in darling.json for that server, Darling applies them on every start (it sets the login, or replaces it when the file's differ), and the log says which server and the shape of the secret, never the login or the reference. Only an `env:` or `file:` reference is written, and only to the server whose connection settings match the entry. Leaving the keys out of darling.json never clears a login the server already has, and neither does a single key. Services that share one store should declare the same logins, because each one copies its own darling.json when it starts. The other way is to set them on the store row, as the store's owner role (the web dashboard, the MCP `edit_server` tool and the desktop viewer cannot set either pair, by design). For example, in `psql` as the owner:

```sql
UPDATE config.config_monitored_servers
SET hypothetical_index_username = 'pm_index_check',
    hypothetical_index_encrypted_password = 'env:PM_INDEX_CHECK_PASSWORD',
    modified_at = (now() AT TIME ZONE 'UTC')
WHERE name = 'my-pg-server';
```

The service picks the change up when it next reloads its server list. Once the password column is set, the web dashboard, the MCP `edit_server` tool and the desktop viewer refuse to change how that server is reached (host, port, database, authentication, username, encryption), as they do for a remediation login: change it on the service host, with the same `UPDATE` as the owner. Removing the two columns (`SET ... = NULL`) lifts that, unless darling.json still has both keys for the server: the next start puts them back.

A bring-your-own store needs one step after the service upgrades the store to V203. Run `tools/provision-roles.sql` again, so `config.edit_monitored_server` and the password-rules trigger know the new password column. The service replaces both on each start when its login owns them, and warns once, naming the script, when it does not. The compose store and managed provisioning do this on their own.

A bring-your-own store needs one step after the service upgrades the store to V213 (the Query Store replica setting). Run `tools/provision-roles.sql` again. The service replaces the three `config.edit_monitored_server` functions on each start when its login owns them, but the new 18-argument one is a new function that only the script grants to the `viewer` and `mcp` roles, and only the script grants them the new `query_store_replica` column; until then a web or MCP edit of a server fails with `permission denied`. The compose store and managed provisioning do this on their own.

A bring-your-own store needs one step after the service upgrades the store to V214 (known patterns). Run `tools/provision-roles.sql` again. It creates the three functions that add, change and delete a known pattern and grants them to the `admin` and `viewer` roles. Until you run it, adding, changing or deleting a known pattern fails. Reading them keeps working. The compose store and managed provisioning do this on their own.

**What a role that can create functions can read through this check.** The check runs `EXPLAIN` on a stored statement. If a role can create a function in that database, and a statement that calls it with constant arguments is among the statements the check plans, that function runs while the statement is planned, as this login. So a role that can create functions in that database can read, through the check, whatever this login can `SELECT`. Grant the login `SELECT` only on the tables you want to test, and treat the right to create functions in that database accordingly. The transaction is read-only, so the function cannot write.

The login needs no write grants. The check tests `SELECT` statements and `WITH` statements only. An `INSERT`, `UPDATE` or `DELETE` is refused with a plain message, because planning one needs a write grant on its table.

With no login set, the check is refused. The message names the login, the grants and the two keys. Darling never falls back to the monitoring login.

### Ending a PostgreSQL session

On a PostgreSQL server the remediation login ends one session with `pg_terminate_backend` (`kill_session`, #5632). The agent names the session by its `backend_id` and its pid. `get_pg_blocking` returns them as `root_backend_id` and `root_pid`, and `get_pg_session_states` as `backend_id` and `pid`. The `backend_id` holds the session's start time in whole seconds, then its pid in seven digits. The two must agree, or Darling refuses.

Darling checks the pid and the start second in the same statement that ends the session. A pid given to a session that started in another second is never ended. This is not a full guarantee. On Linux a pid comes back only after many others have been handed out. On Windows a pid can come back within about a second, so a new session there can have the same pid and the same start second.

If the monitoring login lacks `pg_read_all_stats`, the collector cannot read another role's start time, and it stores the server's start time in `backend_id` instead. Such an id names no session, so Darling refuses it as `backend_id_has_no_session_start`. Grant `pg_read_all_stats` (or `pg_monitor`) to the monitoring login; the ids collected after that work.

The login needs two grants: `pg_signal_backend`, and `pg_read_all_stats` (or `pg_monitor`, which includes it). Without `pg_read_all_stats` the server hides another role's start time. Darling then cannot check the session and refuses. Darling reads what the login can do before it tries. It refuses with `not_permitted` and a message that names the missing grant, and it does not try the kill anyway.

Darling also refuses a session that belongs to a superuser. PostgreSQL lets only a superuser end one, and `pg_signal_backend` does not change that.

Darling ends client sessions only. A background process or a replication sender is refused as `not_user_session`, and Darling's own connection is refused as `own_session`. Ending a session rolls back its open transaction. A session that is waiting for a synchronous standby has already committed on the server, so its commit stays. There is no undo.

`pg_terminate_backend` only sends the signal. After a kill Darling reads the session once more and says in `still_there` whether it was still there. When that read fails, or the caller cancels during it, `still_there` is null and the kill still counts as done. On SQL Server the same holds for `rollback`. If the reply to the kill is lost, for example because the connection drops or the server ends the kill's own connection, the answer is `outcome_unknown`: the session may have ended. The agent changes list then shows the kill as "result unknown", not failed. The SQL Server kill answers the same way. A kill also needs that server's `kill_session` switch, which is off after an upgrade.

#### How the remediation login signs in to PostgreSQL

The remediation connection always opens the `postgres` database. It does not use the database that the server entry names. `pg_stat_activity` and `pg_terminate_backend` cover the whole cluster, so that database is enough. If the login cannot connect to `postgres`, Darling refuses with `sign_in_failed` and names that database. A person can run `GRANT CONNECT ON DATABASE postgres TO <remediation login>`.

The connection sets `search_path` to `pg_catalog, pg_temp` when it signs in. A setting made with `ALTER DATABASE` or `ALTER ROLE` does not change it. If a connection pooler drops that setting, Darling reads `search_path` before it runs anything, refuses with `search_path_not_pinned`, and names the value it found. The server entry then needs to point at the PostgreSQL server directly. Each statement that Darling runs also names its views, functions and operators with the `pg_catalog` schema. Darling never uses a function or an operator of the same name in another schema.

The remediation login signs in with SCRAM-SHA-256 only. If the server asks for a password in clear text or as MD5, or asks for no password, Darling refuses with `sign_in_not_strong_enough`. It does not send the password. If the server entry does not verify the server's certificate in full, the sign-in also needs SCRAM channel binding. Channel binding ties the sign-in to the TLS session. The sign-in then fails when that TLS session does not end at the server that holds the login. The monitoring connection does not change.

Before a kill or a dry run can sign in, do these steps:

1. Set `password_encryption = 'scram-sha-256'` on the server. It is the default from PostgreSQL 14.
2. Set the password of the remediation login again, so that the server stores it as SCRAM.
3. Give the remediation login a `scram-sha-256` line in `pg_hba.conf`.
4. Turn TLS on at the server (`ssl = on`). Channel binding needs TLS, and a verified certificate needs TLS too.
5. If you do not want channel binding, verify the certificate. In the server entry, set an encryption mode other than Optional and turn off Trust server certificate.

### Managed PostgreSQL

None of the three providers' pages says whether its admin role holds `pg_signal_backend`. Check your own login: a dry run asks the server and ends nothing. These are the facts each page states.

- Amazon RDS and Aurora: `rds_superuser` can stop connections with `pg_terminate_backend`. The internal `rdsadmin` role does the work of a superuser ([AWS](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/Appendix.PostgreSQL.CommonDBATasks.Roles.rds_superuser.html)).
- Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server: the server admin belongs to `azure_pg_admin`, a pseudo-superuser role. It does not belong to `azuresu`, the role with superuser privileges ([Microsoft](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/postgresql/security/security-access-control)).
- Google Cloud SQL: `cloudsqlsuperuser` holds limited superuser privileges. `cloudsqladmin` is a system user with superuser privileges ([Google](https://docs.cloud.google.com/sql/docs/postgres/users)).

On any of them, a login that is not a superuser cannot end a superuser's session. Darling refuses it as `not_permitted`.

> Phase 1 ships **no consumer of this credential**. It is accepted, stored and resolvable so the arming model can be reviewed and provisioned ahead of the write path; the service logs at connect that an armed server is inert in this build. Nothing in this release can write to a monitored server.

### allowedAwsRoles (array, optional)

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `allowedAwsRoles` | `[]` | The AWS roles the web, MCP, desktop viewer and `--add-server` may set on a server. Each entry is an IAM role ARN (matched exactly) or a 12-digit AWS account id (every role in that account in the `aws` partition; write `aws-cn:123456789012` or `aws-us-gov:123456789012` for an account in the China or GovCloud partition). The service runs a role only if it is listed here or a `servers[]` entry in this file names it in `awsRoleArn`. With no list, the web and MCP cannot set a role. Read at start: restart the service after you change it. Example: `[ "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/darling-monitor", "123456789012" ]` |

### capturePlans (boolean, optional)

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `capturePlans` | `true` | Capture execution plans into `query_stats.query_plan_xml` and `query_store_stats.query_plan_text`. PostgreSQL TOAST compresses the plan text transparently (LZ4 on the managed store) and TimescaleDB chunk compression squeezes it further, so plans are cheap to keep — unlike Lite, which stores to DuckDB/Parquet and deliberately never captures them. Set `false` to skip plan capture (e.g. to shave storage across a very large fleet). |

### collectSchemaChangeEvents (boolean, optional)

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `collectSchemaChangeEvents` | `true` | Record `Object:Created` / `Object:Altered` / `Object:Deleted` schema-change (DDL) events in the built-in default-trace collector. Set `false` on a noisy or benchmark box where a create/drop-happy workload floods the viewer's **System Events > Default Trace** tab — e.g. HammerDB's TPC-H Query 15 creates and drops a `revenue` view thousands of times, and the collector faithfully records every create/delete. Only the Object DDL slice is suppressed; file auto-grow/shrink, ErrorLog, and security-audit events are still collected. The shared collector's equivalent of the full Dashboard's `@include_object_events`. A file-only knob (not stored in the control plane): edit and restart. |

### procedureStatsPlanCycleInterval (integer, optional)

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `procedureStatsPlanCycleInterval` | `4` | How many collection cycles pass between execution-plan captures for `procedure_stats`. `1` captures on every cycle and is byte-identical to releases before this knob existed; `4` captures on one cycle in four. Clamped to `[1,60]`, and a nonsense value (`0` or negative) degrades to `1` rather than to the default, so a bad setting never silently starts skipping plans. **Why it exists:** `procedure_stats` is normally the most expensive collector against a monitored server, and nearly all of that cost is plan XML — a controlled decomposition put **73.8%** of its read loop in RENDERING the plans and **26.0%** in shipping them, against **0.2%** for the same query with no plan apply at all. The render happens inside `sys.dm_exec_text_query_plan`, a server-side TVF, so it is CPU on the **monitored** server rather than on the collector host; skipping a cycle omits the apply entirely rather than rendering and discarding. **What you trade:** plan freshness — a stored plan can be up to this many cycles old, and `4` keeps the worst case inside the collector's own ten-minute candidate window. Runtime statistics (execution counts, CPU, duration, reads) are collected every cycle regardless, and the collector stays on its 1-minute schedule. The capture cycle is offset per server, so a fleet spreads across the interval instead of every server rendering together. Only `procedure_stats` is gated; `query_stats`, `query_store`, `deadlocks` and `blocked_process_report` still capture plans every cycle. A file-only knob (not stored in the control plane): edit and restart. |

### queryStatsDeferredPlanFetch (boolean, optional)

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `queryStatsDeferredPlanFetch` | `true` | Whether `query_stats` fetches execution-plan XML only for the plans this collector host has not already stored. On (the default), the main query reads the statistics with no plan, and a second query on the same connection renders just the plans whose identity (plan handle, `creation_time`, `plan_generation_num`) the host has not committed; a row for a plan it has stored reuses the stored digest, so every reader sees the same rows and plans as before. Off restores the inline capture, which renders and ships every row's plan on every run. **Why it exists:** on one large store the inline capture rendered 54 GB of plan XML an hour on the monitored servers and shipped 29 GB of it, against 1.1 GB an hour of distinct plans, about 47 times the plans there are. The render is CPU on the **monitored** server (`sys.dm_exec_text_query_plan`), so this is load taken off the servers you watch. **Why you would turn it off:** to get the plan re-rendered on every run (for example to compare against the old behavior while testing), or if a server is so constrained that the second round trip is unwelcome. **What you trade by leaving it on:** a plan's text is fetched once per identity, so a plan the engine changes in place without a recompile is not re-read until it leaves the cache (entries unused for an hour are dropped; a restart empties it and each server renders its plans once more). A statement recompile between the two queries can pair the new plan with the old identity for that one run's row; the old identity leaves `sys.dm_exec_query_stats` at once, so it is not read again. **Azure SQL Database keeps the inline capture** regardless of this setting, because its per-database collection path has no second query to defer to. Only meaningful while `capturePlans` is on. The run records `plans_rendered`, `plans_rendered_bytes` and `plan_fetch_ms` in its `collection_log` note. A file-only knob (not stored in the control plane): edit and restart. |

### procedureStatsDeferredPlanFetch (string, optional)

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `procedureStatsDeferredPlanFetch` | `"on"` | Whether `procedure_stats` fetches execution-plan XML only for the module plans this collector host has not already stored. Three values; anything else is read as `off` and logs one warning. **`off`** keeps the inline capture and is the way to turn the deferred fetch off: every captured module plan is rendered and shipped, as before. **`shadow`** does exactly what `off` does, and every stored row is the same as `off` would store; in addition it recognizes each module plan by its identity (plan handle, `cached_time`, and a fingerprint of the module's statements read from `sys.dm_exec_query_stats`: how many, the latest `creation_time`, the sum of `plan_generation_num`) and checks that identity against the plans this host has already stored. It writes nothing different. What it measures lands in the run's `collection_log` note, beside `plans_rendered`: `deferred_would_hit` (identities it would have skipped rendering), `deferred_false_hit`, `deferred_same_shape` and `deferred_miss`. Of the would-hits that rendered different bytes, `deferred_false_hit` counts the plans whose shape changed, or whose shape could not be compared. This is the number that must stay 0. `deferred_same_shape` counts the plans whose bytes changed but whose shape held, for example a memory grant that SQL Server adjusted in place. `on` treats that as a correct hit. Each false hit logs one warning. It names the identity and says why. One reason is that the plan's shape changed. The other reasons mean the shapes were not compared: the cached entry has no shape yet, the rendered plan is over the size cap, or its XML did not parse. The note also splits `deferred_false_hit` by reason into `deferred_false_hit_shape`, `deferred_false_hit_no_shape`, `deferred_false_hit_over_cap` and `deferred_false_hit_unparsed`, which add up to it, so a real shape change reads apart from a plan that was not compared (an over-cap plan is still compared by its byte size, so a memory grant change can count there). **`on`** (the default since 3.10) skips the render for a recognized plan and sends its stored digest instead; the others are rendered in one second query on the same connection, at most 150 per run (the main query is already `TOP (150)`, so the limit is a guard, and `deferred_over_cap = 0` is expected). The run records `plans_rendered`, `plans_rendered_bytes`, `plan_fetch_ms`, `deferred_hit` and `deferred_miss`. `procedureStatsPlanCycleInterval` still decides which runs may render plans: a run between captures sends the digest for a recognized plan and renders nothing. An identity is trusted for four capture cycles, then rendered again. **Why a shadow mode:** a module plan changes in place when one statement recompiles, with the same plan handle and `cached_time`; shadow measures the identity against the plans it just rendered before `on` is allowed to trust it. Move from `shadow` to `on` only after `deferred_false_hit` has stayed 0 over a representative period. A rising `deferred_same_shape` is not a reason to wait. **Azure SQL Database stays `off`** regardless of this setting. Only meaningful while `capturePlans` is on. A file-only knob (not stored in the control plane): edit and restart. The plan cache starts empty under whichever mode the service starts in. |

### alerts

The shared alert engine's switches and thresholds. Every default mirrors Lite's alert defaults exactly, so an empty section alerts like a fresh Lite install. `enabled: false` turns off all alert evaluation **and** analysis finding notifications (the analysis itself still runs and persists findings).

| Key | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `enabled` | `true` | Master switch for alert evaluation + finding notifications |
| `cpuEnabled` | `true` | |
| `cpuThresholdPercent` | `80` | |
| `cpuMode` | `"total"` | `"total"` = SQL + other processes; `"sql"` = SQL process only |
| `blockingEnabled` | `true` | |
| `blockingCountThreshold` | `1` | Blocking waits (rolling window) that trip the alert on a SQL Server target. One wait is one blocked session, blocker and wait start (#5644): the several blocked-process reports SQL Server writes for one long wait count once |
| `pgBlockingCountThreshold` | `1` | The PostgreSQL twin (#3444, V122): distinct ROOT blockers in the rolling window that trip `Blocking Detected` on a PostgreSQL target, floor 1. Its own key because the two count different things — engine-recorded blocked-process reports there, roots found in a periodic SAMPLE of `pg_stat_activity` here — so moving one deliberately does not move the other. `blockingEnabled` governs both engines. Inert on a store with no PostgreSQL targets |
| `blockingWaitSecondsThreshold` | `0` | Total blocked wait, in seconds, summed across the latest blocking snapshot; `0` = off. A second gate beside the count one, because a count cannot tell one session blocked for an hour from one blocked for a second. Reports as its own "Blocking Wait Time" alert, and unlike the count gate it is level-triggered: it re-fires every cooldown while the wait stays above the threshold and clears when it drops below. Since #3653 the fire sits behind the shared persistence gate as a disjunction: one snapshot at or above **3× the threshold** fires at once, anything between the threshold and that multiple must hold for **3 consecutive collected snapshots** (counted per collection, not per 30 s sweep; a quiet collection between two breaching ones starts a new episode, because the collector writes rows only while something is blocked). The card's `Fired By` field says which arm admitted it (`single_snapshot_3x` / `consecutive_k3`). Measured on one production store class: 97 of 102 episodes were a single snapshot, so a plain consecutive gate would have dropped exactly the severe pile-ups |
| `deadlockEnabled` | `true` | |
| `deadlockCountThreshold` | `1` | Deadlock count (rolling window) that trips the alert on a SQL Server target |
| `pgDeadlockCountThreshold` | `1` | The PostgreSQL twin (#3444, V122): distinct deadlocks in the rolling window that trip `Deadlocks Detected` on a PostgreSQL target, floor 1. Its own key because the SQL Server figure is calibrated against the fleet's deadlock health band (`health_bands.deadlock_warn_per_hour` on `get_alert_settings`), a band a PostgreSQL server does not have. `deadlockEnabled` governs both engines. Inert on a store with no PostgreSQL targets |
| `poisonWaitEnabled` | `true` | THREADPOOL / RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE / RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE_QUERY_COMPILE on SQL Server; IPC:BtreePage / IPC:BufferIo on Aurora PostgreSQL. **One switch, one alert name, one shape on both engines**: the alert fires when a poison wait type accumulates 600 s of wait inside a rolling ten-minute window — an average of one task (SQL Server) or backend (PostgreSQL) continuously stuck — as WARNING, and 6,000 s (ten stuck) as CRITICAL, judged per wait type. The bars are constants shared by both evaluators (`PoisonWaitEvaluator`), fleet-calibrated ~100× (SQL Server) and ~160× (PostgreSQL) above the worst healthy ten-minute bucket measured, and a mute rule on `Poison Wait` means the same thing whichever engine the server runs |
| `poisonWaitThresholdMs` | `500` | **No longer consulted.** Formerly the SQL Server per-wait average bar; retired by the shared accumulation shape above. Still read, written and reported (`get_alert_settings`) so existing configuration files and control-plane rows round-trip |
| `runnableQueueEnabled` | `true` | The Runnable Queue alert (#5641, V177): SQL Server only. Fires when the average of runnable tasks per scheduler (`cpu_scheduler_stats.avg_runnable_tasks_count`: the collections in the last 5 minutes when there are at least 3, otherwise the newest 3 if all fall in the last 10 minutes (or in 4 times the gap between cpu_scheduler_stats collections when that is longer, so the Low-Impact preset's 5-minute collection still evaluates)) reaches the Warning or Critical bar below. Stored as `config_alert_settings.runnable_queue_enabled`; `get_alert_settings` reports it as `runnable_queue.enabled` and `update_alert_settings` writes it |
| `runnableQueueWarningPerScheduler` | `8` | Warning bar, runnable tasks per scheduler (the window average above). At least 1. Settings window, `runnable_queue.warning_per_scheduler` |
| `runnableQueueCriticalPerScheduler` | `16` | Critical bar, same unit. Never below the Warning bar: the engine raises a lower value to the Warning bar, and the Settings window and `update_alert_settings` refuse a pair in the wrong order rather than rewrite it. Settings window, `runnable_queue.critical_per_scheduler` |
| `clientTimeoutsEnabled` | `true` | The Client Timeouts alert (#5640, V179): SQL Server only. Fires when client attentions per minute reach the Warning or Critical bar below. Each bar is relative to the server's own normal rate (#5736). An attention is the event that SQL Server records when a client cancels a query or its timeout expires. The rate is the attentions that the attention collector counted, divided by the minutes that its last runs cover (about 3 minutes). The alert is on by default since V216 (#5736): the upgrade turns it on for a store that had it off, because nothing offered off as a choice before then. Switch it off again in the Settings window if you do not want it. Stored as `config_alert_settings.client_timeouts_enabled`. `get_alert_settings` reports it as `client_timeouts.enabled`, and `update_alert_settings` writes it |
| `clientTimeoutsWarningPerMinute` | `10` | Warning FLOOR, attentions per minute (the rate above). At least 1. Since #5736 the Warning bar is the higher of this floor and `clientTimeoutsWarningMultiple` times the server's own baseline. Settings window, `client_timeouts.warning_per_minute` |
| `clientTimeoutsCriticalPerMinute` | `60` | Critical FLOOR, same unit. Never below the Warning bar. The engine raises a lower value to the Warning bar. The Settings window and `update_alert_settings` refuse a pair in the wrong order rather than rewrite it. Settings window, `client_timeouts.critical_per_minute` |
| `clientTimeoutsWarningMultiple` | `3` | The Warning bar is at least this many times the server's own baseline, the p99.9 of its 3-minute average attention rate over the last 7 days (#5736, V211). At least 1. Settings window, `client_timeouts.warning_multiple` |
| `clientTimeoutsCriticalMultiple` | `10` | The Critical bar is at least this many times the baseline. Never below the Warning multiple. Settings window, `client_timeouts.critical_multiple` |
| `longRunningQueryEnabled` | `true` | |
| `longRunningQueryThresholdMinutes` | `30` | |
| `tempDbSpaceEnabled` | `true` | |
| `tempDbSpaceThresholdPercent` | `80` | |
| `lowDiskEnabled` | `true` | Volume free space; graded CRITICAL when critically low |
| `lowDiskThresholdPercent` | `10` | Fire below X% free; `0` disables this dimension (clamped 0–100) |
| `lowDiskThresholdGb` | `5` | Fire below X GB free; `0` disables this dimension |
| `longRunningJobEnabled` | `true` | SQL Agent job running long vs. its history |
| `longRunningJobMultiplier` | `3` | Fires at 3x the job's historical average |
| `failedJobEnabled` | `true` | Live msdb check for recently failed jobs |
| `failedJobLookbackMinutes` | `60` | Clamped 1–1440 |
| `cooldownMinutes` | `5` | Minimum minutes between repeats of the same alert condition (clamped 1–120) |
| `excludedDatabases` | `[]` | Excluded from blocking/deadlock/long-running-query **alert evaluation** (collection unaffected) |
| `excludedVolumes` | `[]` | Mount points the Volume Free Space alert ignores on every server (#6098): the whole mount point, case-insensitive, a trailing `\` or `/` on either side ignored (`S:`, `S:\` and `s:\` are one volume; `/var/opt/mssql` equals `/var/opt/mssql/`); never a prefix match. Dropped before the breach test, so the worst volume, the severity and the card come from the volumes that remain, and a server whose only low volumes are excluded resolves. Seeded into the store on first run (migration V200); the store row wins afterwards, edited in the Viewer's Settings window, the web admin page or `update_alert_settings`. Collection is unaffected |
| `longRunningQueryExcludedProgramNamePrefixes` | `["SQLAgent - TSQL JobStep"]` | Long-Running Query opt-out knob, program arm (#3653): sessions whose `program_name` *starts with* an entry are not evaluated by that alert (case-insensitive, no wildcards; applied ahead of the row cap). Seeded from a 7-day read of one large production store — SQL Agent job steps, ~460 sessions a week. A default, not a constant: `[]` excludes nothing by program |
| `longRunningQueryExcludedLogins` | `["NT AUTHORITY\\SYSTEM", "NT AUTHORITY\\NETWORK SERVICE"]` | Long-Running Query opt-out knob, login arm (#3653): sessions whose `login_name` *equals* an entry are not evaluated (case-insensitive, whole name). Seeded with the two service principals that carried the read's permanent multi-day background; the application's admin login is deliberately not a default (it also runs real ad-hoc long-runners). `[]` excludes nothing by login |

The long-running-query read shape is configurable too, on the same defaults Lite ships: `longRunningQueryMaxResults` (`5`, clamped 1–1000) and the five noise opt-outs `longRunningQueryExcludeSpServerDiagnostics`, `longRunningQueryExcludeWaitFor`, `longRunningQueryExcludeBackups`, `longRunningQueryExcludeMiscWaits`, `longRunningQueryExcludeCdc` (all `true`). Since #3653 the SQL Server read also carries the Long-Running Query **opt-out knob**: sessions whose `program_name` starts with a configured prefix or whose `login_name` equals a configured login are *not evaluated* by the alert at all — not read into the decision, not counted, not fingerprinted, the opposite of a mute rule — and the exclusion is applied inside the read ahead of `longRunningQueryMaxResults`, because the sessions it names are the longest-running on the server by construction and would otherwise fill the cap on every sweep. Its defaults come from a 7-day read of one large production store, whose long-running population was four classes: (1) SQL Agent job steps (`SQLAgent - TSQL JobStep…`, ~460 sessions a week across 11 jobs, medians 35–62 min) — the seeded **program-name prefix**; (2) the `NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM` / `NT AUTHORITY\NETWORK SERVICE` logins (~70 sessions across 42 servers, medians 4.8–8.6 *days*, CDC-capture shaped) — the seeded **exact logins**; (3) the application's admin login — deliberately *not* a default, because it carries the job wave but also real ad-hoc long-runners, and the job-step prefix already removes its share; (4) named humans — 3 a week, never excluded. Matching is case-insensitive with no wildcard grammar; the fired card's `Excluded Count` item splits the sessions removed by arm (a session matching both arms counts once, under the prefix). **On Darling the knob lives on the settings row since V135**: two `text[]` columns on `config_alert_settings` (`longRunningQueryExcludedProgramNamePrefixes` / `longRunningQueryExcludedLogins` in `darling.json`, seeded on first run; the store row wins afterwards like every alert knob), edited in the Viewer's Settings window or through `update_alert_settings`, reported by `get_alert_settings`; a pre-rung row reads as the seeds, and a list an operator clears stays cleared. Lite ships the same defaults in `settings.json`. The PostgreSQL twin consults the same knob through the same shared predicate builder (#3743): `application_name` is the `program_name` twin and `usename` (stored as `username`) the `login_name` twin, so an entry means the same thing on both engines — prefix for programs, whole name for logins, case-insensitive — applied ahead of the row cap, with the fired card's `Excluded Count` split rendered the same way. The seeds name SQL Server internals and match nothing on PostgreSQL by construction; whether that engine wants seeds of its own is a production read, not a guess. On a PostgreSQL target the read also honours `longRunningQueryExcludeBackups` (it drops `pg_dump` / `pg_dumpall` / `pg_restore` / `pg_basebackup` sessions by `application_name`) and `excludedDatabases` through the same builder's database arm, ahead of the same row cap (#3742) — the PostgreSQL twin applies all three exclusions in the read, so an excluded database's sessions can no longer consume the page, and the fired card carries an `Excluded By Database` count beside the knob's when the list is set — and unconditionally skips non-client backends (autovacuum workers, walsenders) and the `VACUUM` / `ANALYZE` / `REINDEX` / `CLUSTER` statements; the other three switches name SQL Server internals with no PostgreSQL reading and are ignored there — see the engine-coverage table below. Not configurable: the analysis-finding notification policy (notify at severity >= 1.5, 6-hour per-finding cooldown).

#### More `alerts` keys

These keys are in the same `alerts` section and are first-run seeds like the table above.

| Key | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `notifyConnectionChanges` | `true` | Send the Server Unreachable and Server Restored alerts |
| `notifyConnectionDownAtStartup` | `false` | Also announce a server that is already down on its first connect attempt after the service starts |
| `connectionRefireMinutes` | `0` | Re-announce a server that stays down every N minutes. `0` is off |
| `notifyAgHealth` | `true` | Master switch for the Availability Group alerts (failover, replica disconnect and reconnect, sync fell behind, database suspended) |
| `agLagAlertSeconds` | `300` | AG Sync Fell Behind fires when a secondary's lag reaches this many seconds. `0` is off |
| `agRedoQueueAlertKb` | `0` | The second AG Sync Fell Behind trigger: a secondary's redo queue in KB. `0` is off |
| `agDisconnectRefireMinutes` | `0` | Re-announce a replica that stays disconnected every N minutes. `0` is off |
| `pvsEnabled` | `true` | The persistent version store alert (SQL Server databases with accelerated database recovery) |
| `pvsThresholdPercent` | `40` | Fire when the version store reaches this percent of the database's data files. `0` turns the check off |
| `pvsFloorGb` | `1` | A breach also needs at least this many GB of version store. `0` removes the floor |
| `fileGrowthEnabled` | `false` | The database file-growth alert. Off by default |
| `fileGrowthRiseMb` | `10240` | Fire when a database file grows faster than this many MB per hour, averaged over `fileGrowthLookbackMinutes`. `0` turns this gate off |
| `fileGrowthVolumePercent` | `60` | Also fire when a growing database file is over this percent of its volume. `0` turns this gate off |
| `fileGrowthLookbackMinutes` | `60` | The window the growth rate is averaged over. It does not rescale `fileGrowthRiseMb` |
| `selfDiskFreeWarnPercent` | `10` | Store Disk Pressure warns below this percent free on the store's volume. `0` turns the check off |
| `selfDiskFreeWarnGb` | `50` | The percent also needs free space below this many GB, so a large volume at a low percent does not page. `0` removes the floor |
| `collectionStaleMinutes` | `30` | How long collection can go quiet before Collection Stopped fires |
| `collectionFailureThreshold` | `10` | Consecutive failures with no success that fire Collection Stopped without waiting out the window above |
| `diskCriticalFreePercent` | `3` | The Low Disk alert is graded Critical below this percent free |
| `diskCriticalFreeGb` | `2` | The Low Disk alert is graded Critical below this many GB free |
| `analysisNotifyCooldownMinutes` | `360` | Cooldown between notifications for the same analysis finding (clamped 30 to 10080) |
| `storeJobCadenceWarnPercent` | `25` | Store Job Over Cadence warns when a store job's last run reaches this percent of its schedule interval. Critical is fixed at 100 |
| `retentionHoldWarnRatio` | `2.0` | Retention Held warns when a policy held by rollup coverage is holding this many times its own horizon |
| `retentionHoldCriticalRatio` | `4.0` | The same for the Critical tier |
| `fleetSweepEnabled` | `true` | Run the scheduled fleet sweep. It has its own switch and keeps running when `enabled` is `false` |
| `fleetSweepIntervalMinutes` | `60` | How often the fleet sweep runs, 15 to 1440 |
| `deadlockWarnPerHour` | `5` | The deadlock health band's Warning tier, in deadlocks per hour |
| `deadlockCriticalPerHour` | `20` | The deadlock health band's Critical tier |
| `databaseStateEnabled` | `true` | The database-state alert (a database in a state other than its expected one) |
| `deliveryMode` | `"Summary"` | How deadlock and blocking alerts are delivered: `"Summary"` is one batched card per cycle, `"PerEvent"` is one notification per incident. A server's `alertDeliveryModeOverride` wins over it |
| `perEventMax` | `5` | In `"PerEvent"` mode, the most incidents sent one by one per cycle before a trailing "+N more" (clamped 1 to 100) |

#### Which engine each built-in alert and health band covers

One alert name means one condition on both engines wherever both engines can honestly measure it; where only one engine can, the row says why rather than leaving the gap to be discovered (#3539). The health bands are the fleet card's dots (`get_fleet_overview`, `/api/fleet`, the viewer's Overview).

| Condition | SQL Server | PostgreSQL | Why one-sided, where it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Deadlocks** (alert) | Extended-event graphs, `deadlockCountThreshold` per rolling window | Server-log deadlocks parsed into `pg_deadlocks`, `deadlocks.pg_count_threshold` (V122) | Both. Separate count knobs because the instruments differ (a captured graph vs a parsed log entry) and an operator tuning one should not silently move the other. The SQL Server fire is **graded** since #3653: WARNING by default, CRITICAL when the hour's rate reaches the health band's `deadlock_critical_per_hour` tier below — the count knob decides whether it fires, the band's pair decides the colour, so the alert row and the fleet card agree about the same hour. The PostgreSQL host's fire is graded the same way since #3653 — the same grader, the same one-hour count, the same store tiers — on its own deduped report count (the number the row quotes), not on the counter difference the band row below uses; the read caps at 50 distinct reports, so a Critical tier raised past 50/hr is unreachable on that host. |
| **Deadlocks** (health band) | `v_deadlocks` graphs per hour | `pg_stat_database.deadlocks` — the server's own lifetime counter, differenced between consecutive samples per database, clamped at zero across a statistics reset, summed across the cluster's databases | Both, through the **same** `health_bands.deadlock_warn_per_hour` / `deadlock_critical_per_hour` tiers: a deadlock per hour is the same quantity whichever engine recorded it. The PostgreSQL arm bands only when at least one difference was taken in the window (a difference of fewer than two samples is not a zero), so a target nothing has collected from reads Unknown rather than Healthy. The fleet total's `deadlock_coverage.postgres_servers` names how many servers were counted the counter way; `get_pg_deadlocks` has the graphs. Never `SUM(deadlocks)` on the store — the column is a lifetime counter repeated in every one-minute sample. |
| **Blocking** (alert) | Blocking waits counted from blocked-process reports + DMV snapshots (one per blocked session, blocker and wait start), `blockingCountThreshold` | Distinct root blockers in a periodic sample of `pg_stat_activity`, `blocking.pg_count_threshold` (V122) | Both; separate knobs because the denominators differ (engine-recorded reports vs sampled sightings). The PostgreSQL fire carries an explicit WARNING tier and no CRITICAL tier (#3653): the band row below says why no measured bar exists for sampled sightings, and a fire bar is not a tier. |
| **Blocking** (health band) | Reports per hour + longest block, the #3596 tiers | **Unknown, by design** | The band's count tiers were measured in *reports per server-hour* on engine-recorded evidence. PostgreSQL blocking is SAMPLED once a minute: a waiter seen in three consecutive captures is one wait observed three times, not three reports, so a sighting count through those tiers would band on a denominator they were never measured against. An honest PostgreSQL band needs its own sampled-shape tiers (share of captures holding a waiter; longest observed wait) measured on that fleet's distribution, which has not been taken. Unknown-with-reason beats a fake Healthy; the alert speaks for PostgreSQL blocking meanwhile. Tiering pending: #3601's `lock_wait` log events (`log_lock_waits` "still waiting" lines, one per wait past `deadlock_timeout`) are the event-grain evidence a report-rate band would read, and the join point for a future PostgreSQL arm. |
| **Blocking Wait Time** | Sum of blocked wait across the latest live DMV snapshot, `blockingWaitSecondsThreshold`; since #3653 gated: 3× the bar on one snapshot, or 3 consecutive collections | — | SQL-only. The condition is a *live total* of how long sessions are currently blocked, which the DMV snapshot gives exactly. `pg_blocking` is a per-minute sample of `pg_stat_activity`: summing observed waits across one capture is a lower bound on the same quantity taken at an arbitrary instant, and a threshold in seconds against a once-a-minute sample would fire on the sampling grain as much as on the workload. A sampled twin is possible but needs its own stated denominator; not faked under the SQL Server name. |
| **Long-Running Query** | `sys.dm_exec_requests`, five noise opt-outs (sp_server_diagnostics, WAITFOR, backups, misc XE waits, CDC) + `session_id > 50` + `excludedDatabases` + the #3653 opt-out knob (`program_name` prefixes / exact logins, seeded with the SQL Agent job-step prefix and the two `NT AUTHORITY` service logins, applied ahead of the row cap) | Latest `pg_session_states` capture, same threshold and switch | Both. PostgreSQL opt-outs: non-client backends (unconditional — mirrors `session_id > 50`), `VACUUM`/`ANALYZE`/`REINDEX`/`CLUSTER` by command tag (unconditional — SQL Server needs no statement-shape switch because its row carries the text and a mute rule can match it; `pg_session_states` stores no text, so the tag is the only handle), dump/restore utilities by `application_name` on the shared `longRunningQueryExcludeBackups`, the #3653 opt-out knob over `application_name` / `usename` through the same shared predicate builder and ahead of the same row cap (#3743; the SQL Server seeds match nothing here), `excludedDatabases` as the same CTE's third flag ahead of the same cap (#3742). `CREATE` is reported (with its tag) because the tag cannot tell an index build from `CREATE TABLE AS`. The remaining three SQL Server switches name SQL Server internals; `pg_sleep` (WAITFOR's cousin) is invisible without text. The PostgreSQL fire carries an explicit WARNING tier and no CRITICAL tier (#3653): the only bar the alert has is the operator's threshold, and no measured duration distribution exists on either engine to cut a second tier from. |
| **Poison Wait** | THREADPOOL / RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE / RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE_QUERY_COMPILE | IPC:BtreePage / IPC:BufferIo (Aurora) | Both, **one shape** since #3593: accumulated wait over a rolling ten-minute window, graded WARNING/CRITICAL from the shared `PoisonWaitEvaluator` bars on both engines. The earlier divergence (always-CRITICAL on SQL Server, graded on PostgreSQL, under one mute key) is closed. Both hosts clear a standing alert only on an **observed** window (#3593 SQL Server, #3653 PostgreSQL): a collector that stops delivering holds the alert open rather than announcing a recovery nobody measured. On PostgreSQL the witness is the `pg_wait_stats` collector's own `collection_log` runs inside the window, because since #2694 a quiet poison event writes no row and the rows alone cannot tell a finished storm from a dead collector. |
| **Runnable Queue** | `cpu_scheduler_stats.avg_runnable_tasks_count`, the average over the collections in the last 5 minutes when there are at least 3, otherwise over the newest 3 if all fall in the last 10 minutes (or in 4 times the gap between cpu_scheduler_stats collections when that is longer, so the Low-Impact preset's 5-minute collection still evaluates), WARNING at `runnableQueueWarningPerScheduler` (8), CRITICAL at `runnableQueueCriticalPerScheduler` (16) | **None** | SQL Server only: the signal is runnable tasks per scheduler, which the SQL Server scheduler collector stores and PostgreSQL has no counterpart for. A window with neither 3 collections in 5 minutes nor 3 inside the fallback window neither fires nor clears a standing alert, and a standing alert clears only on an observed quiet window or, after 30 minutes with no evaluated reading, resolves with that reason stated. The 30 minutes count from the last evaluated reading, and a window that is not an observation never restarts the count. A Warning that worsens to Critical is delivered at once, past the cooldown (the email and webhook cooldowns and the viewer's tray cooldown let it through). Mute it with `MetricName = "Runnable Queue"` (no wait type) |
| **Client Timeouts** | `attention_events` rows from the attention collector. The bars are relative to each server's own normal rate (#5736): WARNING at the higher of `clientTimeoutsWarningPerMinute` (the floor, 10) and `clientTimeoutsWarningMultiple` (3) times the server's baseline, CRITICAL at the higher of `clientTimeoutsCriticalPerMinute` (the floor, 60) and `clientTimeoutsCriticalMultiple` (10) times the baseline. The baseline is the p99.9 of the server's 3-minute average rate over the last 7 days, read about once a day, and a server with under a day of capture, or under 720 minutes (half a day) in which the collector ran, is graded on the floors alone. On by default | **None** | SQL Server only: the signal is an Extended Events session that PostgreSQL has no counterpart for. The alert is on by default. A Warning that worsens to Critical is delivered at once, past the cooldown. A mute rule with a client application pattern lowers the count in proportion to the share of the sampled events that the rule covers. Mute it with `MetricName = "Client Timeouts"` |
| **High CPU** | Ring buffer, 3-sample persistence gate | Performance Insights, banded on ACU headroom, same gate | Both. Both fires are graded since #3653: WARNING at the configured threshold, CRITICAL at the CPU health band's 95% bar — the card's ladder, not a new number. On PostgreSQL the grade is the band's own classifier on the same two figures the card is handed, so the 95% is of the configured ACU ceiling (the figure the gate thresholded), never of the currently allocated capacity. |
| **Database File Growth** | Per-file growth over the lookback window vs `FileGrowthRiseMb` | — | SQL-only. It is a SQL Server *file-model* condition: data/log files with explicit growth increments and ceilings, where a rise means a file autogrew. PostgreSQL has no file growth to watch — relations grow a page at a time and the disk-level answer is Volume Free Space. The PostgreSQL relative is *bloat growth* (`pg_table_bloat_stats` hourly and `pg_index_bloat` daily are collected); a "bloat growth" alert is a future item and would not share this name, because it answers a different question. |
| **TempDB Space**, **Volume Free Space**, **Long-Running / Failed Agent Job** | Yes (TempDB Space: 3-sample persistence gate) | — | SQL-only by construction (tempdb, host volumes via `sys.dm_os_volume_stats`, SQL Agent). TempDB Space sits behind the same shared persistence gate as High CPU since #3653 (A5): 3 consecutive collected `tempdb_stats` samples at or above the threshold to fire, the first collected sample below it to resolve, counted per collection rather than per 30 s sweep, and a reading that stops arriving freezes the gate rather than resolving it — measured on a 43-server production fleet, every one of one server's 22 crossings in 14 days was 1–4 samples long. TempDB Space fires with an explicit WARNING tier and no CRITICAL tier (#3653): the product has no measured "tempdb nearly full" bar to cite, and the percent's denominator is the growth ceiling where one exists and the allocation where not, so a single reserved-percent tier would mean two distances from failure. |
| **PostgreSQL Wraparound Risk**, **PostgreSQL Vacuum Horizon Blocked** | — | Yes | PostgreSQL-only by construction: MVCC transaction-id wraparound and the xmin horizon have no SQL Server counterpart. |
| **PostgreSQL Replication Slot Retention** | — | `pg_replication_slots` retained WAL, `wal_status` | The SQL Server analogue is a database whose log cannot truncate — `sys.databases.log_reuse_wait_desc` reading REPLICATION / AVAILABILITY_REPLICA / ACTIVE_TRANSACTION / LOG_BACKUP for consecutive collections. That column IS collected, but only as the load-time `database_config` snapshot (frequency 0), not as a time series, so a persistence-gated "Log Reuse Blocked" condition cannot be evaluated honestly today; it needs the column on a cadenced per-database collector (`database_states` is the natural home), which is a collector change with its own census and pins. Named here so the pair is on record: slot retention on PostgreSQL, log-reuse wait on SQL Server. |
| **Server unreachable**, **Collection stopped**, **Retention Held**, collector-health bands | Yes | Yes | Both — these are about the monitor, not the engine. |

**Same-statement pileup** is the one analysis finding evaluated on the COLLECTION cadence rather than the analysis interval, because the shape it describes does not survive a 30-minute wait: N concurrent sessions executing one statement, every copy past 10 seconds, at least one suspended on an IO-class wait, where that statement's own snapshots over the prior 45 minutes were sub-second — the live signature of a shared parameterized plan flipping to an IO-heavy shape, which takes every caller down together and is usually over in about ninety seconds. It is computed from the active-query snapshot collector alone, so it works on deployments with the Query Store readers disabled, and it rides the notification policy above unchanged (severity >= 1.5 pages; the severity scales with concurrent sessions × elapsed, so a brief threshold pileup persists as a visible finding without paging). Episodes of the same statement share one incident id, so a recurrence folds onto the same trail as an occurrence rather than arriving as a fresh surprise — and, like every standing condition, it does not re-page inside the cooldown unless it worsens. Unlike the scheduled pass it has no 24-hour data-span requirement: its evidence is the snapshot plus that statement's own recent history, so it works on a young install.

### analysis

Scheduled analysis: the triage engine runs per server on a cadence, persists its findings to `analysis_findings` (the Recommendations surfaces, `get_analysis_findings` and the web dashboard read them the instant they land) and, independently, notifies on the ones that cross a severity floor. The first four keys are control-plane knobs (`config_alert_settings`, editable from the Viewer and `update_alert_settings`); the last is file-level.

| Key | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `enabled` | `true` | Run the scheduled analysis pass at all |
| `intervalMinutes` | `30` | Per-server cadence (clamped 5–360) |
| `notificationsEnabled` | `true` | Deliver findings to the channels at all (analysis still runs and persists with this off) |
| `notifySeverity` | `1.5` | The severity floor (0–2) a finding must reach to be notify-worthy |
| `uncorroboratedRoute` | `"digest"` | **Where a notify-worthy but UNCORROBORATED finding goes** (#3712). `"digest"` keeps it off email, Teams, Slack, PagerDuty and the generic webhook: it is still persisted, still visible on the web dashboard and MCP the moment it fires, recorded in the alert history as `notification_type: digest` with the gate's reason (`routing_reason` on `get_alert_history`), and named once a day in the **Analysis Singles Digest**. `"page"` restores the pre-#3712 behaviour where every finding at or above `notifySeverity` pages. **The file half of a two-home knob** since V137: `config_alert_settings.analysis_uncorroborated_route` (nullable, CHECK `digest`/`page`) is the store half, and the running service resolves **store non-NULL wins over file** — a route set in the Viewer's Settings → Automated Analysis combo or through `update_alert_settings` is live within one collection sweep and wins over this value; NULL there (every store after the V137 upgrade, and every fresh seed — the seed deliberately does not copy this value in) defers to this key, read once at service start; a value here that is neither spelling defers to the shipped `digest`. `get_alert_settings` reports the effective route and which home decided it (`analysis.uncorroborated_route_source`: `store` / `file` / `default`); sending `analysis.uncorroborated_route: null` to `update_alert_settings` clears the column so this key governs again. |

**Confidence chooses the channel.** A finding earns a page by *corroboration*, never by severity alone: two or more facts in its story chain (each traversed edge is a fired predicate onto a fact that itself scored), or a matched co-fire check on its root fact (every check the scorer defines is a predicate over ANOTHER fact — a sibling anomaly in the same window, an absolute fact at its concerning bar, a wait that is significant), or one of the two by-construction stories (the absolution, the same-statement pileup). A lone fact — one fact, no matched check — is a *single*, and singles route to the digest under the default. The gate reads those components, not the confidence scalar, so a future re-weighting of the confidence formula cannot move the paging bar by itself. The extremity escape keeps its severity power (an extreme single still clears the floor and still leads its story), but its published intent — extreme *and* corroborated — is the enforced bar for a page. The measurement behind the default: the first day the recalibrated engine ran on a large production fleet it paged forty-plus times in seven hours on anomaly singles at severity 1.5–1.9 and confidence 0.20–0.35, interleaved with the handful that needed a human; most were true and redundant with a page something else had already sent.

**Escalation is the corroboration event.** A single that later gains a fact or a matched check pages at that moment as a NEW firing — the digest route cools in its own key namespace and digest rows are excluded from the page cooldown's restart seed, so an earlier digest entry can never be the cooldown that eats the page. An uncorroborated single riding in an incident with a corroborated finding is named on that page as co-fired, exactly as before.

**The Analysis Singles Digest** is the third daily document beside the Collector Cost Digest and the Fleet Sweep Rollup (family `reports`, INFO, one post per trailing day, delivered-today gate in the store, empty day posts nothing): "Anomaly singles: N across M servers", the top movers by severity and a by-family, by-server breakdown, each line carrying the same alert-history key (`Analysis: <category> [<hash>]`) and #1140 dedup fingerprints a page would have carried, so any single can be promoted to a full drill-down without archaeology.

### smtp

Email delivery is enabled when `host` and `from` are set — there is no separate enable flag. `to` is the default recipient list: an alert that no notification route covers goes there, so with `to` blank such an alert sends no email (the recipients of a covering route are used instead).

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `host` | `""` | |
| `port` | `587` | |
| `useSsl` | `true` | |
| `username` | *(none)* | For authenticated relays |
| `encryptedPassword` | *(none)* | Same `--encrypt-password` DPAPI pattern as SQL auth |
| `password` | *(none)* | A literal or an `env:NAME` / `file:/path` reference (#1804) — the non-Windows email path |
| `from` | `""` | |
| `to` | `""` | Comma-separated default recipients, for alerts no notification route covers; may be blank when routes name the recipients |
| `emailCooldownMinutes` | `15` | Email/webhook channel cooldown (clamped 1–120) |
| `emailSendResolutions` | `false` | Also send the email when an alert resolves |

### webhooks

A channel is enabled by a non-empty URL.

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `teamsUrl` | `""` | Teams incoming webhook |
