| `teamsProxy` | `""` | Optional proxy address |
| `slackUrl` | `""` | Slack incoming webhook |
| `slackProxy` | `""` | Optional proxy address |
| `slackSendResolutions` | `false` | Also post to Slack when an alert resolves |
| `teamsSendResolutions` | `false` | Also post to Teams when an alert resolves |
| `genericUrl` | `""` | Generic webhook endpoint, stored sealed |
| `genericHeaders` | `""` | JSON object of request headers, stored sealed |
| `genericBodyTemplate` | `""` | JSON body with `{{metric}}` and `{{server}}` placeholders, or empty for the shared default |
| `genericProxy` | `""` | Optional proxy address |
| `genericSendResolutions` | `false` | Also post to the generic webhook when an alert resolves |
| `pagerDutyRoutingKey` | `""` | PagerDuty Events API v2 routing key, stored sealed |
| `pagerDutyUseEuRegion` | `false` | Use the EU PagerDuty endpoint |
| `pagerDutyProxy` | `""` | Optional proxy address |
| `pagerDutyAutoResolve` | `false` | Close the PagerDuty incident when the alert resolves |

Webhook and heartbeat posts do not connect to a link-local address (169.254.0.0/16, fe80::/10) or to the cloud instance addresses `fd00:ec2::254`, `168.63.129.16` and `100.100.100.200`; loopback and private addresses stay allowed. The address check applies to direct connections: when a channel posts through a proxy, Darling checks the address the URL itself spells out when it is saved (a trailing dot and the IPv4-mapped, NAT64 and IPv4-compatible forms included) but cannot see what the proxy connects to, so the proxy decides what it can reach.

#### Generic webhook: status, one key per alert, and incident.io (#6718)

The generic webhook posts the JSON in `genericBodyTemplate` to `genericUrl`, with `genericHeaders` added. Every `{{token}}` in the template is replaced as the body is built, and text tokens are JSON-escaped. The tokens are `{{metric}}`, `{{server}}`, `{{value}}`, `{{threshold}}`, `{{severity}}`, `{{context}}`, `{{detail}}`, `{{timestamp}}`, `{{status}}`, `{{dedup_key}}`, `{{resource_name}}`, `{{database}}`, `{{triage_url}}`, `{{context_json}}`, `{{incidents_json}}` and `{{investigation_json}}`. The last three are raw JSON, so use them unquoted.

- `{{status}}` is `firing` on an alert and `resolved` on a resolution. The Settings window's test send says `firing`. Resolutions go out only when `genericSendResolutions` is on (see "Send resolutions" below).
- `{{dedup_key}}` is the same for an alert and its resolution. A resolution renders the key the alert's own post rendered, so a receiver that matches on a key can close what the alert opened. The key is the first incident's own key, or `<server id>:<metric>` when the alert has no incident. A restart between the alert and its resolution does not change this.
- `{{metric}}` on a resolution is the resolution's title, such as `CPU Resolved`, and not the alert's name. A template that needs the alert's name on a resolution can build it from `{{server}}` and `{{dedup_key}}`. The resolution's `{{detail}}` text also names the alert (`Alert: ...`).
- `{{value}}` on a resolution is the word `resolved`, and `{{threshold}}` is empty.
- A recovery notice that is its own alert, such as "Server Restored" or "AG Replica Reconnected", is sent as an alert and not as a resolution, so its `{{status}}` is `firing` and its key is its own.

**Example: open and close an alert in incident.io.** Create an HTTP alert source in incident.io. It gives you a source config ID and a token.

| Key | Value |
|---|---|
| `genericUrl` | `https://api.incident.io/v2/alert_events/http/<ALERT_SOURCE_CONFIG_ID>` |
| `genericHeaders` | `{"Authorization": "Bearer <ALERT_SOURCE_TOKEN>"}` |
| `genericSendResolutions` | `true`. Without it incident.io never gets a `resolved` event and the alert stays open. |

```json
{
  "title": "{{metric}} on {{server}}",
  "status": "{{status}}",
  "deduplication_key": "{{dedup_key}}",
  "description": "{{detail}}",
  "source_url": "{{triage_url}}",
  "metadata": {
    "severity": "{{severity}}",
    "server": "{{server}}",
    "database": "{{database}}",
    "resource": "{{resource_name}}",
    "context": {{context_json}}
  }
}
```

Use that as `genericBodyTemplate`. incident.io's alert event takes `title`, `status` (`firing` or `resolved`), `deduplication_key`, `description`, `source_url` and `metadata`, and has no priority field. To set a priority, see the next paragraph.

**Set the alert's priority from `{{severity}}`.** `{{severity}}` is one of Darling's own words: `CRITICAL`, `WARNING`, `INFO` or `ALERT`. incident.io maps it to a priority. On the alert source, set Priority to a dynamic value and add a Query expression on Payload. Give it a step that extracts fields from JSON:

```
$.metadata.severity == "CRITICAL" ? "<URGENT_PRIORITY_ID>" : "<OTHER_PRIORITY_ID>"
```

Parse the result into Alert Priority. Use each priority's ID or slug, not its display name, because a display name parses to nothing. The editor's preview shows the priority each value resolves to.

What to know about incident.io:

- incident.io allows 120 events a minute for each alert source. A request over that gets a 429 and incident.io drops the event. Darling makes one attempt and does not retry a 429, so the channel records a failed send.
- Tested on 2026-10-24 against one incident.io HTTP alert source, with the template above and Darling's own alert and resolution sends:
  - The alert and its resolution carried the same `{{dedup_key}}`, and the resolution closed the alert.
  - The alert kept the title from its first event. The resolution's title did not replace it.
  - A key with spaces and colons, such as `3:High CPU`, was accepted.
  - An empty `source_url` was accepted. `{{triage_url}}` is empty when Darling has no triage link, and the alert then has no link.
  - Nested objects in `metadata` were accepted. Whether a Priority or attribute expression can read a nested path was not tested.
  - A `resolved` event with a key that matches no open alert got a 202 and changed nothing.
  - A new alert with the same key after a resolution opened a new alert in incident.io.
  - An event with no `status` got a 422.
  - With no Priority expression, the alerts got a default priority. Add the expression if your alert routes use priority.
- Not confirmed: any limit on the key's length, and the largest body incident.io accepts.

Another receiver that matches on a key and takes a status works the same way. Darling has no native incident.io channel.

#### Notification routes — sending alert families to different channels

The channels above (and the SMTP, PagerDuty and generic-webhook ones the Viewer's Settings window adds) are the **parent** channel set: one destination per channel type, and by default every alert goes to every configured one. One stream then interleaves four audiences — the monitor's own health, scheduled reports, Agent job failures, and the performance pages the channel exists for — and the reader routes in their head on every post. Since V131 (#3598) a sparse **routes** table layered over the parent row lets the operator make that call once: **Settings → Notifications → Manage Notification Routes…**, or read it back with `get_notification_routes`.

A route names what it matches and a destination per channel type, and **an empty channel on a route is passed on: to the next less specific matching route that sets it, and to the parent's when none does** — so a route can point one family's Slack at a second webhook and leave PagerDuty exactly as the parent has it, or put a PagerDuty key on the `performance` route *alone* with none on the parent, which is how "only pages page" is spelled. What a route matches is either a **family** or one **exact metric name** (as `metric_name` reads in Alert History; an exact route also catches that alert's recovery — `Server Restored` routes as `Server Unreachable`, `AG Replica Reconnected` as `AG Replica Disconnected`). The four families are a closed taxonomy the product writes down (`AlertFamily`), pinned by a census so a new alert cannot ship unclassified:

| Family | Owns |
|---|---|
| `self-monitor` | The monitor about itself: `Collection Stopped`, `Capture Down`, `Compression Job Stuck`, `Refresh Job Stuck`, `Retention Job Stuck`, `Store Job Failing`, `Store Disk Pressure`, `Store Runtime Upgrade`, `Store Job Over Cadence`, `Retention Held`, `Custom Alert Rules Unhealthy`, `Stale Mute Rules`, `Web TLS Certificate Expiring`, `MCP TLS Certificate Expiring`, `Store Settings Need Attention`, `Collector Cost Regression`, `Collection Falling Behind`, `Collection Gap At Start` (once per start, when the service was not collecting for 15 minutes or more before it started), `Collection Gaps In History` (once a day, after 03:00Z, for the previous UTC day: an hour whose servers, or perfmon collection passes, fell below half of the usual for that hour; changing the perfmon collection schedule can trigger it for a few days) |
| `reports` | Scheduled prose, never a page: `Collector Cost Digest`, `Fleet Sweep Rollup`, `Analysis Singles Digest` |
| `agent-jobs` | `Failed Agent Job`, `Long-Running Job`, `Agent Not Running` |
| `performance` | Everything about a monitored server the on-call is paged for: `High CPU`, `Blocking Detected`, `Blocking Wait Time`, `Deadlocks Detected`, `Poison Wait`, `Runnable Queue`, `Client Timeouts`, `Long-Running Query`, `tempdb Space`, `Volume Free Space`, `Version Store (PVS)`, `Database File Growth`, `Database State`, `Forced Plan Failing`, the three PostgreSQL outage predictors, the five `AG …` alerts, `Server Unreachable` / `Server Restored`, every `Custom:<id>` rule and every `Analysis: …` finding — and any metric the taxonomy does not name, so an unclassified alert lands where every alert landed before routes existed |
| `external` | Alerts another service raised through its own database login, named `External:<source>:<key>`. With no route they go to the parent channels like any other alert. |

Resolution is **per channel** (scoped routes add a first level, described below): an enabled route matching the metric exactly, then one matching its family, then the parent — and within a level the lowest `route_id` with a non-empty column. Routing sits *after* the delivery cooldown and the per-metric repeat budget, so it changes where a post lands and never whether it is sent; a store with zero routes posts exactly what it posted before, byte for byte. Every delivered alert-history row records where it went (`route` on `get_alert_history`: the family, the matched `route_id`, and each delivered channel with the route that supplied it), so a misrouted post can be traced. What a route does **not** do: an empty column inherits, so a route cannot *silence* a channel the parent has — put the channel on a route instead of the parent, as above; proxies, the generic channel's headers/template and the PagerDuty region stay on the parent (they say how a channel type is reached, not where a family lands); and the email column only names recipients, in place of the default `to` list or with it blank — SMTP host, from-address and credentials live on the parent. The pre-routes workaround — a generic-webhook payload template branching on `{{metric}}` in an external router — still works; it just is no longer the only way.

##### Scoping a route to a server or a tag (V181, #5750)

A route can apply to all servers (the default), to one server, or to one tag. A tag route covers every server that has the tag or any of its sub-tags. A route has one scope at most.

You pick the scope in the Applies to box of the route dialog. The server box shows the display name, and the tag box shows the tag path, for example `Production / East`. With a scope you can also pick `Every metric (*)`. That route catches every alert for its scope that no more specific route of the same scope matches. A route for all servers cannot use `*`.

The most specific route wins, in this order:

1. The scope: a server route first, then a tag route, then a route for all servers.
2. The metric: the exact name, then the paired firing (a recovery follows its alert), then the family, then `*`.
3. For tag routes at the same metric level, the deeper tag wins. Deeper means more parent tags.
4. If two routes still tie, the lowest route id wins.

A scoped route replaces the default for each channel it sets, as every route does. A channel that the route leaves empty still goes to the next route that sets it, or to the Notifications channel. An alert never goes to two destinations on one channel. The generic webhook headers and the body template stay app-wide. A scoped route changes only where the post goes.

If the server or the tag of a route is removed later, the route is **orphaned**. An orphaned route matches nothing, so its alerts go to the next route or to the default. The Notification Routes window shows `Orphaned: server removed` or `Orphaned: tag deleted` in the Status column, and `get_notification_routes` returns `orphaned`. The service never deletes an orphaned route for you. Delete it, or re-scope it, in the window. If the viewer cannot read the tag data, a tag route shows `Not active: tag data unreadable` and not `OK`.

A bring-your-own store needs one step after the service upgrades the store to V181. Run `tools/provision-roles.sql` again, so the `viewer` and `mcp` roles can read the two new columns. The compose store and managed provisioning do this on their own.

Destinations are bearer secrets, so the `viewer` and `mcp` roles never read a webhook URL or key: the table's generated `configured_channels` column says *which* channels a route sets, and the MCP surface administers routes in full: `create_notification_route` makes a route (with no destination of its own, each channel goes where it went before, to the next matching route and then to the channels configured in Settings), `update_notification_route` changes a route's match, scope, enabled flag, resolution override and fallback, and both set a route's own destinations (a Teams, Slack or generic webhook URL, a PagerDuty routing key, email recipients; `-` removes one). The service seals each webhook value for the route's own row, in the transaction that creates or edits the route, exactly as the Viewer does, so a value copied to another route does not open there; an answer never shows a webhook URL or key: it says whether one is saved (with the host for Teams and Slack only), it shows the recipient list in full, and the undo of a destination change carries the earlier recipient list and says to pass an earlier webhook URL or key again. `set_notification_route_enabled`, `set_notification_route_fallback` and `delete_notification_route` change the rest. A route with an explicit agent setting (Off included) keeps its match and scope in the Viewer, and an agent route is changed only in the Viewer. The `mcp` role has no INSERT on the table, no write on the destination columns and no read of the webhook and key columns (it reads the recipient list and `configured_channels`); the route tools write through `config.create_notification_route`, `config.update_notification_route` and `config.set_notification_route_destinations`, functions that store only a sealed value or an empty one for a webhook and refuse an agent route. `config.set_notification_route_destinations` hands back only whether a webhook or key was saved before, for the columns the call names, and never a stored value; for a changed webhook value it accepts only the sealed form (`sealed:v1:`, the key id and base64, at most 16384 characters), and for a changed recipient list it refuses control characters, angle brackets, double quotes and more than 50 entries. Every writer (the Viewer's settings and route windows, the MCP route tools) refuses a Teams, Slack or generic webhook URL at save unless it is an absolute http or https URL with a host and no user name or password and whose host is not a link-local or cloud instance address written out, and refuses an email recipient list that is not comma separated plain addresses (the function the `mcp` role writes through checks only the shapes listed above, and the service checks the rest before it calls it), and a direct webhook post never connects to a link-local address (169.254.0.0/16, fe80::/10), whether it came from the URL, a DNS answer or a redirect (a channel that posts through a proxy is only checked against the URL's own address at save, because Darling cannot see what the proxy connects to); loopback and private addresses stay allowed for internal receivers. Routes hot-reload through the same `config_version` beacon as the parent row (the V131 trigger), so a change lands on the next firing with no restart.

##### Send resolutions (#5825)

A resolution is the message that an alert has cleared: the condition that fired it is no longer there. Each channel (Slack, Teams, the generic webhook and email) has a **Send resolutions** box in the Viewer's Settings > Notifications, off by default. When it is on, a resolution goes only to the destinations of that channel that the alert's fire reached; an alert whose fire was not sent gets no resolution. A service restart between the fire and the clear does not lose this: the clear reads the fire's alert-history row, which names each channel that delivered and the route that supplied it, and sends to that route's destination as it is now (or the channel's own destination when no route supplied it). A route deleted or disabled since the fire gets nothing, with or without a restart between the fire and the clear: nothing is sent to that channel, and the history row says so. A mute rule stops a resolution when it matches the alert's fire, including a rule scoped to a database, wait type, job or query. Each resolution carries the server, the alert, when it fired and cleared, how long it lasted, the last value its fire sent and the fire's triage link. In alert history, the resolution row records what its send did: `get_alert_history` reports `resolution_sent_at` (UTC, set when a channel delivered), `resolution_channels` and `resolution_outcome` (`sent`, `failed: <channels>`, `muted` or `route deleted or disabled: <channels>`), null on every other row; the Viewer's Alert History grid has a **Resolution** column and Alert Details shows the three fields; the web Alerts page shows them in the row's detail. A route can override the channel setting for its own destinations: in the route dialog, **Send resolutions** is `Use channel setting` (the default), `Always` or `Never`. PagerDuty is not one of the four: its incidents close through **Auto-resolve the incident when the alert's condition clears**. Over MCP, `get_alert_settings` reports the four settings as `delivery.send_resolutions`, `update_alert_settings` changes them, and `get_notification_routes` reports each route's `send_resolutions` (null means the channel decides). A bring-your-own store needs `tools/provision-roles.sql` run again after the service upgrades the store to V187, so the `viewer` and `mcp` roles can read the new columns.

##### Hand an alert to your own agent (#5632)

Darling ships no agent. It can hand an alert to one you run yourself. The route sets how far the agent can go, from reading and reporting to changing alert settings and monitored servers. A gate records every change. The guide [Agent dispatch](../docs/darling-agent-dispatch.md) covers each step. In short:

1. **Turn it on.** Set `mcp.enabled` and `mcp.allowInvestigations` to `true` in `darling.json`, and give MCP a bearer token (`network.encryptedToken` or `network.token`). Keep `mcp.allowWrites` off. With both on, the write tools win and the investigation tools stay off. `--validate-config` and the start-up log warn about a setup that cannot work. The warning never changes the exit code or stops the start.
2. **Pick the alerts.** In the Viewer's route editor, set **Agent investigation** on a route. `Investigate` (Level 1) reads and reports. `Act` (Level 2) also changes alert settings on the request's server. `Act on servers` (Level 3) also runs server actions. `Off` stops it for that route, and `Inherit` asks the next broader route. The route also sets a limit per hour (20 by default) and per day (100). It sets a lease (15 minutes) and an expiry (4 hours) too. A muted alert, a recovery, a scheduled report and a digest never open a request.
3. **The agent's loop.** `wait_for_investigation_requests` (or `list_investigation_requests`) finds a request. `claim_investigation_request` takes it and returns the brief. `get_investigation_evidence` reads the evidence that Darling froze. `submit_investigation_report` ends the work. At Level 2 and Level 3, the agent changes things first and reports last, because writes refuse after the report. A report can ask for a later follow-up request with `follow_up_at`.
4. **Read the results.** The Viewer's **Manage Investigations...** window and the web alert page show each request, its brief, its report and its journal. In the Viewer, a person can close a request and approve or reject a note. A person can also write a note and set the environment profile. The **Agent changes** list shows every change an agent made, and each one has an **Undo** (see "Agent Changes: List, Undo and the Switch").

`mcp.dataDetail` masks the brief and every tool answer at the level you set. [`AGENTS.example.md`](AGENTS.example.md) is a starting point for your agent's instructions.

At Level 1 an agent reads and reports. At Level 2 it can change alert settings on the request's server only: it can mute one metric, write or change a custom alert rule, set a per-server threshold override and merge deadlock signatures (see "Level 2: agents change mute rules and custom alert rules"). At Level 3 it can also force or unforce a Query Store plan, evict a cached plan and kill a session. A server action needs the server's own action switch and a remediation login (see the `remediationUsername` key and "Ending a PostgreSQL session"). An agent turns an alert off only with a mute, never mutes the service's own alerts, and gives a reason for every change. A note that an agent proposes waits for a person to approve it before it reaches a later brief.

   **Tell Darling whether a report was right (#5913).** Open a reported request and say whether its report was right, partly right or wrong, what it should have said (`real`, `false_positive` or `needs_person`), the step the agent missed, and a comment. Do it in the viewer's Investigations window, on the **Report Feedback** tab, or on the web Admin page's **Report Feedback** tab. Each person has one feedback per report; saving again replaces it, and the author is the login you are signed in as. Feedback is recorded only under the admin login: the viewer needs `postgres.connectAs` set to `"admin"`, and on a store where the web connects as the read-only viewer role (managed and compose stores) the web tab lists feedback with no form, says so, and feedback is given in the viewer instead (the web asks the store whether its login may write, so an admin sign-in on such a store gets no form either). Feedback feeds two things. `get_investigation_reports` returns each report's feedback next to it, and, for a time window, a count per alert family of how many investigations ended as false positives, once as the agent reported them and once after people's corrections (a correction can raise or lower the count). The settled verdict of a request is the corrected verdict of the newest feedback that gave one; a later feedback that gives no corrected verdict does not displace an earlier one that did. The same counts are at the top of both Report Feedback tabs, each stating its window: the viewer's tab defaults to the requests opened in the last 30 days (a 7, 30 or 90 day picker), the web tab to the last 7 days; both list the newest 200 reports, and the counts cover every reported request in the window, not only the listed ones.

### Practice requests: how an agent does on cases you already judged (#5918)

Before you trust an agent with Level 2, watch it work cases you have already judged. A practice request replays a reported case that has feedback (see "Tell Darling whether a report was right" above) and scores the agent's report against that feedback.

1. **Start one in the Viewer.** In **Manage Investigations...** open the **Practice** tab, pick a judged case (a reported request somebody gave feedback on), and press **Start Practice Request**. The viewer needs an admin seat; a read-only seat can read the scores but not start a request. A case with no report, no feedback, or that is itself a practice request cannot be replayed, and a case with a practice request still open is not replayed twice.
2. **The agent claims it like any request.** The practice request copies the case's route, server, family, alert count and alerts, and is stamped Level 1. The brief it gets says it is practice and holds none of the case's answers: no earlier report, no prior link, no note that the case's report proposed, and no verdict, corrected verdict, missed step or comment. The agent cannot read the replayed case while its practice is live: under the investigations arm (`mcp.allowInvestigations`, writes off), `get_investigation_reports` leaves out the case's report, feedback and journal for as long as a practice request replaying it reads open or claimed (not once its report is submitted, or it has expired); the case's request row and every other report still show. The same holds for every other brief: while that practice is live, no other brief's `earlier_reports` or `prior_links` carries the case's report, and a practice request's own report and links are never history for any brief. The writes arm (an operator's token) and the web Admin page read everything. `list_investigation_requests` and the Viewer's Requests tab show which requests are practice: a practice row carries `practice_of`, the id of the case it replays, and every other row has no such field. The family counts in a window read still count the case, as one among many; they never count a practice request. The score stays advisory.
3. **A practice request is not real traffic.** Every Level 2 action on it is refused with `practice`, a live alert never joins it, it does not count against the route's hourly or daily limit, and the Report Feedback list and the per-family counts leave it out; its report shows only in the Practice scores. An approved note that the replayed case proposed still reaches other briefs, as any approved note does.
4. **The score.** The agent's report verdict is compared with the answer the feedback settles on. The newest corrected verdict, if anyone gave one, is the answer. Otherwise `right` makes the original report's verdict the answer, `partly` makes it the answer at half credit, and `wrong` with no corrected verdict names no answer, so that case is shown as "No answer" and is not scored. The score is points over possible credit per agent, counting each agent's newest report on each case. Cases whose feedback names a missed step are counted so you can read the practice report against that step yourself; Darling does not judge the step.
5. **Where scores show.** The Viewer's Practice tab, and the web dashboard's Admin page, **Practice Scores** tab (list-only). The score is advisory: it turns no route on or off.

Provisioning: the `admin` role holds INSERT on `config.investigation_requests` (the columns a request is opened with) and `config.investigation_request_alerts`, so the Viewer can open a practice request. Run the provisioning script again on a bring-your-own store; managed and compose stores re-apply it on start.

### Export the judged cases to test your own agent (#6236)

Every case somebody gave feedback on (see "Tell Darling whether a report was right" above) is a labelled test case: you know what the alert was, what the agent was given, what it concluded and whether a person agreed. The export writes them all out so you can replay them against your own agent.

```
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --export-investigations cases.jsonl [--since 2026-09-01] [--until 2026-10-01] [--server <name>] [--verdict wrong] [--force] [--config <path>]
```

- **One case per line, JSON lines.** Each line is one object: `request_id`, `alert` (the server, the family, the first metric, when and how often it fired), `brief` (the alert and the evidence the agent was given), `report` (what it wrote: verdict, root cause, summary, evidence by basis) and `feedback` (every verdict, corrected verdict, missed step and comment people recorded, with the author).
- **Filters.** `--since` and `--until` are dates (a date alone is its midnight UTC) and select by when the request was opened; `--since` must be before `--until`. `--server` takes one registered server's storage name or display name exactly (case does not matter, a part of a name matches nothing, and a name two registrations share is refused with both listed); a server that was disabled can still be exported. `--verdict` keeps the cases a person marked `right`, `partly` or `wrong`. Without filters you get every case with feedback, newest first, up to 2000; the verb says so when the filter held more. A practice request is not a case and is left out, and so is a case a live practice request replays (the verb says how many were held back). While the practice is live that case is also taken out of every other case's earlier reports and prior links, in the exported brief and in the stored brief `get_investigation_reports` serves an investigating agent; when the practice ends it is back in all of them.
- **The file.** It is written beside the target as a hidden partial file, then moved into place. The partial file is readable by you only on Linux and macOS, and on Windows by you, the administrators and the service's operator group (the same people who can read `darling.json`). Ctrl+C or a failure deletes it, and if it cannot be deleted the verb prints its path. A folder, a link or an existing file is refused (an existing file is replaced only with `--force`, and one that appears while the store is being read is never replaced without it). The verb prints the full path it wrote.
- **Masked as the reports are, on both paths.** Query text and other collected text are masked at your `mcp.dataDetail` level, the way `get_investigation_reports` masks them (a `maskLiterals` or `metadataOnly` install exports masked text, with the same login numbering), and a password statement in a report, a comment or the brief is withheld at every level. The command line and the web download run the same code, so the same cases at the same level give the same bytes. The command line reads `mcp.dataDetail` from the file when it runs (the verb prints the level it used); the web download uses the level the service read when it started.
- **From the web dashboard.** `GET /api/investigation-export` (the same filters as query parameters: `since`, `until`, `server`, `verdict`) downloads the same lines as `investigation-cases.jsonl`. Only a signed-in dashboard account with the admin role may download: a viewer account gets a 403, and so does a dashboard with no sign-in configured. The store login must also be able to record feedback, the `admin` role (`postgres.webConnectionString` on a bring-your-own store); the viewer role gets a 403. The route counts as a heavy read from its first call, and only one export runs at a time: a second answers busy (429 with a retry-after) until the first has been sent. The read slot is held while the store is read and released before the file is sent. The cases are written one at a time to a file in the service's own data folder (an `exports` folder beside the store's data folder), which is deleted when the send ends and removed by the system if the service stops in the middle. No MCP tool serves the export.

**How to use it.** After you change your agent's prompt or model, replay the cases: give your agent each line's `alert` and `brief` the way a claimed request would, collect its new verdict, and compare it with the recorded `feedback`. A person's corrected verdict, when there is one, is the answer; otherwise `right` means the original `report.verdict` was correct, and `wrong` means it was not. Count where the new agent agrees, and read the cases where it now disagrees. For a live trial inside Darling use a practice request (above), which scores the agent for you.

### mcp

The embedded MCP server, over Streamable HTTP bound to `localhost` by default (see [Opt-in Network Endpoints (LAN)](#opt-in-network-endpoints-lan) to reach it — and the store — from the LAN). It exposes nearly all the tool names Lite exposes. It adds Darling-only reads (PostgreSQL, fleet, store). It also adds Darling-only write surfaces, each off until you turn on its switch: Custom Views management, alert tuning, server onboarding, collector control, quiet windows, query tags, agent actions and investigations. The default path (`/`) serves every tool; a client that only needs the fact-finding path can point at `/core` instead, a fixed subset of about 70-90 tools reachable from `list_servers`, `get_fleet_overview`, `analyze_server` and `get_tool_guide` by following each finding's `next_tools`, served behind the exact same Host, token and CIDR checks as `/`:

- **Diagnostic-analysis tools** — `analyze_server`, `get_analysis_facts`, `compare_analysis`, `audit_config`, `get_analysis_findings`, `mute_analysis_finding`, `unmute_analysis_finding`.
- **Plan-analysis tools** — `analyze_query_plan` (by `query_hash`), `analyze_procedure_plan` (by `sql_handle`), `analyze_query_store_plan` (by `database_name` + `query_id`), `analyze_plan_xml` (raw showplan XML, no fetch), `get_plan_xml` (raw stored plan XML by `query_hash`), and the five raw-plan reads behind the web grids' plan buttons: `get_query_store_plan_xml` (by `database_name` + `query_id`, optional `plan_id`), `get_procedure_plan_xml` (by `sql_handle`), `get_active_query_plan_xml` (by an Active Queries row's `collection_time` + `session_id`, optional `request_id`, `live`), `get_blocking_plan_xml` (by a `get_blocking` row's `event_time` and both spids, `side=blocking` for the blocker's plan) and `get_deadlock_plan_xml` (by a `get_deadlocks` row's `collection_time` and `deadlock_time`). `get_query_repro_script` builds a paste-ready T-SQL repro script from a stored query's text and plan (`kind` = `query_hash`, `query_store` or `active_snapshot`, plus that kind's key); store-only, nothing runs and no live plan is fetched. The `analyze_*` tools run the shared execution-plan analyzer over the plan XML the collectors already captured into the store — a stored-plan read, never a live query against the monitored server. `analyze_query_plan`/`get_plan_xml` accept an optional `database_name`, and `analyze_query_store_plan` an optional `plan_id`, to pin the exact stored plan when the caller knows it.
- **Core data-read tools** — the diagnostic reads an assistant needs to investigate a server, each a stored read of the collected data (never a live query against the monitored server):
  - *Resource metrics* — `get_cpu_utilization`, `get_wait_stats`, `get_wait_trend`, `get_wait_types` (the distinct observed wait types, to pick one for `get_wait_trend`), `get_memory_stats`, `get_memory_clerks`, `get_file_io_stats`, `get_tempdb_trend`, `get_perfmon_stats`.
  - *Query performance* — `get_top_queries_by_cpu`, `get_top_procedures_by_cpu`, `get_query_store_top`, `get_query_store_query_history` (one query's per-plan history behind the web grid's History button; these hand back the `query_hash` / `sql_handle` / `query_id` + `plan_id` keys the plan-analysis tools consume).
  - *Query Store clutter* — `get_query_store_clutter` (#3797), composed from rows already collected rather than from a new query against the server: per database the `query_store` collector's read cost (how often and by how much it was the slowest fan-out item, off `collection_log`'s V80 rollup), plan churn (`plan_id` cardinality on the raw `query_store_stats` rows — plans per query at p95, arrivals per day, the one-shot fraction) and the newest `query_store_health` options row, each with raw numbers and a decomposed `verdict`; plus ONE per-server overhead block — the non-sleep `QDS_*` wait deltas with the four sleep waits `IgnoredWaitDefaults` drops named as excluded, and `MEMORYCLERK_QUERYDISKSTORE`. A database whose `readonly_reason` carries the readable-secondary bit is excluded by architecture with the reason on the row (a replica's Query Store is its primary's); `query_capture_mode` is the V137 (#3796) column read verbatim, with `capture_mode_known` beside it — a null there is a capture older than the rung, never `NONE` — and the churn recommendations name the mode (`ALL` beside churn is the switch-to-`AUTO` case, `AUTO` beside it points at the workload); `fleet_median` is opt-in because it walks two raw hypertables fleet-wide. The same rows reach the web server page's **Queries** tab (one fetch, three panels: the per-database rows, the per-server wait block and the memory clerk) and the Viewer's **Queries → Query Store Clutter** sub-tab, both reading the same statements and the same composition, so a verdict and a remedy are worded once.
  - *Discovery / health* — `list_servers` (with collection-freshness status, each row's engine — `engine_kind` is the registry token, `engine_version` the engine-aware version label, and `sql_version` a **deprecated** alias of `engine_version` kept so existing consumers keep working — and the [declared peer stores](#peers) when the fleet is split across several Darling boxes), `get_collection_health`, `get_server_properties`.

  These are the tools the analysis findings' `next_tools` recommendations point at, so a client following a finding's advice resolves them on this same server. Result shapes match Lite's (the store is Lite's collector schema); where Lite and the Dashboard's shapes diverge, Darling follows Lite — the shape its collector-mirror store can serve faithfully.
- **Diagnostic-depth data-read tools** — deeper reads for a blocking / deadlock / session / configuration / storage investigation, each a stored read:
  - *Blocking / deadlocks* — `get_blocking` (blocked/blocking pairs from the blocked-process-report XE + the always-on DMV fallback), `get_deadlocks`, `get_deadlock_detail` (raw graph XML), `get_blocked_process_xml` (raw report XML), and the per-minute count series `get_blocking_trend` / `get_deadlock_trend`.
  - *Sessions* — `get_session_stats` (latest per-application connection counts), `get_active_queries` (captured running-query snapshots), `get_waiting_tasks`.
  - *Config* — the change history `get_server_config_changes`, `get_database_config_changes`, `get_trace_flag_changes`, plus the latest-snapshot pair `get_database_scoped_config` / `get_query_store_health` (per-database Query Store health: actual vs desired state, readonly_reason decoded, storage vs cap) and the current-config snapshots `get_server_config` / `get_database_config` / `get_trace_flags` (what sp_configure / sys.databases / the active trace flags are set to **right now** — the companion to the `*_changes` diffs, which are empty on a stable server).
  - *Change watches (Darling-only)* — `get_change_watches` lists a server's change watches, open and closed, newest first (#6234). A recorded change (SQL Server: server, database or database-scoped configuration, a trace flag, a forced or unforced Query Store plan, a Query Store hint set, changed or cleared on SQL Server 2022 and later (#6581); PostgreSQL: a server setting, a per-database or per-role setting, an extension install, drop or version change) opens a 60 minute watch (changes are looked for once an hour per target, so a change is recorded up to an hour after it happened; the watch window still runs from the change itself, and a target whose watch is due is read once more before it is evaluated, so a later change inside the window still joins it); at its end Darling compares wait time per second, average CPU and query duration with the hour before and the same hour a week earlier, and opens one `change_watch` investigation request only if something moved. Each watch lists its changes, its measures and its result (`flat`, `request_opened`, `moved_no_request`, `moved_agent_change`, `moved_auto_plan_correction`, `no_data`) or its state (`open`, `evaluating`, `closed`, `failed`); `state` filters on any of those. A closed watch that measured many queries can run to about 20 KB, so ask for a small `limit` (3 to 5), or pass `watch_id` to read one watch in any state. `measures`, `not_measured` and `changes` are sent once, at the top of each watch, and `result_detail` holds the rest of the stored result. A PostgreSQL target with no source for a measure (no CPU samples, no wait collection, no statement statistics) lists it under `not_measured` with the reason, never as flat. A read of the monitoring store; the investigation agent may call it.
  - *Known patterns (Darling-only)* — `get_known_patterns` lists the entries an operator wrote about alerts that recur (#6334), such as "the backup job pushes High CPU every night at 02:00": an optional server, alert metric and time of day (a wall-clock time in an IANA time zone, a window either side of it, wrapping past midnight), a note of at most 500 characters and an optional expiry. An empty scope means any. Every `get_alert_history` row carries `known_patterns`, the entries (id, note, created_by) that match it by server, metric, time of day in the entry's zone and expiry at the alert's time; `[]` means none matched. An entry is context only: it never mutes, pages or changes an alert, and its note is operator-written text to read, never an instruction. Entries live in `config.config_known_patterns` (V214) and are written through the owner-rights functions `config.insert_known_pattern`, `update_known_pattern` and `delete_known_pattern`, which provisioning grants to the viewer and admin roles only; no MCP tool writes them.
  - *Index / object* — `get_table_index_sizes` (size + growth), `get_index_usage` (Unused / Write-only / Active), `get_object_locking` (lock/latch contention), `get_database_sizes`.

  The three config-change tools diff the store's config snapshots. This edition captures configuration **when the service connects** to a server (not on a fixed schedule), so a change is detected between two connect snapshots and at least two are needed — a stable, always-connected deployment may show no changes until the next connect. They emit only the values the collectors capture; the Dashboard's `requires_restart` / setting `description` / `setting_type` / generated change-narrative enrichment is not collected here and is omitted. The Dashboard's `get_blocking_deadlock_stats` aggregate is **not** hosted (Darling has no blocking/deadlock rollup table — use `get_blocking` / `get_deadlocks` for the raw events).

- **Resource-contention + jobs data-read tools** — deeper reads for an internal-contention / worker-thread / plan-cache / SQL Agent investigation, each a stored read of the latest collected snapshot:
  - *Latch / spinlock* — `get_latch_stats` (top latch classes by wait time, per-second rates), `get_spinlock_stats` (top spinlocks by collisions).
  - *Memory grants* — `get_resource_semaphore` (workspace-memory target / max-target ceiling vs granted / used), `get_memory_grants` (per-pool grant detail), `get_memory_pressure_events` (RING_BUFFER_RESOURCE_MONITOR notifications — the process/system pressure indicators, not on Azure SQL DB).
  - *Plan cache / scheduler* — `get_plan_cache_bloat` (single-use vs multi-use + bloat level), `get_cpu_scheduler_pressure` (runnable queue, worker utilization, pressure level).
  - *Jobs* — `get_running_jobs` (running SQL Agent jobs vs historical average / p95).

  The Dashboard's per-class latch `severity` / `description` / `recommendation`, spinlock `description`, plan-cache `bloat_level`, and CPU-scheduler `pressure_level` / `recommendation` are the Dashboard / reporting-view CASE derivations (not collected columns), reproduced service-side so the full result shape is served. Per-second latch/spinlock rates divide by each row's stored `sample_interval_seconds` (the measured seconds its deltas accrued over, stored beside the deltas on every delta family since V128 — SQL Server and PostgreSQL alike) and are `null` rather than 0 when that interval was unknowable — a restart, not quiet. `get_resource_semaphore` emits the Dashboard's `sample_interval_seconds` again now that the memory-grant collector stores it (V128), `null` with `interval_known: false` on a restart marker or a pre-V128 row; `max_target_memory_mb`, the workspace-memory ceiling, is added since the store carries it.

- **PostgreSQL data-read tools** — the read surface for a PostgreSQL target's collectors, each a stored read. The `get_pg_*` family is larger than the reads called out here (see [PostgreSQL targets](#postgresql-targets)):
  - *Waits and queries* — `get_pg_wait_stats` (top wait events in the window, decoded to type + event name), `get_pg_top_queries` (query shapes by total execution time, carrying Aurora's storage-vs-cache I/O split and per-statement peak memory).
  - *Outage predictors* — `get_pg_wraparound_risk` (XID and MultiXact freeze headroom per database), `get_pg_xmin_horizon` (why vacuum is reclaiming nothing, attributed to the specific holder), `get_pg_replication_slots` (slot health, including whether retained WAL is still growing).
  - *Maintenance* — `get_pg_autovacuum_health` (tables behind on vacuum or analyze, ranked by how far past each table's OWN trigger threshold it is — the ratio, not the dead-tuple count, because the same count is routine on a large table and urgent on a small one).
  - *I/O attribution* — `get_pg_io_stats` (reads, hits, extends and evictions by backend type, object and context). The context dimension is the one with no SQL Server counterpart and the one that changes the remedy: it separates ordinary buffer-pool misses, where more `shared_buffers` or a better index helps, from sequential scans that deliberately bypass the pool through a small ring buffer, where neither will.
  - *Per-database counters* — `get_pg_database_stats` (temp-file spills, cache hit ratio, deadlocks, and the commit/rollback split, from `pg_stat_database`). **Temp files are the reason it exists**: they are work `work_mem` could not hold, which is the most common cause of a PostgreSQL query being slow for a reason its plan shape does not show, and on stock PostgreSQL it is the only temp-file evidence available anywhere. A statistics RESET is reported as a reset — an explicit count and a lower-bound caveat — rather than surfacing as a negative rate or a spike. Since V133 each row also carries `peak_numbackends`, the window's peak of connected backends for that database — a level, never differenced, `null` on a pre-V133 history rather than 0 — deliberately not summed across databases (the peaks land at different instants); the `max_connections` fraction lives on the `PG_CONNECTION_SATURATION` fact, which sums at one instant.
  - *Storage* — `get_pg_table_bloat` (the per-table bloat ESTIMATE, suppressed rather than captioned when its statistics cannot be trusted) and `get_pg_index_usage` (per-index scan counts with the constraint, replica-identity and validity facts that decide whether an unscanned index can be dropped at all).
  - *Sessions and contention* — `get_pg_blocking` (blocking chains that were SAMPLED, with the root blocker attributed and both sides' state captured — PostgreSQL has no engine-side blocked-process recorder, so blocking shorter than the interval leaves no trace anywhere) and `get_pg_session_states` (who is holding a transaction open, and whether they actually pin the xmin horizon). **That second half is a different question from the first**, and the tool exists to keep them apart: an `idle in transaction` session under READ COMMITTED that has only read, or whose write matched no rows, holds neither a snapshot nor a transaction id and starves vacuum of nothing — both measured on a live instance. So the read gates its causal claim on `peak_horizon_age`, where `-1` means the session pinned NOTHING, and tells you so rather than letting a long duration imply otherwise. Pairs with `get_pg_xmin_horizon`, which names the CLASS of holder where this names the session.

  These are separate tools rather than widened SQL Server ones. PostgreSQL's waits are a two-level type/event taxonomy with no signal-wait concept reported in microseconds, and the wraparound / horizon / slot signals have no SQL Server counterpart at all — sharing a result shape would mean lying about a unit or emitting mostly-null columns. The three outage predictors are the ones worth wiring to a pager: each names a condition that stops the server outright, and each is silent until it is nearly too late.

- **Trend data-read tools** — windowed time-series siblings of the core reads, each a stored read of the collected series over the window (BOTH-sides, naive-UTC):
  - `get_memory_trend` (total / target server memory, buffer pool, plan cache over time), `get_perfmon_trend` (a single counter's value + delta, `counter_name` required), `get_file_io_trend` (read/write latency per database and file type, the five with the most I/O stall plus one `(other)` line; `database_name` for one database per file), `get_query_trend` (one query's per-collection history by `query_hash` + `database_name`), `get_query_duration_trend` (overall elapsed-ms/sec + executions/sec).

  Each mirrors the viewer's proven chart read (byte-identical Postgres SQL); the shape follows Lite where the SKUs diverge. `get_perfmon_trend` reproduces Lite's miss vocabulary (Page Life Expectancy is intentionally not collected; an unknown counter hands back the collected names). `get_memory_trend` carries a `total_granted_mb` field for field-for-field parity with Lite, joined per point from the memory-grant series (the same `v_memory_grant_stats` sum the viewer's overlay charts plot) — a point no grants snapshot covers within 30 seconds stays an explicit null, with a `granted_note` naming `get_memory_grants`, never a fabricated 0 (#3529, #3548).

- **System-health parse-on-read tools** — the Dashboard's `get_health_parser_*` family, over Darling's raw `system_health_events`:
  - `get_health_parser_system_health` (corruption + contention counters), `get_health_parser_severe_errors` (severity ≥ 19, with `database_id` resolved to a name), `get_health_parser_scheduler_issues`, `get_health_parser_memory_conditions`, `get_health_parser_memory_broker`, `get_health_parser_memory_node_oom`, `get_health_parser_cpu_tasks`, `get_health_parser_io_issues`.

  Where the Dashboard reads its server-side-parsed `collect.HealthParser_*` tables, these shred the raw extended-event XML **on read** with the shared `SystemHealthParser` (the same parser the viewer's System Events tab uses) and gate with the service-side twin of the viewer's `SystemEventSignificance` — returning the same SIGNIFICANT warning set the Dashboard surfaces (sp_HealthParser at `@warnings_only = 1`). `get_health_parser_system_health` is the one UNGATED category (its counter series plots every snapshot). Each row carries the full sp_HealthParser column set keyed on the event's `event_time`; the tools window on `event_time` (the event's real time), so "last 24 hours" means events that happened in the last 24 hours.

- **Tool reading guides** - `get_tool_guide(tools, topics)` (#3898, both editions). A converted tool's description is split at a `<<GUIDE>>` marker: `tools/list` serves the short head, which keeps every fact needed to read the answer correctly and ends "Reading guide: get_tool_guide.", and this tool returns the long-form guide by tool name, plus named cross-tool topics such as `system_health_empty_windows`. Unknown names are reported, not errors; called with no arguments it lists the tools that have guides and the topics. The `get_health_parser_*` family is converted first.

- **Alert + health-overview tools** — the fleet-triage reads the fleet edition previously lacked, each a stored read over the monitoring store (no live hit):
  - *Alerts* — `get_alert_history` (what fired, value vs threshold, delivery success/failure, muted, and since V131 `route` — where the post went — fleet-wide by default, or scoped to a server; `external_source` names the external alert source an alert came from and is null on every built-in and custom alert), `get_alert_settings` (the current alert config the service is using — per-alert enable/thresholds, cooldown, excluded databases, delivery mode, analysis cadence), `get_mute_rules` (the alert mute rules in force, so a suppressed server is distinguishable from a healthy-quiet one; each rule carries `external_source`, null unless the rule is limited to one external alert source), `get_notification_routes` (the alert-family taxonomy and the [notification routes](#notification-routes--sending-alert-families-to-different-channels) — which channels each route sets, never the destination values).
  - *Health overview* — `get_server_summary` (one-shot per-server CPU / memory / recent blocking / recent deadlocks), `get_daily_summary` (a day's composite health band — Healthy / Warning / Critical — folded through the shared `DailyHealthBandCalculator`, plus the signals behind it).

- **Fleet sweep reports (Darling-only)** — `get_sweep_reports` reads the scheduled [Fleet Sweep Reports](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3466) — the stateful whole-fleet summaries the service persists at the configured cadence (`get_alert_settings`' `fleet_sweep` group; default hourly): the sweep timeline over a caller-set window with each sweep's document embedded, the newest sweep in full (per-server verdicts with band transitions pre-computed, and the would-have-paged ledger whenever a sweep ran under the alert master switch — present-and-empty on a muted sweep with nothing to report, ABSENT on an alerts-on sweep where no such check was made), and the watch-item worklist with its hysteresis position. One `sweep_id` (as a STRING — the ids are too large for a JSON number to round-trip) fetches one sweep in full. The same `FleetSweepPresentation` builders and store reads the web feed's `/api/sweeps` routes serve, so an agent and a browser cannot disagree about what a sweep looks like. The description carries the two field contracts an agent must read: `alerts_enabled: false` on a sweep means DELIVERY WAS OFF while it ran, and `instruments_alive: false` means the sweep could not prove its own data sources — quiet is not clean, and the `instrument_liveness` block says which instrument went unproven. A stored read; no monitored server is touched.

- **Custom Views tools (Darling-only)** — discover, create, and manage the saved dashboards/notebooks a user composes from the curated measure catalog (the same views the web viewer's editor builds), stored in `config.custom_views`. None touches a monitored SQL Server or the collected performance data — the write tools write only view definitions to the monitoring store.
  - *Discover* — `describe_custom_view_catalog` (the compose vocabulary — measures with their source/kind/valid-aggregates/allowed-dimensions/units/per-server-type availability, dimensions, unit families, aggregates, time buckets, filter ops, and viz types). An MCP client calls this FIRST so a composed panel uses only legal identifiers instead of guessing at names; it returns the SAME `/api/catalog` vocabulary the web composer's picker binds to. Read-only static reference — no store, no server.
  - *Read* — `list_custom_views` (summaries: id, name, description, kind, folder, personal, created_by, version, updated_by; an optional `folder` argument lists one folder, and an empty string lists the top level; every personal view is listed, marked `personal: true`), `get_custom_view` (one view's full definition + version).
  - *Author* — `validate_custom_view` (dry-run a definition against the catalog + composer rules, no save), `create_custom_view` (validate then save; takes an optional `folder`), `update_custom_view` (validate then replace in place, optimistic-concurrency on `version`; `folder` omitted keeps the current one), `delete_custom_view`. Over MCP a view is always shared, never personal, because MCP has no person identity, and a personal view cannot be updated or deleted over MCP (it answers `refused`): only its owner can change it, in the web dashboard.
  - *Self-test* — `run_custom_view_panel` (compile + run a single composed panel and return `{sql, rows, annotations}` — the composer's live preview, for checking a generated panel's data before saving).

  The create/update/delete tools are the one view-authoring **write** surface; create/update run the SAME `ValidateDefinition` authority as `validate_custom_view`, so an invalid definition is rejected before it stores; every tool routes through the SAME store + validator + compile-and-run + catalog the web viewer's editor uses (no divergent second implementation). This write surface is part of what the MCP token gates — see [What a token can reach](#opt-in-network-endpoints-lan) below.

- **Alert-tuning write tools (Darling-only)** — `update_alert_settings`, `create_mute_rule`, `update_mute_rule`, `delete_mute_rule`, `purge_expired_mute_rules` (deletes every expired rule, as the Viewer's Purge Expired does, and returns each one it deleted with `undo: null` because an expired rule had no effect), `set_mute_rule_enabled`, and the route writes `create_notification_route` / `update_notification_route` / `set_notification_route_enabled` / `set_notification_route_fallback` / `delete_notification_route`, and `update_notification_channels` (the default channels the Viewer sets in Settings > Notifications: SMTP host, port, SSL, user, password, from and recipients; the Teams, Slack and generic webhook URLs, the generic headers, body template and proxies; the PagerDuty routing key, region, proxy and auto-resolve. Only the fields passed change and `-` clears a text field. Each secret is sealed by the service the way the Viewer seals it and is shown in an answer only as set or not set; the `mcp` role holds no read or write on a secret column and writes them through the store's `config.set_notification_channels` function, which makes the Viewer's refusals on the stored row (a change to the host, port, SSL flag, user or a proxy while a secret tied to it is saved needs the secret again; a saved signing key blocks a generic URL or proxy change). The undo restores only the non-secret settings.) (both route tools set a route's own destinations: Teams, Slack and generic webhook URLs, a PagerDuty routing key and email recipients, sealed for the route's own row and shown in an answer only as saved or none (with the host for Teams and Slack), apart from the recipient list, which is shown in full; `create_notification_route` with no destination makes a route with every channel empty, so each channel goes where it went before (the next matching route, else the channels configured in Settings); `update_notification_route` changes the match, scope, enabled flag, resolution override, fallback and destinations of any route that is not an agent route, and the match or scope only on a route with no explicit agent setting (a route with an agent setting of Off keeps its match and scope in the Viewer, and the store function refuses the change for any login); both refuse an agent route, and the answer of an edit gives the previous values and the undo call, which for a destination says to pass the earlier value again. Each write is logged with the tool, the identity the service knows for the token (its first 8 SHA-256 hex characters, never the token), the client's own name labelled `client says` (any client can send any name) and the names of the fields changed, never a value), plus `dismiss_alert_history` / `restore_alert_history` (the flag the web dashboard's Dismiss sets, and the call that undoes it; nothing is deleted). None of these is callable by an agent Darling sends to investigate an alert let an MCP client TUNE the alert engine the fleet shares (`create_mute_rule` / `update_mute_rule` also take an optional lower-case `external_source` that limits a rule to the alerts one external source raised; a name that is not a valid source name is refused, and `update_mute_rule` clears it with an explicit null) — the SAME config `get_alert_settings` / `get_mute_rules` read and the Viewer's Settings window writes. `update_alert_settings` is a PARTIAL update of the alert configuration: read via `get_alert_settings`, change fields, and send only those back in the same nested shape; every field is validated against the SAME ranges/enums the Settings window enforces BEFORE any write, an out-of-range or unknown field returns `{status:"invalid"}` and writes nothing, and the write self-bumps `config_version` so the running service hot-reloads within one collection sweep. `create_mute_rule` / `update_mute_rule` / `delete_mute_rule` / `set_mute_rule_enabled` reuse the SAME `PgMuteRuleStore` `get_mute_rules` reads through (and the same GUID id-generation the Viewer's mute-create path uses). `set_mute_rule_enabled` is the headless twin of the Viewer's mute-rule checkbox: it writes `enabled` and nothing else, so a rule can leave force and return keeping its id, its reason and the `created_at_utc` the `Stale Mute Rules` self-alert ages it from — where a delete-and-re-create resets that clock and delivers the suppressed alerts in between. `update_mute_rule` is the headless twin of the Viewer's Edit dialog on the same terms: a PARTIAL update (send only the fields to change, in the shape `get_mute_rules` returns; an explicit JSON `null` clears a field, so a bounded rule can be made permanent again) written through the store's own full-row `UpdateAsync` — whose SET list does not name `created_at_utc`, so no edit, however sweeping, resets the staleness clock the way the delete/recreate workaround did for every field the enable flag cannot express. `update_alert_settings` spans the two singleton config rows the alert engine reads: every knob is on `config_alert_settings` except the per-fingerprint DELIVERY cooldown, which is stored as `config_notification.email_cooldown_minutes` and reported and accepted as `delivery.cooldown_minutes` — a channel-neutral name, because that one number is the only throttle on a Slack / Teams / PagerDuty / generic-webhook post as well as on email, so a headless deployment with no SMTP configured at all is still governed by it (the stored spelling is accepted as a write-only alias). Note the two cooldowns are different stages: top-level `cooldown_minutes` gates whether the engine FIRES, `delivery.cooldown_minutes` bounds the resulting post — twice over, once per alert FINGERPRINT and once per METRIC across the whole fleet for a RE-notification, so one fault on forty servers does not cost forty posts an hour and the servers that second bound holds back are named on the post that does go out. A first notice is never held back by either bound, and `delivery.mode` `PerEvent` opts out of the per-metric one. None touches a monitored SQL Server or the collected data — only the shared alert configuration; the one tool that sets delivery credentials is `update_notification_channels`, which sets the SMTP password, the webhook URLs and the keys sealed and never shows them back (the `mcp` role itself can read and write exactly the one non-secret cooldown column of the notification table and cannot read or write any secret column; the sealed values reach the store through the `config.set_notification_channels` function). It is part of what the MCP token gates — see [What a token can reach](#opt-in-network-endpoints-lan) below.

- **Server-onboarding write tools (Darling-only)** — `add_servers` (BULK) and `remove_server` (which drops this install's own Extended Events sessions on the server and leaves the shared ones: `PerformanceMonitor_Deadlock`, `PerformanceMonitor_BlockedProcess`, `PerformanceMonitor_Attention` and the old `PerformanceMonitor_LongQueryCompletions`; once the server is removed, [`--drop-xe-sessions --print-sql`](#drop-the-extended-events-sessions-a-removed-server-left-behind---drop-xe-sessions) prints the statements that drop what is left, because the named form `--drop-xe-sessions <server>` works only before the removal) let an MCP client stand up or tear down FLEET monitoring conversationally ("monitor these twenty servers with this login"), the service-side twin of the Viewer's Add / Manage Servers dialogs. `edit_server` changes one saved server in place and is served under the same `mcp.allowServerAdmin` setting as `add_servers` and `remove_server`; an edit that points Windows authentication at a connection whose server identity is not verified is refused as `add_servers` refuses it, before any connection is made. `add_servers` takes a JSON **array** of server objects (`host` required; optional `display_name` / `database` / `read_only_intent` / `multi_subnet_failover`; `auth` `Windows`/`SQL` with `username`+`password` for SQL; and the exposed TLS options `encrypt_mode` `Optional`/`Mandatory`/`Strict` + `trust_server_certificate`) and processes them **in order**: it validates each entry, PROBES the connection in-process (reusing the same `DarlingServerConnector.ProbeAsync` the `--test-connection` verb runs — the service holds the network path + credentials, so no `test_connect` command plane is needed), skips a case-folded duplicate (`duplicate`) of an already-monitored server or an earlier entry, DPAPI-encrypts the SQL password (the service identity, so it round-trips at collection time), and INSERTs the row mirroring the service's own seed shape. A server that fails to connect is `connection_failed` and the batch continues. `auth` also takes the two non-interactive Microsoft Entra modes (#3484). With `ServicePrincipal`, `username` is the app's client id and `password` is its client secret. The other mode is `ManagedIdentity`. Only the interactive Entra modes (MFA, device code, default credential) are `invalid`, because they need a signed-in user and cannot run headless. Off Windows, a literal SQL password or client secret is refused as `invalid` too, because encryption at rest is Windows DPAPI. Give an `env:NAME` or `file:/run/secrets/<name>` reference instead, the same two forms that `darling.json` takes for every secret. A server is refused as `collides` when its probed connection lands in a database that another monitored server already covers. That happens when it names one database but connects to a different one, such as a wrong Initial Catalog. Adding it gives one database two identities, so its history is stored twice and its alerts fire twice. A server whose id is already held by a different server is refused as `collides` as well, because two identities can hash to one id. Nothing is written for a `collides` entry. If saving an entry fails, that entry and every entry after it are `not_saved` and are not probed. The entries before it stay `added`. `remove_server` DELETEs a monitored server by name (resolved the same way every `server_name` is) — already-collected history is kept. Both write only the monitoring store's `config.config_monitored_servers` registry; neither runs anything on a monitored server beyond the one-time probe. **The SQL password travels to the endpoint inside `add_servers`' request** and is DPAPI-encrypted at rest (never returned) — it is part of what the MCP token gates, and it puts a credential on the wire; see [What a token can reach](#opt-in-network-endpoints-lan) below.

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `enabled` | `false` | **Off by default** — a headless service does not open a local port unless you ask |
| `port` | `5152` | Chosen so all three editions coexist on one machine (Dashboard 5150, Lite 5151) |
| `allowTokenlessLoopback` | `false` | On, a loopback-only MCP server with no token serves local clients without one, with the write and investigation tools off, and every start logs a line saying so. Off, an enabled MCP server with no token does not start. A configured token gates every request either way. |
| `allowWrites` | `false` | Serves the MCP tools that change the monitoring store's configuration, such as alert settings and notification routes, mute rules, custom alert rules and Custom Views. Served only with a bearer token that is at least 16 characters with at least 6 different ones, and every request must then present it, loopback included. File-only: edit `darling.json` and restart. See [MCP server](../docs/mcp-server.md#setup). |
| `allowServerAdmin` | `false` | Also serves `add_servers`, `edit_server`, `remove_server` and `set_server_action`, which change which servers the service connects to, or where, as its own account. No effect while `allowWrites` is off. File-only and restart-only. |
| `allowInvestigations` | `false` | Serves `claim_investigation_request` and `submit_investigation_report` to your own agent (#5632). They write investigation state. The same setting serves the 13 Level 2 agent action tools: each call needs an `agent_action` argument and a live claim, and `agent_actions_enabled` (default on). The 9 store actions need a Level 2 route. The 4 server actions (`kill_session`, `force_query_store_plan`, `unforce_query_store_plan` and `evict_cached_plan`) need a Level 3 request, the server's own action switch (default off) and a remediation login reference. Served only with a bearer token (`network.encryptedToken` or `network.token`) and while `allowWrites` is off: one token cannot tell an agent from a person, so with both on they stay off and the start logs one Critical line. The reads `list_investigation_requests` and `get_investigation_reports` need no setting. |
| `allowWindowsAuthOverUnverifiedTls` | `false` | `add_servers` defaults to Windows authentication, where the service presents its own computer account to the host the caller names. Off, `add_servers` refuses a Windows-authentication entry that also sets `trust_server_certificate` or an `encrypt_mode` other than `Mandatory` or `Strict`, because the service could not confirm which server it reached. On, it accepts them. SQL authentication and the Entra modes are not affected. The `--add-server` command and the web dashboard's server edits are not gated. `edit_server` is gated like `add_servers`: an edit that points Windows authentication at an unverified connection is refused. No effect unless `allowServerAdmin` is on. File-only and restart-only. |
| `tokens` | none | Named bearer tokens with a role (#6542): a list of `{ "name", "encryptedToken" or "token", "role" }`, where `role` is `operator` or `agent`. One service then serves an operator and its investigation agents at once. An `operator` token sees the reads and the write tools and never the claim or submit tools. It sees the four server admin tools (`add_servers`, `edit_server`, `remove_server` and `set_server_action`) only when `mcp.allowWrites` and `mcp.allowServerAdmin` are both on, the same rule the legacy token follows. An `agent` token sees the reads, the investigation tools and the Level 2 action tools, never a never-agent tool, and its `agent_id` must be the token's name. Each name and each value is used once, and every request, loopback included, must present a token. The legacy `network` token keeps working as before. Read once at start. See [Named tokens](../docs/darling-agent-dispatch.md). |
| `network` | none | The token and the opt-in network exposure of the MCP server: `listen`, `allowFrom`, `encryptedToken` or `token`, and `tls`. With no `network` block, the server listens on loopback only. The token gates the loopback listener too. `listen` and `allowFrom` work in managed mode or the compose container, and are ignored with your own PostgreSQL outside the container. File-only. See [MCP endpoint](#mcp-endpoint-assistant-over-the-lan). |
| `dataDetail` | `full` | How much collected text every MCP tool answer carries. `full` changes nothing. `maskLiterals` keeps query text but replaces its literal values (numbers, strings, parameter values) with `?`. `metadataOnly` holds back query text, plan XML and input buffers, and masks login and host names (see below); object and database names, counts, durations, waits and times stay. An unknown value is read as `metadataOnly`. A tool's own status sentence (the `message` beside `status`, for example the step a `precondition` asks for) stays readable at both levels, with its literal values replaced; under `metadataOnly` a login or client address it quotes from the monitored server's own error text is replaced by the same pseudonym the rest of the answer uses. An investigation brief is composed at the same level (#5632). **A login or host keeps one pseudonym (#6066):** under `metadataOnly` a name is shown as `login#` or `host#` plus 8 hex characters of a keyed hash (`login#3fa9c2d1`), the same in every answer, brief, journal read and webhook from one store, across calls, sessions and service restarts. Case is ignored (`CORP\alice` and `corp\alice` read the same) and a login and a host with the same text differ. If two different names in one answer hash alike, the second gets `-2`. The key is 32 random bytes made by migration V198 in `config.pseudonym_key`; no role but the store owner can read it, the service reads it once and keeps it in memory, and no tool, log line, journal row, brief, webhook or export carries it. A copied or restored store keeps its key, so its pseudonyms stay the same; the key is never rotated. If the service cannot read the key, an answer numbers names per answer (`login#1`) and still hides every name. Briefs claimed before this release keep their per-brief numbers. On a bring-your-own store with no `postgres.mcpConnectionString`, the MCP connection falls back to the store owner and can read the key table, the same exposure `password_key` has there; no MCP tool runs caller-written SQL over that connection (every tool builds its statements from fixed text and bound parameters) |

**A token is required on loopback too.** Once `mcp.enabled` is on, the MCP server starts only with a bearer token. Set `mcp.network.encryptedToken` (from `--encrypt-password`) or `mcp.network.token` (the token itself, `env:NAME` or `file:PATH`), and leave `mcp.network.listen` out to stay loopback-only. On Windows, use `encryptedToken`. `env:NAME` and `file:PATH` references are for the compose and Linux setups, where there is no DPAPI. To make a token, use [the PowerShell one-liner under MCP endpoint](#mcp-endpoint-assistant-over-the-lan). With no token the server does not start, and the service log names these settings. `--print-mcp-token`, run elevated on the service host, prints the configured token. To serve local clients with no token, as earlier builds did, set `mcp.allowTokenlessLoopback` to `true`. The write and investigation tools then stay off, and every start logs a line saying so.

Register with Claude Code, sending the token as a header:

```
claude mcp add --transport http --scope user sql-monitor-darling http://localhost:5152/ --header "Authorization: Bearer <token>"
```

In a project's `.mcp.json`, read the token from an environment variable, because project files get committed:

```json
{ "mcpServers": { "sql-monitor-darling": { "type": "http", "url": "http://localhost:5152/", "headers": { "Authorization": "Bearer ${DARLING_MCP_TOKEN}" } } } }
```

Set `DARLING_MCP_TOKEN` in the environment that starts Claude Code, or keep the token in the user-scope entry above.

If `mcp.network.tls` is set and `mcp.network.listen` is `0.0.0.0` or `::`, the one MCP listener serves HTTPS to everyone, loopback included. Local clients then need `https://`, and the certificate must name the host they use. With a specific listen IP the loopback listeners stay plain HTTP, and the command above still works.

If the port is already in use at startup, the MCP server logs an error and does not start; collection is unaffected.

### web

The embedded **web dashboard** — a browser view of the monitoring store, served over HTTP on its OWN port (default **5153**), separate from the MCP server. It is a distinct surface from [`### mcp`](#mcp): its own enable flag, port, token, and exposure block, because the two gate different blast radii (the MCP token guards `analyze_server`'s **live outbound** connections to your monitored SQL Servers; the web dashboard reads the collected store and, for a sign-in that can make changes, queues commands and edits settings in that store, as below, but never runs SQL an operator wrote against a monitored server). It connects to the store as the least-privilege `viewer` role on a managed store and on the compose distribution's own store; on any other bring-your-own store it connects as `postgres.webConnectionString`, or as the owner when that is unset (see [What a web token can reach](#web-endpoint-browser-over-the-lan)). Loopback-only by default; see [Opt-in Network Endpoints (LAN)](#opt-in-network-endpoints-lan) to reach it from the LAN.

**Quiet window (#6231).** `set_quiet_window` (server or the whole fleet, an end, an optional start, a reason) and `clear_quiet_window` give planned work a time-boxed mute. A quiet window is a mute rule with only a server scope (or none) and an end; the new optional start (`starts_at_utc`, store rung V208) lets it be set ahead of the work, and until the start passes `get_mute_rules` lists it with `state: upcoming` and it mutes nothing. Both tools are write tools (served only with `mcp.allowWrites` and a token) and are never offered to an investigating agent. `create_mute_rule` and `update_mute_rule` take the same start, the Mute Rules page and the Viewer's mute dialog show and set it, and an end that is not after the start is refused. A window lasts at most 7 days and starts at most 7 days from now. A server, or the fleet, can have one quiet window upcoming or active at a time: a second `set_quiet_window` for the same scope answers `refused` and names the first, which `clear_quiet_window` removes. Each answer gives the rule id and the call that undoes it.

**Database state expectations (#6397).** `get_database_state_expectations` lists a server's databases with the state each is in now, the state the Database State alert expects (`expected_state`, `is_user_override`) and a status (`as_expected`, `deviating`, `ignored` or `pending`). `set_database_state_expected` sets one database's expected state, `IGNORE` (never alert for it) or `CLEAR` (remove the row; the service learns a baseline again from the next observation). The SQL is one copy, `DatabaseStateExpectedStore`, that the Viewer's override editor runs too. The write is served only with `mcp.allowWrites` and a token, is never offered to an investigating agent (an agent working an alert could otherwise silence it), refuses a database that is not in the latest collected snapshot and a state outside the allowed list, and answers with `previous` and the `undo` call. The `mcp` role gets INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on `config.database_state_expected`; its read already ran on the SELECT it holds on all of `config`. On a bring-your-own store, re-run `tools/provision-roles.sql` to add the grant.
