**Investigation admin over MCP (#6400).** With `mcp.allowWrites` and a bearer token, `write_investigation_note` (`note_text`, optional `server_name`, `metric_name`, `days`; approved as written), `approve_investigation_note` and `reject_investigation_note` (`note_id` of a pending agent note) and `close_investigation_request` (`request_id`, `reason`) do what the web's Write a note, Approve, Reject and Close do, through the same store code, with the same checks and refusals. `start_practice_request` (`request_id` of a judged case), `save_environment_profile` (`profile_text`, optional `server_name` or `tag_id`, optional `expected_modified_at`; a blank text removes the profile) and `record_investigation_feedback` (`request_id`, `verdict`, optional `corrected_verdict`, `missed_step`, `comment`; recorded under the token's name) do what the web's Start practice, profile Save and feedback form do the same way. Each answer shows the row before and after. The change is recorded as `person:mcp` and the token's name (`named token NAME`), or `legacy token` for the endpoint's one legacy token, and the service log line carries the client's own name. The first four have no undo, because the web has no way to reopen a request, return a decided note to pending or delete a note; a practice request is closed with `close_investigation_request`, a replaced feedback record is restored by recording its 'before' again, and a profile edit is restored by saving the answer's 'before' profile text again. An investigation agent is never offered these tools: it proposes a note through `submit_investigation_report`, and a person decides it.

**Run collectors now (#5941).** On a server's **Collection Health** tab, a seat that can edit sees a **Run all collectors now** button and a **Run now** button on each collector row. Run all runs every enabled collector for that server once, now, and leaves disabled collectors out. Run now runs just that collector once, even if it is disabled; its schedule and enabled state do not change. A collector that does not apply to the server's engine is skipped and the result says so. Use it to check collection right after installing an extension or changing a setting. Guards: a second request for the same server is refused while one is queued or running, a request within 60 seconds of the last one for that server is refused (the message says when the next is allowed), and the run takes a slot from the same limit as scheduled collection, so clicks on several servers cannot run more at once than the schedule does. A read-only seat sees no buttons and gets 403. The same action is the MCP tool `run_collectors_now` (served with `mcp.allowWrites`). Run all puts extra load on the monitored server, so prefer one collector when one is enough.

**Generate now and Ask AI on the web (#6389).** On a server's **Recommendations** tab, a seat that can edit sees a **Generate now** button. It queues the same `analyze_now` command the Viewer's Generate now queues (no arguments, one server), through `config.request_analyze_now`, because the web's `viewer` login holds no INSERT on the command queue. The page shows progress, then re-reads the findings when the service finishes. Guards: a second request for the same server is refused while one is queued or running, and one within 15 seconds of the last is refused, and a web click and an MCP `analyze_now` for the same server share one guard. A read-only seat sees no button and gets 403. A store you brought yourself needs `Darling/tools/provision-roles.sql` run again to add the function; until then the tab says so and points to the Viewer. Every finding card also has **Ask AI**, which copies the same investigation prompt the Viewer's Ask AI copies, built by one shared builder with the finding's window on the server's own clock. It only builds text for your own MCP client: the web holds no model key and makes no model call.

**Incident actions over MCP (#6230).** With `mcp.allowWrites` and a bearer token, `test_connection` (one registered server, by name), `analyze_now` (one server) and `purge_now` (whole store, optional `retention_days` 1 to 3650; the first call queues nothing and shows each collector's retention and the oldest time kept with a `confirm_value` the first call issues, and only a second call that passes it back queues the purge; the value works once and expires after 5 minutes) queue the same command the Viewer and the CLI queue, through `config.request_mcp_command`, the only way the `mcp` login writes a command row (it names `analyze_now`, `purge_now`, `test_connect` and `reload`, builds a connection test from the stored server row, and refuses any other type). Each answer gives a `command_id`; `get_command_result` shows the result once the service has run it on its next poll. A purge cannot be undone. `get_agent_changes` lists agent changes with their `called_id` and `can_undo`, and `undo_plan_change` undoes a plan force or unforce: it shows the statement first and writes only on `confirm: true` with that statement, and it needs that server's `force_plan` switch and remediation login, the same gate as the force tools. The three queued tools are never offered to an agent sent to investigate an alert. `set_agent_actions_enabled` (argument `enabled`) turns the service-wide agent switch off (`enabled: true` is refused: a person turns it on in the Viewer or the web dashboard), and `undo_agent_change` (argument `called_id`) undoes a mute rule, custom alert rule, threshold override or deadlock signature merge change, both by the code behind the Viewer and the web routes (`PUT /api/agent-changes/switch` and `PUT /api/agent-changes/{id}/undo`), so the refusals are the same (`already_undone`, `not_found`, `expired`, `not_undoable`, `changed_since`) and a plan change is pointed to `undo_plan_change`. Each answer gives the state before and after, and the journal names the caller as `mcp:` plus the token and client. Neither is ever offered to an agent sent to investigate an alert.

**Collection state over MCP (#6230).** With `mcp.allowWrites` on, an MCP client can `pause_collection` and `resume_collection` (all collection), `disable_server` and `enable_server` (one server), `set_server_excluded_databases` (one server's excluded databases, the same write the Viewer's Excluded Databases editor makes) and `reload_config`. `get_collection_state` reads whether collection is paused and each server's enabled flag, excluded databases and monthly cost, and `get_server_settings` (#6402) reads the fields `edit_server` changes for one server (never a password or external ID). Pause and resume queue the same `pause` and `resume` commands the Viewer queues, through `config.request_mcp_command`: the answer gives the command id and the read that shows the result (`get_command_result`, then `get_collection_state`), and the row is stamped `mcp`. The other writes run the command plane's own plan, so the SQL and the checks exist once; each answer gives the previous value and the exact call that undoes it, and each server write logs at Warning with the tool, the server and the caller. A server is found the way `edit_server` and `remove_server` find one (the storage name `list_servers` shows works); a name that matches several servers is answered with the candidates. None of the six is offered to an investigation agent. The `mcp` role holds no write on `config.config_service.paused`: a store that an earlier build granted it loses the grant on the next start, and a self-managed store re-runs `tools/provision-roles.sql`. `edit_server` still refuses `is_enabled` and `excluded_databases`, and its refusal names these tools.

**Known patterns over MCP (#6398).** With `mcp.allowWrites` on (and a token), `create_known_pattern`, `update_known_pattern` and `delete_known_pattern` add, replace and remove the entries `get_known_patterns` lists, the same writes the web editor makes (`note` is required and at most 500 characters; `server_id`, `metric_name`, `time_of_day`, `time_zone`, `window_minutes` and `expires_at_utc` are optional, and a field left out means any / UTC / the default window / never). They call the same store functions as the web routes and refuse the same things (a missing or over-long note, an unknown time zone, a `server_id` that is not registered, a bad time or expiry), and write nothing when they refuse. `update_known_pattern` replaces the whole entry, as the web editor's save does. Each answer carries the entry before and after and an `undo` call (a delete's undo re-creates the entry under a new id). The `mcp` role holds EXECUTE on the three functions and nothing on the table. These tools are never offered to an agent that Darling sends to investigate an alert; it may read with `get_known_patterns`.

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `enabled` | `false` | **Off by default** — a headless service does not open a local port unless you ask |
| `port` | `5153` | Chosen so all four local surfaces coexist on one machine (Dashboard 5150, Lite 5151, Darling MCP 5152) |
| `allowTokenlessLoopback` | `false` | On, a loopback-only dashboard with no token serves local browsers without one, for reads only, and every start logs a line saying so. A change (adding a server, editing a view, muting an alert) is refused with 403 and a message that names the token setting. Off, an enabled dashboard with no token does not start. A configured token gates every request either way. |
| `allowAdminWrites` | `true` | Managed store only. On, a person signed in to the dashboard as an admin (the shared token, or an OIDC admin) can make its Admin writes (routes and environment profiles, threshold overrides, report feedback, server actions, note decisions, undoing an agent change and the agent switch, closing a request, writing a note, practice requests, and case export) through a second small connection pool. That pool signs in as the managed `admin` login, the one the Viewer uses. Every read stays on the read-only `viewer` login. Off keeps the dashboard list-only on the managed store. Every start logs which of the two it is. A store you bring yourself is unchanged. File-only; restart to change |

**What the dashboard can change.** Besides reading the store, a sign-in that can edit adds, edits and removes monitored servers. It also edits alert routes, mute rules and custom alert rules, and runs the commands and changes the settings described above.

**A token is required on loopback too.** Once `web.enabled` is on, the dashboard starts only with a token. Set `web.network.encryptedToken` (from `--encrypt-password`) or `web.network.token` (the token itself, `env:NAME` or `file:PATH`), and leave `web.network.listen` out to stay loopback-only. On Windows, use `encryptedToken`. `env:NAME` and `file:PATH` references are for the compose and Linux setups, where there is no DPAPI. To make a token, use [the PowerShell one-liner under MCP endpoint](#mcp-endpoint-assistant-over-the-lan). The browser then shows a sign-in page: paste the token there once, and it is exchanged for a session cookie. With no token the dashboard does not start, and the service log names these settings. `--print-web-token`, run elevated on the service host, prints the configured token. To serve local browsers with no token, set `web.allowTokenlessLoopback` to `true`. The dashboard then serves reads only, and every start logs a line saying so. A token shorter than the floor below (16 characters, 6 of them different) still signs a browser in, but the dashboard serves reads only until a longer one is set.

Once enabled, open `http://localhost:5153/` in a browser on the service host. Like the MCP server, `enabled`/`port` here are the file SEED; after first start they live in the control plane and the Viewer's Settings toggles them LIVE (the service starts/stops/rebinds the dashboard within seconds — no restart). If the port is already in use at startup, the web host logs an error and retries on a calm cadence; collection is unaffected.

**What you see.** The dashboard opens on a **Fleet Overview**: a card per enabled server with a status dot, six per-metric health bands (CPU, threads, memory, blocking, deadlocks, collectors), and its last collection time — all banded server-side, so the browser only renders (a server the service has just registered and not yet collected from shows an amber "Awaiting first collection", never a red offline; one that stopped reporting reads Offline however long ago it stopped). Above the cards a worst-first "Needs attention" list surfaces the servers to look at, or an all-healthy line when there is nothing to chase. Click a card to **drill into one server**: an overview, wait stats with a trend for the heaviest wait, active queries, a CPU chart, memory and file-I/O trends, and collection health — the same collected data the viewer shows, over inline charts. A fleet-wide **Alert History** page (with a server filter box) rounds out phase 1, a **Custom Views** page hosts the composer for the saved dashboards/notebooks in `config.custom_views`, and an **Alert Rules** page edits the user-authored custom alert rules (evaluated on a managed or compose store; on any other bring-your-own store they can be authored but do not fire, since its `viewer` role comes from `tools/provision-roles.sql`, which does not create the resolve function the evaluator needs, [#3970](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3970)). A **Fleet Sweeps** page ([#3466](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3466)) renders the scheduled whole-fleet sweep reports as a timeline at a configurable viewing span (default the last hour): each sweep's document diffs against the previous sweep (band transitions with reasons, arrivals and departures), carries the watch-item worklist with its hysteresis position, states the alert master switch on every sweep and renders the would-have-paged ledger whenever one was taken under mute — and a sweep that could not prove its instruments were alive renders LOUDLY (a red banner and a marked timeline row), because quiet is not clean. The sweep cadence shown there is read from `get_alert_settings`' `fleet_sweep` group; the Settings window and `update_alert_settings` own the knob. Channel delivery of sweep content is bounded at a **daily rollup**, a ceiling rather than a default, per the issue's delivery ruling: at most one INFO post per day through the existing alert channels, summarizing the trailing day's band transitions, watch-item openings and closings, instrument-liveness incidents, and — whenever any covered sweep ran under the alert master switch — the would-have-paged ledger those sweeps carried, so the operator sees what the silence cost. A day with nothing to say posts nothing (the collector-cost digest's own discipline — the web feed is where the affirmative, instrument-proved all-clear lives at full cadence), and the rollup's delivery is itself master-gated: with `alerts_enabled: false` the sweeps keep publishing here and to `get_sweep_reports`, nothing reaches the channel, and the first rollup after re-enable states how many of its covered sweeps ran muted — older muted days stay readable on this page permanently rather than arriving as a re-enable flood. The HTTP API also carries the mute-rule write endpoints (`/api/mute-rules`, [#3450](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3450)) — their page UI is deferred; the endpoints are the deliverable — all writes gated on the editing seat. Otherwise it is a read-only view — no settings, no live-server queries — and it refreshes every 60 seconds (pausing while the tab is hidden). The frontend ships fully self-contained (no CDN, no fonts, no remote anything), so it works on an air-gapped host with no internet access.

### peers

**Declared peer stores** — optional, and only relevant when the fleet is split across **several Darling boxes**, one store each (SQL Server primaries on one box, their readable replicas on another, PostgreSQL on a third). Each box's MCP server answers over **its own** store only, so a server monitored by a sibling resolves as not-found — which an agent cannot tell apart from *"nobody monitors this server."* Declaring the siblings fixes that at the three places an agent forms its picture of the fleet.

**Disclosure only.** There is no address and no credential in this block, and nothing behind it: the service never contacts a peer, cannot read a peer's data, and cannot tell whether a peer is even running. A peer is a **name** plus a **sentence**, so an agent (or its human) can pick the right endpoint. Everything here is sent verbatim to every connected MCP client, so the service **refuses to start** if any peer text looks like a connection string or credential.

| Key | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `storeName` | `""` | **Opt-in.** A short label for THIS store itself; when set, every store self-alert fires under it instead of `Monitor Store` — see below, setting it re-keys the self-alert fingerprints |
| `thisStoreCovers` | `""` | One sentence naming what THIS store monitors — the anchor the peer list is relative to |
| `stores[].name` | — | **Required.** Whatever an operator would recognize (the box name, "the use1 store") |
| `stores[].covers` | `""` | A short sentence naming what that store monitors. Human prose — never parsed, only shown |
| `stores[].matches` | `[]` | Optional server-name **substrings** that store monitors, case-insensitive. The only machine-checked field |

```jsonc
"peers": {
  "storeName": "prod-sql-use1-monitor-01",
  "thisStoreCovers": "the 42 us-east-1 SQL Server primaries",
  "stores": [
    {
      "name": "prod-sql-use2-monitor-01",
      "covers": "the readable replicas of those same 42 primaries, in-region from us-east-2",
      "matches": ["use2"]
    },
    { "name": "prod-sql-pg-monitor-01", "covers": "the Aurora PostgreSQL clusters", "matches": ["-aurora-"] }
  ]
}
```

What it changes, with peers declared:

- **The MCP instructions** gain a Fleet Coverage section, high enough that an agent reads which store it is talking to before it reads the tool census.
- **`list_servers`** gains `this_store_covers`, a `peer_fleets` array, and a `peer_note`. Both are always present: an *empty* `peer_fleets` has two very different meanings (this really is the only store, or nobody declared the siblings) and the service cannot tell them apart, so `peer_note` says exactly that rather than letting an empty array read as "this is the whole fleet." An **empty registry** answers in prose rather than JSON, and carries the peer list too — a store with nothing registered is a fresh or just-restarted box, which is the worst place to drop the disclosure.
- **The server-resolution miss** appends the disclosure to the existing "Could not resolve server. Available servers:" listing, naming the peer whose declared coverage matches — so *not monitored here* stops looking like *not monitored anywhere*.

**`storeName` names the store on its own self-alerts.** Every fleet-level self-alert — `Store Disk Pressure`, `Retention Held`, the `Collector Cost Digest`, the `Fleet Sweep Rollup`, all twelve families and their resolution edges — fires under the constant `Monitor Store`, which is unambiguous with exactly one store and collides with exactly two: on a shared channel the alert cannot say which host it is about, and the delivery fingerprint downstream automation dedups on is identical on both stores, so the second store's incident lands on the first store's ticket as a recurrence rather than opening its own. Set `storeName` on each store and its self-alerts carry that label instead — into the cards, the alert history, the mute-rule matching and the fingerprint, the same way a monitored server's display name travels. **Setting it is accepting a re-key**: the label is part of the fingerprint, so open work items keyed on the old one are orphaned and fresh ones start beside them. That is why it is opt-in with the constant as the default (unset is byte-identical to every earlier release), and why there is deliberately no machine-hostname fallback — a fallback would perform that re-key on every existing install at upgrade, silently. Two couplings to plan for: **mute rules match the alert row's spelling**, so re-scope any self-alert mute from `Monitor Store` to the new label; and **do not reuse a monitored server's display name** — the two would be indistinguishable on every shared surface including the triage page, and nothing polices it, because the registry is store-authoritative after seeding and config validation cannot see it.

`matches` is deliberately plain substrings, no globbing and no regex: it exists to answer "which region/role prefix is this name?", and a pattern language would be a config surface with its own failure modes. Blank entries are dropped — an empty substring matches every name, which would make one peer claim the whole fleet. A peer with no `matches` is still disclosed everywhere; it just cannot be singled out on a miss, and the miss message says so instead of implying the server is unmonitored.

A **file-only** block (not seeded into the control plane): it describes the deployment topology of *this* box, which must not be editable from a peer's Viewer. An edit takes effect on the next service restart. There is deliberately **no cross-store connectivity** here — actual federated reads (auth between stores, latency, partial failures) are a much larger surface, and may never be worth building if disclosure alone makes the split legible.

**Declaring nothing changes nothing, with one exception worth knowing about on upgrade.** The instructions, the resolution-miss message, and `list_servers`' empty-registry sentence are byte-for-byte what they were. But `list_servers`' JSON envelope carries `this_store_covers`, `peer_fleets` and `peer_note` on *every* response, declared or not — so a script comparing that tool's exact shape sees three new keys even if you never write a `peers` block. That is deliberate: an empty `peer_fleets` means *either* "this is the only store" *or* "nobody declared the siblings", and a note that only appeared when peers were declared would say nothing in precisely the case that produces the wrong conclusion.

**A `peers` block that fails validation is refused whole, and nothing is disclosed** — not the valid subset. An unfinished block that asserts coverage which may be wrong is worse than no block, and the service logs each problem at Critical. The check runs inside the publish rather than only in config validation, because the MCP host loads its own config and deliberately never validates it (its fail-closed checks are host-local), so validation alone would leave the one path that actually broadcasts uncovered.

### heartbeat

An optional outbound ping to a dead-man's-switch URL, off unless `url` is set. File-only: the web Admin and MCP cannot edit it, and no page returns it. See [Watching the service from outside](#watching-the-service-from-outside).

### reads (optional)

`reads.maxHeavyReads` (default 2) is how many long reads the MCP server and the web dashboard run at once. A read is long when the 90th percentile of its tool or page is above 2 seconds at that window size over the last 7 days (with no history yet, a 7-day or longer window counts as long). Up to 16 more long reads wait, oldest first, for up to 45 seconds; past that a read is refused: `busy` on MCP and HTTP 429 with `Retry-After` on the web. Short reads, the health reads (`get_read_latency`, `get_slow_reads`, `get_collection_health`, `get_collection_log`, `get_store_metrics`, `get_store_log`, `list_servers`) and MCP write tools never wait. `0` turns the limit off. The limit is per service process: the WPF viewer reads the store directly and is not counted. The value is read when each server starts.

An identical long read is answered from memory for a short time: 15 seconds for a read with no window or a window up to 1 hour, 30 seconds up to 24 hours, 60 seconds up to 7 days and 120 seconds beyond. A read answered from memory takes no slot. The cache is per service process too.

`get_read_latency` (and its grid on the web) counts these per route and window size. `count` and the percentiles cover only the reads that ran. `queued` is the reads that waited for a slot, `queue_wait_ms` their summed wait and `queue_wait_p95_ms` the 95th percentile of that wait. `rejected` is the reads answered `busy` or 429, `cache_hits` the reads answered from memory and `cancelled` the reads whose caller left. `wait_total_ms` stays the wait for a store connection only. The `admission` object is the live state of this process since it started: the limit, the long reads running and waiting now, and per route the reads admitted, queued, refused, answered from memory and missed by the cache. It is not stored, so it starts again at zero after a restart.

### No Schedule Knobs, by Design

There are deliberately **no collection-schedule settings and no per-collector retention settings** in `darling.json`. The service consumes the shared per-collector defaults (`CollectorScheduleDefaults`) — the same cadences and retention horizons a fresh Lite install uses, identity-pinned by tests so the two editions cannot drift. If a schedule knob is ever genuinely needed, it will be added then, not speculatively.

One retention setting does exist in the file: `planContentRetentionDays`. It sets how many days a stored plan outlives its last sighting before it can be removed. The default is `21`, `0` turns it off, and any other value is held to 7 through 365. It is a first-run seed (see [Where each setting lives](#where-each-setting-lives-first-run-seed-or-file-only)), so after the first start you change it in the store. The plan's query statistics keep their normal retention. A plan older than this limit shows as a missing plan.

The overrides that do exist live in the **store**, not the file. `config.config_collector_schedules` is a sparse table where an absent row means "the code default". The Viewer's Collector Schedules window is where cadence, retention, database scope, the enabled flag and the run time are edited, per fleet or per server. The two columns an operator *must* be able to change without a desktop are the enabled flag and the run time. The next two sections are those paths.

#### What the run time sets

The run time sets one thing: the time of day a collector that runs once a day or less often starts. The `config.config_collector_run_times` table holds it, one row per collector (fleet-wide or for one server). The table stores it as minutes after midnight on the *monitored server's own clock*. A collector with no row has no run time.

The Viewer's Collector Schedules window writes the run time in the same Save as the schedule rows. Three actions delete the run times of the schedule they reset (the shipped defaults have no fixed time):

- The window's Reset to Defaults.
- A server's Use default schedule.
- Apply Default to All Servers.

A Viewer released before the table existed resets the schedule rows only. A run time set here therefore survives a Reset done in an older Viewer.

The Viewer and the CLI take the run time as a 24-hour `HH:MM` time such as `02:00`.

Without a run time, such a collector starts when the service happened to start. That can be the busiest hour of the server's day. With one, it starts in the hour you chose.

A run time layers like the other columns: the server's own value, then the fleet-wide one, then no fixed time. On a server's row, `None` (stored as `-1`) stops a fleet-wide time on that server only. `Use default` (NULL) lets the next level apply.

A run time works only when the collector's effective interval is a whole number of days (1440 minutes or a multiple of it). On an hourly collector a time only picks the minute past the hour. The Viewer and the CLI refuse it and tell you to change the frequency or clear the time.

The Viewer's editor shows what the time means in server time and in UTC. A fleet row shows the spread, and a server row shows its next run. For an Azure SQL Database, which always reports UTC, it says so.

#### What stays fixed

Two numbers around the run time are not settings.

- Each server starts at the run time plus a fixed offset of under 60 minutes. The offset depends only on the server id, so it is the same after every restart. A fleet-wide `02:00` spreads the fleet across 02:00 to 03:00 instead of starting every server in the same minute.
- A run can start from that slot until 60 minutes after it. A collector that starts inside that hour runs at once. One that cannot (the service was down or busy) waits for the next day's slot. A missed day is skipped, not replayed. A run held by the concurrency cap starts when a place under the cap frees, which can be after the grace hour.

The time follows the server's clock through a daylight-saving change, so it stays at the same local time.

### Enable or disable a collector headlessly (`--enable-collector` / `--disable-collector`)

One collector ships **off**: `long_query_completions`, the long-running-query completion trace. A completion trace fires per statement or batch even though the duration predicate discards most of them, so it is opt-in per fleet — and opting in is a real action on the monitored servers: **enabling it creates this install's own long-query Extended Events session (`PerformanceMonitor_Darling_<id>_LongQueryCompletions`) on the next per-server sweep, and disabling it drops that session there** (the `CollectorScheduleDefaults` entry for it says exactly this, and `DarlingXeSessions.ReconcileLongQueryCompletionsAsync` is the code that follows the flag both ways). On-premises, disabling it keeps the session while another registration of this install on the same instance still has the trace on. On Azure SQL Database the session is database-scoped. Enabling it creates the session in each monitored database and drops it from any other database that has it. Disabling it drops the session from every database that has it. Neither drop removes the session from a database where another registration of that database still has the trace on. A database that is also monitored as its own server is left to that registration. Removing the server also drops this install's session on it, with one attempt within 15 seconds, unless another registration of this install keeps it. If that attempt failed or ran out of time, run [`--drop-xe-sessions --print-sql`](#drop-the-extended-events-sessions-a-removed-server-left-behind---drop-xe-sessions) and run the statements it prints on the server. Until [#3752](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3752) the only writer of that flag was the Viewer's Collector Schedules window — an interactive desktop plus the `admin` role, which a remote viewer seat does not hold by default (`network.role` defaults to `viewer`). Two verbs, run on the **service host** (or, in bring-your-own mode, anywhere the store is reachable), close the gap:

```
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --enable-collector long_query_completions
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --disable-collector long_query_completions
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --enable-collector long_query_completions --server sql01
```

Without `--server` the change is **fleet-wide** — the schedule row with no server, which every server inherits unless it has a row of its own. `--server <name>` writes that one server's row instead; the name is the display name or storage name the Viewer's sidebar and the MCP tools show, matched exactly first and as a partial name only when the partial is unique — an ambiguous fragment is refused with the candidates listed rather than guessed at, since a toggle landing on the wrong sibling is the same coin flip `remove_server` refuses to make. Only the `enabled` flag is written: a cadence or retention override already on the row survives. The verb owns no SQL of its own — it hands the same `enable_collector` / `disable_collector` command the store's command plane carries to the service's own command executor and runs the executor's plan, so an unknown collector name is refused with the executor's message and the full list of known collectors, and the upsert is one implementation rather than two. After the write it reads the collector's rows **back from the store** and prints them — scope, enabled, cadence, retention, database scope — so what you see is what the service will resolve, not an echo of what you typed. The write bumps the config version the running service polls every sweep, so it takes effect within one sweep with no restart. Add `--config <path>` if `darling.json` is not next to the exe and `DARLING_CONFIG` is not set. Exit `0` only when the row was written and read back, so it works as a step in a provisioning script. A failure exits `1` for a usage or configuration problem and `2` when the store cannot be reached, refuses the login, or refuses the change (a wrong password exits `2`, not `1`), so a script can tell a problem with its own arguments or config from a problem at the store. Managed mode needs Windows (the store credential is DPAPI); a bring-your-own store works on any platform, exactly as `--add-server` does. There is deliberately **no MCP tool** for this: the MCP surface has config writers (`add_servers`, alert tuning, custom views) but no collector-schedule writer, and adding one is a product decision the verb does not take — the CLI was the smallest surface that closes the headless gap.

### Make a new password key (`--reset-password-key`)

The service keeps one key pair for the SQL login passwords saved in the store: it publishes the public half, and every place that saves a password seals it to that key. The verb marks the current key replaced, so the next service start retires the old key file and makes a new key.

```
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --reset-password-key
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --reset-password-key --config /etc/darling/darling.json
```

It changes the store only, as the store owner, and never touches a key file. It prints how many saved passwords were sealed to the old key: restart the service, then enter those passwords again.

### Set the time of day a daily collector runs (`--set-collector-run-at`)

A collector that runs once a day or less often, such as `index_object_stats`, can start at a time you choose. The verb writes the same row as the Viewer's Collector Schedules window. It takes a collector name and one of three values:

```
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --set-collector-run-at index_object_stats 02:00
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --set-collector-run-at index_object_stats 03:30 --server sql01
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --set-collector-run-at index_object_stats none --server sql01
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --set-collector-run-at index_object_stats default --server sql01
```

- `HH:MM` is a 24-hour time from `00:00` to `23:59` on the monitored server's own clock.
- `none` on a server's row keeps the fleet-wide time from applying to that server. On the fleet-wide row it clears the time.
- `default` clears the row's time so the next level applies again. A server's row falls back to the fleet-wide time. It creates no row.

Without `--server` the change is **fleet-wide**. `--server <name>` writes that server's own row, with the name matched as `--enable-collector` matches it. Only the run time is written. The enabled flag, cadence and retention already on the row are kept.

The verb refuses a time that is not a 24-hour `HH:MM` time. It also refuses a time on a collector whose cadence, for the row being written, is more often than once a day. In both cases it prints the text the Viewer shows, exits `1` and changes nothing. `none` and `default` are never refused, because clearing is the remedy that refusal names.

After the write the verb reads the collector's rows back from the store and prints them. A row that carries a time prints `run_at=02:00 server time`, so what you see is what the service will resolve. The exit codes, `--config` and the managed-mode rule are those of `--enable-collector`.

The write needs the run-time table, which the service adds when it starts. If the service has not migrated the store yet, the verb says so. It tells you to start the service once to migrate the store, then run the command again. It exits `1` and changes nothing. `--enable-collector` and `--disable-collector` read back without the `run_at=` part instead of failing.

### Stop, start or reschedule a collector from MCP (#6227)

With `mcp.allowWrites` on (and a token), seven MCP tools change collector schedules, and one read shows the result. Each write takes a `collector_name` and an optional `server_name`. Without a server it changes the fleet-wide row, which every server inherits unless it has a row of its own.

| Tool | What it does |
|---|---|
| `disable_collector` / `enable_collector` | Sets the collector's enabled flag. Data already collected stays. |
| `set_collector_frequency` | Sets `frequency_minutes` (0 = once when the server loads), or `clear: true` to remove the override. |
| `set_collector_run_at` | Sets the time of day a once-a-day collector starts (`HH:MM`, `none` or `default`), as `--set-collector-run-at` does. |
| `set_collector_retention` | Sets `retention_days`, or `clear: true` to remove the override. |
| `set_collector_databases` | Sets the collector's database scope (the editor's Databases column) from a comma-separated `databases` list, or `clear: true`. On a server, `databases: ""` is the explicit "no scope" that stops a fleet-wide scope reaching it. Only the collectors that fan out per database read it. |
| `reset_collector_schedules` | The editor's Reset to Defaults: deletes every override row and run time of one scope (the fleet-wide rows with no `server_name`, else that server's), and lists what it removed. The other scope is left alone. |
| `get_collector_schedules` | Lists the stored overrides (enabled, frequency, retention, run time, database scope), for one collector or all, fleet-wide and per server. A read: served without `mcp.allowWrites`. |

The writes run the same plans as `--enable-collector`, `--disable-collector` and `--set-collector-run-at`, and refuse the same things: an unknown collector name, a frequency that is negative or past the cap for a collector that stores deltas, a retention under 1 day, a run time on a collector that runs more often than daily. A refusal changes nothing and says why. A write changes only its own column: the other columns on the row are kept, and a row the tool creates never switches a collector on or off. Each answer gives what changed, the previous value and the exact call that undoes it (`undo`). The service picks the change up on its next config poll; there is no restart.

These tools are never offered to an agent that Darling sends to investigate an alert: it may read with `get_collector_schedules` and may not stop, start or reschedule collection.

The `mcp` database role gets `INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE` on `config.config_collector_schedules` and `config.config_collector_run_times` when the service provisions roles (every start). A bring-your-own store needs `tools/provision-roles.sql` run again to add the same two grants.

### Set the service flags from MCP (#6393)

With `mcp.allowWrites` on (and a token), `update_service_flags` sets the collection flags the Viewer's Settings window saves under Data Collection. Send only the ones to change; an omitted argument keeps the stored value.

| Argument | Meaning | Range |
|---|---|---|
| `capture_plans` | Global plan capture. | true or false |
| `query_store_backfill_enabled` | Query Store history backfill. | true or false |
| `query_store_text_budget_mb` | Query Store text budget. | 4 to 256 |
| `max_concurrent_sweeps` | Server sweeps that run at once. | 1 to 16 |
| `query_store_fetch_top_n` | Plans per database-hour and metric the Query Store collector fetches. | 0 to 100000 (0 = every new plan) |

The ranges are the Settings window's: both call one class (`ServiceFlagLimits`). An out-of-range value answers `invalid`, names the argument and its range, and changes nothing, including the other flags in the same call. The answer carries all five values before and after. The running service reloads on its next sweep.

The MCP on/off flag and port, the web on/off flag and port, and pause are not settable from MCP: turning MCP off from MCP would lock the admin out, and the `mcp` role's column grant covers only these five columns. Change those in the Viewer. Like the schedule tools, `update_service_flags` is never offered to an investigation agent. Lite's MCP server has no write tools, so none of this applies to Lite.

The service grants the `mcp` role `UPDATE` on exactly those five `config.config_service` columns when it provisions roles (every start). A bring-your-own store needs `tools/provision-roles.sql` run again to add that grant; until then `update_service_flags` answers `error` with a permission failure from the store.



### Drop the Extended Events sessions a removed server left behind (`--drop-xe-sessions`)

Removing a server (`remove_server`, the Viewer's Manage Servers dialog) stops collection and drops this install's own Extended Events sessions on the server. The service makes one attempt within 15 seconds. It leaves a session that another registration of this install keeps. A drop that fails or times out never stops the removal. Each install has an id, and the sessions it creates carry that id: `PerformanceMonitor_Darling_<id>_LongQueryCompletions` and, on Azure SQL Database, its own `_Deadlock`, `_BlockedProcess` and `_Attention` sessions. The service never drops the shared sessions: `PerformanceMonitor_Deadlock`, `PerformanceMonitor_BlockedProcess`, `PerformanceMonitor_Attention` and the old shared `PerformanceMonitor_LongQueryCompletions`. Lite, the deprecated Full Dashboard installer and any other Darling service that monitors the same server use those names too. Each session has a 4 MB ring buffer. The long query completions session also tests every completed statement and batch against its duration filter, which is why that collector is opt-in. This verb drops the shared sessions. It also drops this install's own sessions on a server that is still monitored. For a session a removal failed to drop, because the server was unreachable or the attempt timed out, use `--print-sql`. When nothing else monitors the server, run the named form **just before you remove the server**:

```
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --drop-xe-sessions sql01 --dry-run
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --drop-xe-sessions sql01
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --drop-xe-sessions --print-sql
```

`--drop-xe-sessions <server-name>` finds the server the way `--validate-config` does (the store's registry, or `darling.json`'s list when the store cannot be reached), connects with the same connector the service uses and the credentials in the configuration (none are ever taken from arguments), lists which of Darling's sessions exist (the shared names and this install's own), and drops each with `DROP EVENT SESSION [name] ON SERVER`. On Azure SQL Database it does the same for the database-scoped copies in each monitored database (`ON DATABASE`). For the long query completions session it also searches the databases the server excludes, because a session created before a database was excluded stays there with nothing else to drop it. It leaves master, a database that is monitored as its own server, and a database where another registration of the same logical server keeps the session. A registration keeps it when its long query setting is on. That setting is its own schedule row, else the fleet-wide row, else the collector's default, which is off. If the verb cannot read the schedule rows, it counts every other registration as keeping it. On any other server, the verb can leave this install's long query session in place. It does that, with a note on stderr, when another registration of this install on the same instance has the trace on. It matches registrations by the instance name each one last reported, and a name it does not know matches nothing. `--dry-run` lists them and drops nothing; `--config <path>` names a `darling.json`. Only the shared names and this install's own names are ever dropped. The verb reads this install's id from the store and never writes it, because the service makes the id when it starts. With no id, it leaves every session named for an install alone and says so. It lists the sessions of other installs under a heading, each with a guarded `DROP` statement, and never runs them. Run one only when that install no longer monitors the server. A database the server excludes that the login cannot open (no user in it, a paused serverless database) is reported in a note on stderr and does not change the exit code, so a long query completions session left in it needs a manual drop; a monitored database it cannot open makes the verb exit `2`.

The order matters. The named form finds the server in the store's list of enabled servers, so it reaches only a server this service still monitors. Dropping the shared sessions on such a server stops its deadlock and blocked-process capture until this service reconnects to it. The service creates a missing session when it connects. To drop the shared sessions too, run the named form just before you remove the server, and remove the server next.

On Azure SQL Database, a session can't be dropped over a read-only connection. For a registration with read-only intent, the verb first stops the session over the registration's own connection, if it runs there. It then drops the session over a connection without the intent, which reaches the primary. Every other drop is one statement over the registration's own connection. On Hyperscale with several high-availability replicas, that stop reaches only the replica the connection lands on (see the Hyperscale item under Known limits).

The login in the configuration needs the permission that [Microsoft Learn's `DROP EVENT SESSION` page](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/sql/t-sql/statements/drop-event-session-transact-sql) requires. On SQL Server and Azure SQL Managed Instance that is `DROP ANY EVENT SESSION` (SQL Server 2022 and later) or `ALTER ANY EVENT SESSION`; on Azure SQL Database it is `DROP ANY DATABASE EVENT SESSION` in each monitored database. A grant that only lets a login create the sessions (`CREATE ANY DATABASE EVENT SESSION` on Azure SQL Database, for one) does not cover the drop. When the login lacks the grant, the server refuses each drop, the verb prints `[FAILED]` for it, carries on with the next, and exits `2`.

A server that is no longer configured has no definition and no credentials left to connect with, so the named form works only until `remove_server` (or the Manage Servers dialog) removes the server, and a name that matches nothing exits `1` and points at `--print-sql`: `--drop-xe-sessions --print-sql` connects to nothing and prints guarded statements (`IF EXISTS` against `sys.server_event_sessions`, and against `sys.database_event_sessions` for Azure SQL Database) for the three shared names in both scopes, and a query per scope that lists the sessions named for any install, each with its `DROP` statement, to run on the server yourself. Exit codes: `0` when everything found was dropped (or listed) or nothing was there, `1` for a usage, configuration or unknown-name problem, `2` when the server cannot be reached or searched or a drop is refused.

Both modes print one warning, before they list or drop anything: a Lite app, a deprecated Full Dashboard install or another Darling service that still monitors the server uses the same names and creates a missing session again (Lite on its next collection cycle, the Dashboard on its next collection run, Darling on its next connect to the server or, for the long query completions session, within an hour; the long query completions session only where that collector is turned on, and never by the Dashboard), so run this only once nothing else monitors it. A run that drops at least one session also prints one `NOTE:` line after the drops: capture of deadlocks and blocked processes on that server stops until this service reconnects to it, so remove the server next. The line names only the captures whose session was dropped (`long query completions` when that session went too). `--dry-run` and `--print-sql` do not print it.

### Make a login for a system that sends alerts (`--add-alert-source` and friends)

A system outside Darling can hand Darling an alert to deliver through your email, webhook and PagerDuty channels. Each such system is a **source**: a registry entry and a database login of its own. The login can do one thing, call `config.submit_external_alert`, and Darling takes the source name from the login that made the call, so one source cannot send under another's name. These four verbs make and manage the logins. They run as the store owner, the way `--add-server` does, and each takes an optional `--config <path>`.

```
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --add-alert-source orders
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --rotate-alert-source orders
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --list-alert-sources
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --remove-alert-source orders
```

- `--add-alert-source <name>` makes the login `darling_src_<name>`, registers it and gives it the grants, then prints its connection string. **The connection string is shown once.** Darling keeps no copy of the password and the password is never sent to the server as text: the server receives only a SCRAM-SHA-256 verifier. Save the string where the sending system can read it. A name is 2 to 31 characters: a lower-case letter, then lower-case letters, digits or underscores.
- `--rotate-alert-source <name>` sets a new password and prints the new connection string once. The old password stops working at once, for new connections, and the verb then ends the sessions the login already holds and says how many. Use it when a string is lost or leaves the people who should have it.
- `--list-alert-sources` prints each source's name, state, when it was created, when it last sent an alert and how many alerts of its are waiting for delivery. It shows no password, because none is stored. The last-sent time and the waiting count come from the queue rows the store still keeps.
- `--remove-alert-source <name>` removes the source, drops its login and ends the sessions the login still holds. The alerts it has queued but Darling has not yet delivered are removed with it, and the verb says how many. Alerts it already sent stay in the alert history.

The source then sends an alert with one statement, for example `SELECT config.submit_external_alert('fire', 'disk-full', 'warning', 'Disk almost full', '93 percent used', 'web01', 93.5);`. The first argument is `fire` or `clear`, the second is a key of your choosing that pairs a clear with its fire, and the third is `warning` or `critical`.

The host in the printed string is the address this service uses for its store. If the store's owner cannot create logins (a bring-your-own store whose owner lacks CREATEROLE), the verb changes the registry and prints the statements a database administrator runs to make, re-key or drop the login. Those statements end with the login's membership in `darling_alert_sources` (below); `tools/provision-roles.sql` makes that group.

#### Alert sources on another machine

Every source login is a member of `darling_alert_sources`, a NOLOGIN group role that holds no privilege on anything. The service creates it, marks it, and on every start makes the registered source logins its only members (on PostgreSQL 16 and later the membership is granted `WITH INHERIT FALSE, SET FALSE`). A role of that name that Darling did not create stops provisioning; it is never taken over.

For a managed store:

1. Make the store reachable from the network (`postgres.network.listen` and `allowFrom`, see [Remote viewer](#connect-a-remote-viewer)).
2. Add `sources` to `postgres.network.role`, keeping the roles it already lists, for example `"role": "viewer,sources"`.
3. Restart the service. The managed pg_hba block gains `hostssl darling +darling_alert_sources <allowFrom> scram-sha-256`, which admits, from that range over TLS, each role that is a member of `darling_alert_sources` directly or through another role. The service keeps those members to the registered source logins, and writes the line only while they are exactly that: if a member remains that it could not revoke, it leaves the line out (the block's other lines stay) and logs a warning naming the role.
4. `--add-alert-source` and `--rotate-alert-source` print two connection strings: one for a source on this machine, and one for a source on another machine, built the way the remote viewer's is: the store's network address, `SSL Mode=VerifyFull` and `Root Certificate` naming the store's `server.crt`. Copy that file to the source machine and point `Root Certificate` at the copy. Both strings are shown once. When `sources` is not listed yet, the second string is still printed, with a line saying it works once `sources` is listed.

For a bring-your-own store the second string uses the store address from the string the verb connected with, `SSL Mode=VerifyFull`, and a `Root Certificate` placeholder: replace it with the path of the CA certificate that signed the store server's certificate, and set `Host` to a name or address that certificate carries.

When the store owner may not end a login's sessions, or may not turn utility statement tracking off (`pg_stat_statements.track_utility`, which the verbs turn off for their own transaction so the role statement is not recorded), the verb says so and prints the statement a database administrator runs.

A source added later is admitted as soon as `--add-alert-source` returns; there is nothing to restart. For a bring-your-own store, add the same line to your own `pg_hba.conf` (with your database name and range) and reload it. On PostgreSQL 16 and later, a role that is not a superuser and creates the group or a source login (the store owner running `tools/provision-roles.sql` or the add verb, or an administrator running the printed statements) becomes a member of it and cannot revoke that itself; the verbs and the script name every such role, with a `hostssl <database> <role> <range> reject` line to put ABOVE the group line.

A source login can read what every PostgreSQL login can: the system catalogs and statistics views, such as `pg_roles`, `pg_stat_activity` and function definitions in `pg_proc`. In the store it can call `config.submit_external_alert` and nothing else: no table, view or other function of Darling's, and not `pg_stat_statements` (the service and the script take that view from PUBLIC). TimescaleDB's own schemas are not restricted: `timescaledb_information`, `_timescaledb_catalog` and `_timescaledb_config` are readable by every login, and show the store's hypertables, chunks and background job settings (names, intervals and policy configuration, not collected data).

## Operations

### The Store

The service migrates the store itself at startup — plain versioned SQL scripts, each applied once inside its own transaction, tracked in `darling_schema_version`, safe under concurrent starters (advisory-locked). `StorageVersion.SchemaVersion` is the source of truth for the schema version, and a test pins it to the highest rung in the ladder. The service logs the version it found at each start (`Postgres store ready (schema v<N>, ...)`).

The notable rungs are below. For the **complete** current schema, read `Darling/Darling.Tests/Fixtures/migration-ladder-*.sql` — the whole ladder as resolved SQL, regenerated per release; it is generated, so don't hand-edit it.

| Version | Contents |
|---|---|
| **V1** — collector tables | One table per collector, generated from the shared collector definitions (column-for-column identical to Lite's DuckDB schema): `wait_stats`, `latch_stats`, `spinlock_stats`, `query_stats`, `procedure_stats`, `query_store_stats`, `query_snapshots`, `plan_cache_stats`, `cpu_utilization_stats`, `cpu_scheduler_stats`, `file_io_stats`, `memory_stats`, `memory_clerks`, `memory_pressure_events`, `tempdb_stats`, `perfmon_stats`, `deadlocks`, `blocked_process_reports`, `dmv_blocking_snapshots`, `memory_grant_stats`, `waiting_tasks`, `session_stats`, `session_summary_stats`, `running_jobs`, `database_size_stats`, `index_object_stats`, `server_properties`, `system_health_events`, the four config snapshots (`server_config`, `database_config`, `database_scoped_config`, `trace_flags`), and the 28 PostgreSQL tables listed under V63–V69, V71, V83–V94, V96–V99, V102, V103, V106 and V129 below |
| **V2** — observability | `servers` (registry, upserted on every successful connect: identity, display name, engine edition, major version) and `collection_log` (one row per collector run: SUCCESS / PERMISSIONS / ERROR, row count, SQL-phase and storage-phase timings) |
| **V3** — alerting | `config_alert_log` (one history row per fired alert), `config_edge_trigger_watermarks` (restart-surviving edge-trigger and failed-job watermarks), `config_mute_rules` (alert mute rules; starts empty) |
| **V4** — analysis | `analysis_findings` (persisted findings incl. the stored remediation action), `analysis_muted` (muted finding patterns), and 17 `v_<table>` passthrough views so the shared analysis SQL runs verbatim against this store |
| **V5** — viewer passthrough views | The five remaining `v_*` passthrough views (`v_running_jobs`, `v_server_config`, `v_database_scoped_config`, `v_trace_flags`, `v_collection_log`) that complete the viewer's read layer |
| **V6** — memory passthrough views | `v_memory_clerks` and `v_memory_pressure_events`, the two views the Memory tab reads |
| **V7** — plan-capture columns | Nullable plan-XML columns for the viewer's View Plan surfaces: `procedure_stats.query_plan_xml`, `blocked_process_reports.blocked_query_plan_xml` / `blocking_query_plan_xml`, `deadlocks.victim_query_plan_xml` |
| **V8** — schema split (collect/config) | Moves the tables into the `collect` and `config` schemas (least-privilege security split); the shared SQL keeps using bare names, resolved via `search_path = collect, config, public` |
| **V9** — inventory + cost fields | `server_properties` inventory columns (`sqlserver_start_time`, `host_os_version`, `ag_replica_role`) and `servers.monthly_cost_usd` (the FinOps per-server budget) |
| **V10** — latch + spinlock collectors | `latch_stats` and `spinlock_stats` tables plus their `v_*` views |
| **V11** — CPU scheduler + plan cache collectors | `cpu_scheduler_stats` and `plan_cache_stats` tables plus their `v_*` views |
| **V12** — session summary collector | `session_summary_stats` (server-wide connection-leak / idle signal) table plus its `v_*` view |
| **V13** — system health events collector | `system_health_events` (raw `system_health` Extended Events capture) table plus its `v_*` view |
| **V14** — refresh passthrough views | `CREATE OR REPLACE` on every `v_*` view so a store upgraded across a column-adding migration picks up the new columns (Postgres freezes a view's `SELECT *` expansion at create time) |
| **V15** — index metadata columns | Per-index definition columns on `index_object_stats` (ordered key/included column lists, filter, uniqueness/constraint/FK flags, `is_disabled`, and the reconstruct-a-CREATE options — compression, fill factor, page/row locks, etc.) for monitor-side UNUSED/DUPLICATE index analysis, and refreshes `v_index_object_stats` |
| **V16** — server UTC offset | Nullable UTC-offset column on `server_properties` so the viewer can render timestamps in the monitored server's own local time (the Server-time display mode ported from Lite; Server-time = stored naive-UTC + this offset). It is the offset IN FORCE at collection — `DATEDIFF(MINUTE, GETUTCDATE(), GETDATE())` — so it is exact for an instant on the same side of a DST transition as the collection and an hour wrong for one on the other side, and an offset cannot say which; since **V134** the engine's time-zone id sits beside it for that |
| **V17** — config control plane | The viewer-writable DESIRED-state tables (`config_service`, `config_monitored_servers`, `config_alert_settings`, `config_collector_schedules`) plus a `config_version` reload beacon — statement-level bump triggers increment it on any write, and the service polls that one integer each sweep and reloads only when it changes. Server secrets are DPAPI blobs, never plaintext |
| **V18** — alert delivery mode | Global `delivery_mode` (Summary / PerEvent) + `per_event_max` on `config_alert_settings`, plus a nullable per-server `alert_delivery_mode_override` on `config_monitored_servers` (null = inherit the global), resolved through the shared `AlertDeliveryModeResolver` (#1236 / #1141) |
| **V19** — analysis state marker | `collect.analysis_state` — the service-produced per-server "insufficient data" marker (with message + time) the viewer reads, so a not-enough-history analysis pass surfaces a reason instead of a blank |
| **V20** — alert tuning knobs | The previously-hardcoded alert tuning the viewer now customizes on `config_alert_settings`: the long-running-query read shape (`long_running_query_max_results` + five noise-filter opt-outs the shared `AlertEngine` forwards) and `notify_connection_changes` (the Server-Unreachable / Restored connect-edge gate) |
| **V21** — default trace events collector | `default_trace_events` table + its `v_*` view — the significant Default Trace events (file growth, ErrorLog, security audit, optional Object DDL) the viewer's System Events tab reads |
| **V22** — index-object latest index | The engine-agnostic `idx_index_object_stats_latest` partial index backing the latest-capture-per-index reads |
| **V23** — collection-log hypertable | Converts `collection_log` to a TimescaleDB hypertable (an object-invisible no-op on plain PostgreSQL) |
| **V24** — job history collector | `job_history` table + its `v_*` view — the SQL Agent Job History surface (#1433) |
| **V25** — agent status collector | `agent_status` table + its `v_*` view — SQL Agent up/down status (#1433) |
| **V26** — generic webhook channel | The generic-webhook columns on `config_notification` (`generic_url`, `generic_headers`, `generic_body_template`, `generic_proxy`) for POSTing alerts to any endpoint (#1506) |
| **V27** — deadlocks database name | `deadlocks.database_name` (the Azure SQL DB per-database deadlock-capture watermark key, #1535) and a refreshed `v_deadlocks` |
| **V28** — Query Store replica role | `query_store_stats.replica_role` (SQL Server 2022+ AG secondary-replica attribution, #1546) and a refreshed `v_query_store_stats` |
| **V29** — long-query completions collector | `collect.long_query_completions` + its index — the opt-in long-running-query completion trace's store table (#1496) |
| **V30** — web dashboard config | `config_service.web_enabled` + `web_port` — the web dashboard's live enable/port toggle, the twin of `mcp_enabled`/`mcp_port` (#1562) |
| **V46** — automatic plan correction | `collect.plan_correction` + its index — the #1952 collector's store table (FORCE_LAST_GOOD_PLAN enablement plus the engine's live recommendation set). Additive and view-less, so a fresh store gets it from V1's generated schema and V46 is what an already-existing store gets |
| **V47** — ADR persistent version store | `collect.pvs_stats` + its index + the `v_pvs_stats` passthrough view — the #1951 ADR version-store collector's store table. A fresh store gets the table from V1's generated schema; V47 is what an already-existing store gets, and the view is what keeps the Darling viewer's FinOps read byte-identical to Lite's |
| **V61** — per-fingerprint occurrence counters | `config.config_incident_occurrences` — the accumulator’s memory for the monotonic count behind an alert incident (#2216). The count that rides on an incident is a GAUGE (it falls as events age out of the read window), so a consumer seeing only throttled deliveries cannot recover how many events happened between two of them. A NEW table, not columns on `config_edge_trigger_watermarks`: the key is wrong (per (server, metric) vs per (server, metric, fingerprint)) and Lite writes that row with a PARTIAL `INSERT OR REPLACE` column list, so an added column would zero itself every time an alert fired |
| **V62** — plan-XML codec knob | `config.config_service.plan_xml_compression` (#2171). `gzip` (default) keeps today’s write path; `none` stores plain text in `query_plan_xml` so direct-SQL readers get plans back — PostgreSQL exposes no inflate, so gzip bytes are unreadable without an untrusted-language UDF. Rides `config_service` like V58/V59 so the `config_version` trigger makes a flip visible to the next reload poll |
| **V63–V69** — PostgreSQL collector tables | `collect.pg_wait_stats`, `collect.pg_statement_stats`, `collect.pg_wraparound_stats`, `collect.pg_xmin_horizon`, `collect.pg_replication_slot_stats`, `collect.pg_autovacuum_stats`, and `collect.pg_io_stats`, each with its time index — one rung per PostgreSQL collector. Additive and view-less, exactly like V46/V47: a fresh store gets all seven from V1's generated schema, and these rungs are what an already-existing store gets. They add tables only, so a store that monitors no PostgreSQL target carries seven empty tables and nothing else changes |
| **V70** — monitored-server engine + port | `config.config_monitored_servers.engine` (`NOT NULL DEFAULT 'sqlserver'`) and `.port` (`NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` = the driver's default). The registry is authoritative for the server list once seeded, and these were the two `MonitoredServer` fields with no column — so a PostgreSQL target round-tripped as a SQL Server one and was connected to with `SqlConnection`. Every existing row means exactly what it meant before, and the SQL-Server-only writers keep inserting without naming either column |
| **V71** — PostgreSQL blocking edges | `collect.pg_blocking_edges` + its time index — the eighth PostgreSQL collector's store table. One row per (blocked, blocking) pair rather than a rendered tree, which is what lets the read layer compute root blocker, chain depth and fan-out in SQL instead of parsing a string. Additive and view-less exactly like V63–V69. **Sparse by design**: empty on a healthy instance, and because PostgreSQL has no engine-side blocked-process recorder, a gap means "not sampled" rather than "not blocked" — a count over this table measures how often blocking was *caught* |
| **V83** — PostgreSQL per-database counters | `collect.pg_database_stats` + its time index — the ninth PostgreSQL collector's store table (#2539): `pg_stat_database`'s temp-file, cache, deadlock and transaction counters. Additive and view-less exactly like V63–V69 and V71. Every counter column is nullable on purpose — they are cumulative counters differenced at read time, and a NOT NULL 0 default would turn "not reported" into a measurement; `database_name` is nullable because PostgreSQL genuinely emits a NULL-named row for shared relations |
| **V84** — PostgreSQL index usage | `collect.pg_index_usage_stats` + its time index — the tenth PostgreSQL collector's store table (#2541): per-index scan counts and sizes, plus the catalog facts that decide whether an unscanned index can be dropped at all. Most of the width is that second half, and it is stored rather than looked up on demand because the MCP has no ad-hoc path back to a monitored server — whatever is not captured here cannot be recovered later. `last_scan` is nullable with two distinct meanings the read separates (PostgreSQL 15 and below do not record it; on 16+ it is NULL for an index never scanned since the counters were reset), and `stats_reset` is nullable because it is genuinely NULL until a database's statistics are first reset |
| **V85** — PostgreSQL table bloat | `collect.pg_table_bloat_stats` + its time index — the eleventh PostgreSQL collector's store table (#2542): the statistics-based bloat ESTIMATE with the measured sizes and counter-based dead-tuple pair beside it. **Three tiers of certainty share the table and the column names carry the difference**: `heap_bytes`/`toast_bytes`/`index_bytes` are measured (`pg_relation_size` asks the filesystem), `live_tuples`/`dead_tuples` are the server's own counters, and `bloat_bytes_estimate`/`bloat_pct_estimate` are arithmetic over column-width statistics — suffixed `_estimate` in the store so the qualifier cannot be lost between here and a screen. `estimate_unavailable` is load-bearing rather than advisory: it is TRUE when the monitoring login cannot SELECT the table and `pg_stats` filtered every row out, a state in which the estimator does not fail but silently returns large numbers |
| **V86** — PostgreSQL session states | `collect.pg_session_states` + its time index — the twelfth PostgreSQL collector's store table (#2540): who is holding a transaction open and whether it pins the xmin horizon. **`horizon_age` is the column the table exists for**, and its `-1` is load-bearing rather than tidy: `pg_xmin_horizon` can say a session holds the horizon, and only this can say a session does NOT — which is the answer for two of the four idle-in-transaction shapes measured on a live instance. There is **no query-text column, deliberately**: `pg_stat_activity.query` carries literal parameter values and this table fills on a duration floor an ordinary application crosses, so `query_id` (joinable to `pg_statement_stats`, whose text is already `$1`-normalised) and a whitelisted `command_tag` carry the statement identity instead. `state_is_redacted` exists because without `pg_monitor` PostgreSQL does not refuse the read — it returns every row with the state columns NULL while leaving `backend_xmin` and `backend_xid` visible, so the horizon still reads as pinned and nothing can say by what |
| **V87** — PostgreSQL plan-capture readiness | `collect.pg_plan_capture_readiness` + its time index (#2564): whether a target could capture execution plans at all, and if not, WHICH step is missing. One row per facet rather than a single verdict, because “no plans” has several unrelated causes — the module not preloaded, a threshold that captures nothing, a log line prefix that cannot attribute a plan to a query — and each has a different remedy, carried on the row |
| **V88** — PostgreSQL write side | `collect.pg_write_stats` + its time index (#2544): checkpoints, background writing and WAL from `pg_stat_checkpointer`, `pg_stat_bgwriter` and `pg_stat_wal`, as ONE collector because they are one story. A union schema with version-conditional column expressions: PostgreSQL 17 moved checkpoint counters out of `pg_stat_bgwriter`, so a major that lacks a column reports NULL rather than the collector splitting into per-version twins. `wal_bytes` is `numeric(38,0)` because that is how upstream types it |
| **V89** — PostgreSQL extension availability | `collect.pg_extension_availability` + its time index (#2545): four states, not a boolean — `installed`, `outdated`, `available`, `absent`. The distinction is what makes it actionable: `available` is a `CREATE EXTENSION` away and `absent` is not. Absence is not a row in any catalog, so it is derived against an enumerated roster; `auto_explain` and `pg_wait_sampling` are deliberately off that roster, being preload-only modules that never appear in `pg_available_extensions` even where they are loaded and working. Runs per database as of V95 |
| **V90** — PostgreSQL lock states | `collect.pg_lock_stats` + its time index (#2544): lock state by mode, type and relation. Does NOT duplicate `pg_blocking_edges`, which stores blocked/blocker PAIRS — that answers who is stuck behind whom and cannot answer what lock, on what object, is being waited on |
| **V91** — PostgreSQL column statistics | `collect.pg_column_stats` + its time index (#2543): the planner inputs behind a misestimate — `n_distinct`, `null_frac`, `avg_width`, `correlation`. **The SHAPE of the skew, never the values**: `most_common_freqs[1]` and `cardinality(most_common_vals)` are stored and `most_common_vals` / `histogram_bounds` deliberately are not, because those hold raw customer data and every finding this table exists for survives without them. `n_distinct` is a floating type rather than a count because negatives are a RATIO of row count. **Needs `pg_read_all_data`**: `pg_stats` filters on `has_column_privilege` and `pg_monitor` confers no SELECT on user tables, so without that grant this collector succeeds and returns zero rows |
| **V92** — PostgreSQL replication | `collect.pg_replication_stats` + its time index (#2544): connected standbys and how far behind each one is — four byte distances measured from `pg_current_wal_lsn()` and three time lags. The lag columns keep growing during a stall rather than freezing, which is what makes them usable as an alerting signal |
| **V93** — PostgreSQL buffer residency | `collect.pg_buffer_usage` + its time index (#2544): what is resident in shared buffers, by relation — a hit ratio says how often the pool worked, this says what is IN it. Joins `pg_buffercache.relfilenode` to `pg_relation_filenode(c.oid)`, **not** to `c.oid`, which is the join every published example gets wrong, and scopes to the connected database because the pool is cluster-wide while `pg_class` is not |
| **V94** — PostgreSQL index bloat | `collect.pg_index_bloat` + its time index (#2561): b-tree index bloat, measured via `pgstatindex` **as this rung shipped it**. That decision was correct on its own terms and #3234 reversed it: the estimator route was blind because it needs `pg_stats`, which returns nothing to a `pg_monitor` role, and once `pg_read_all_data` was granted the estimator became viable and the census became the on-request path. Read this row as history — the current behaviour is the grant and extension story above. `avg_leaf_density` is stored RAW and never converted to a percentage, and `skipped_reason` exists so a size ceiling can never masquerade as an absence of bloat |
| **V95** — PostgreSQL per-database attribution | `database_name` on `collect.pg_column_stats`, `collect.pg_index_bloat` and `collect.pg_extension_availability` (#2599), and `pg_extension_availability` now runs per database. Two of these collectors ran per database and could not say WHICH database a row described, so on a cluster carrying one schema in two databases their rows collided; the third read the per-database `pg_extension` catalog through a single connection and reported one database's answer for the whole server. Nullable with no backfill — a catalog-only change in PostgreSQL, and rows collected before this rung genuinely do not know where they came from |
| **V96** — PostgreSQL wait sampling | `collect.pg_wait_sampling` + its time index (#2603): wait events attributed to the query that waited, from the `pg_wait_sampling` extension. Wait analysis existed ONLY on Aurora before this — backwards, because self-hosted is where the extension story is richest. `sample_count` is a tally of observations, not a duration, and `profile_period_ms` travels beside it because the count is uninterpretable alone; storing a derived millisecond figure would bake today's sampling period into history that outlives it. No `database_name`, deliberately: the profile is cluster-wide, and attributing its rows to a database would be exactly the scope error V95 removed from three other tables |
| **V97** — PostgreSQL kernel stats | `collect.pg_kernel_stats` + its time index (#2603): the operating system's own CPU and disk numbers per `queryid`, from `pg_stat_kcache`. `pg_stat_statements` reports elapsed time, which cannot separate a query that was WAITING from one that was burning processor — these compose with it to make that split. `exec_read_bytes` measures I/O that reached the DEVICE (a page-cache hit is genuinely zero), and `stats_since` is stored so a reset is a recorded fact rather than something inferred from a counter that went backwards |
| **V98** — PostgreSQL predicate stats | `collect.pg_predicate_stats` + its time index (#2603): which columns are filtered on, how selectively, and where the planner's estimate was wrong, from `pg_qualstats`. `pg_index_usage_stats` records indexes that were USED; this records predicates that were EVALUATED, including the ones with no index behind them — the index-candidate signal, invisible to everything else here. `sample_rate` is stored per row and is usually not 1 (the extension defaults to `1/max_connections`), so counts can never be read as complete; `worst_estimate_error_ratio` is the reason to look — selectivity says an index might help, the error ratio says the planner does not understand the column |
| **V99** — PostgreSQL plan capture | `collect.pg_plan_capture` + its time index (#2566): `auto_explain`'s JSON plans out of the server log. Its own table, NOT `query_plan_dim` — that one is a documented XML contract with direct-SQL consumers, and JSON rows would break their dashboards with no schema change to notice. No query text and no literals reach the store: identity is `query_id`, every remaining string is redacted before storage, and `plan_hash` is of the REDACTED plan so a shape recurs to one hash whatever values it ran with. Self-hosted reads the log with `pg_read_file`; Aurora and RDS have no filesystem and take the RDS log API instead (#2538) |
| **V102** — PostgreSQL server configuration | `collect.pg_server_config` + its time index (#2658): every `pg_settings` row, hourly for a year — SQL Server answers this three ways and PostgreSQL had no answer at all, and no other part of the stack can reconstruct a configuration history that was never recorded. Nothing is filtered at collection: `source` is stored so the READ can state which rows are server configuration and which are the collector's own session, where the rule can be enforced and explained |
| **V103/V104** — PostgreSQL deadlocks | `collect.pg_deadlocks` + its time index, then the `deadlock_hash` lookup index as its own rung (#2661): the full reports PostgreSQL writes to its log — we collected the COUNT (`pg_stat_database.deadlocks`) and nothing else. `deadlock_hash` is the identity, because the `pg_read_file` route re-reads an OVERLAPPING tail deliberately so one deadlock lands many times, and every read groups on the hash to answer once per deadlock. `graph_text` is stored whole beside the parsed columns, so a lock shape the parser does not recognise still arrives readable by a person. The index is a separate rung because the schema-generator pin permits exactly one index per collector rung — split out, BOTH fresh and upgraded stores get it |
| **V106** — PostgreSQL instance CPU | `collect.pg_cpu_utilization` + its time index (#2719): instance-level CPU for an Aurora target, from the Performance Insights API. This collector has no SQL route at all — PostgreSQL exposes no instance-level CPU signal — so every row arrives through the monitoring host's AWS identity, never a database connection |
| **V129** — PostgreSQL log events | `collect.pg_log_events` + its time index (#3601): the classified server-log events — `occurred_at`, `family`, `severity`, `sqlstate`, `database_name`, `user_name`, `application_name`, `pid`, `message` / `detail` / `context` as PostgreSQL wrote them with the SQL in them normalized, `statement_fingerprint` (a hash of the REDACTED statement; the text itself is never stored) and `raw_line_hash`, the identity every read dedupes on because the `pg_read_file` route re-reads an overlapping tail.
