## Troubleshooting

For a table of what you see, the likely cause and the fix, start with [docs/troubleshooting.md](../docs/troubleshooting.md#darling).

**"Cannot load configuration"** (critical, service idles) — no `darling.json` was found at the resolved path. The message names the path it tried; copy `darling.sample.json` there or point `DARLING_CONFIG` at your file. A file another process is holding open is retried for two minutes first, logged as `"Cannot load configuration yet ... attempt N of 25"` at warning; a missing, malformed or unreadable file is never retried, so this line arrives immediately for it.

**"Configuration problem: ..."** (critical, service idles) — validation failed. The messages are literal and per-field, e.g. `postgres.connectionString is required.`, `servers must contain at least one entry.`, `server 'X': host is required.`, `server 'X': sql auth requires username.`, `server 'X': sql auth requires encryptedPassword (preferred; see --encrypt-password) or password.`, `server 'X': auth must be 'integrated' or 'sql'`. Fix the file and restart the service.

**"Managed Postgres bootstrap failed"** — (critical, service idles) the bundled PostgreSQL could not be unpacked, initialized or started. The message names the failing step. A transiently locked file, or a cluster still coming up, is retried for two minutes first, logged as `"Managed Postgres bootstrap failed, retrying ... attempt N of 25"` at warning; a missing or incomplete `pg-runtime.zip`, a missing credential file, or an unreadable data directory is never retried, so this line arrives immediately for those. Managed mode is Windows-only.

**"Cannot reach or migrate the Postgres store"** (critical, service idles) — the store connection string is wrong, PostgreSQL is down/unreachable, or the login cannot create tables. Collection does not start until this succeeds; fix and restart. A transient failure does not reach this line: a store that is restarting, failing over, still coming up or at its connection limit (SQLSTATE 53300), and a sibling instance holding the migration advisory lock, are retried for two minutes first, logged as `"Cannot reach or migrate the Postgres store yet ... attempt N of 25"` at warning. A rung that cannot apply against this store is never retried, so this line arrives immediately for it.

**"uses a plaintext password in darling.json"** (warning, every connect) — you set `"password"` instead of `"encryptedPassword"`. It works, but run `--encrypt-password` on the service machine and switch.

**DPAPI decrypt fails after moving darling.json** — `encryptedPassword` blobs are machine-bound (DPAPI LocalMachine). Re-run `--encrypt-password` on the new machine.

**"Failed to ensure XE sessions"** — the login lacks `ALTER ANY EVENT SESSION` (or the database-scoped equivalent on Azure SQL Database). Deadlock and blocked-process collection read zero rows until the sessions exist, and client-timeout collection records a missing session; grant the permission or have an administrator create/start `PerformanceMonitor_Deadlock`, `PerformanceMonitor_BlockedProcess` and `PerformanceMonitor_Attention`. "Already exists / already started" XE errors are logged as benign and mean the sessions are up.

**Blocked-process reports empty** — the blocked-process threshold may still be 0. On AWS RDS set `blocked process threshold (s)` via a Parameter Group (the `sp_configure` bootstrap cannot run there); on Azure SQL Database the threshold is fixed at 20 seconds. Blocking stays visible either way through the always-on DMV blocking snapshot.

**`PERMISSIONS` rows in `collection_log`** — that collector's reads were denied (SQL errors 229/297/300). Check the [permissions](#permissions-on-monitored-servers); the collector retries every cycle and recovers as soon as the grant lands.

**"Skipping recently-failed-job check"** (info) — the login cannot read `msdb.dbo.sysjobs` / `sysjobhistory`, so failed-job alerts are skipped. Expected for minimal-privilege monitoring logins. If you want job alerts, add the direct msdb table `SELECT`s from the [permissions](#permissions-on-monitored-servers) section — **not** `SQLAgentReaderRole`, which gates the `sp_help_job*` procedures this product never calls and leaves the reads failing with error 229.

**"TimescaleDB setup failed — continuing in plain-PostgreSQL mode"** (warning) — the extension exists but conversion hit a problem. Everything still works (DELETE-based retention, plain tables), and nothing here waits for a restart any more. The *availability* half: the hourly store-maintenance tick re-probes the extension and logs **"TimescaleDB is available after all"** at Information when it comes back, which restores the store background-job health check (policy-job self-heal, job cadence, Retention Held) that the fallback had switched off (#3815). The *conversion* half: the same tick's store-object convergence pass re-runs every ensure the failed block had reached — so the hypertables, policies and aggregates it did not get to are built on the next pass rather than at the next start (#3817).

**The web viewer or MCP tools are slow** — ask the store which statements it spends its time on: `get_store_query_stats` (MCP, or `/api/read/get_store_query_stats` on the web host) ranks the store's own SQL by server-side cost from `pg_stat_statements`, split by the role that ran each statement, with each role's share of the recorded time (#3899). On a **managed** or **compose** store each surface has its own login: `viewer` is the web viewer, remote read-only Darling Viewer seats and the service's custom-alert rule evaluation, `mcp` is the MCP tools, `admin` is the Darling Viewer on the service's machine, and `owner` is the service's collection, maintenance and alerting. A **bring-your-own** store runs its web dashboard and MCP server as the owner login unless `postgres.webConnectionString` / `postgres.mcpConnectionString` give them logins of their own ([#3914](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3914)), and then their statements rank under `owner` beside the service's (the answer says so); the statements themselves still name the query. The figures are cumulative since the counters were last reset and cover server-side execution only, so a slow page whose statements rank low is spending its time elsewhere (transfer, payload size, the client). Utility statements are not tracked, so the collectors' `COPY` ingest is not in them (`get_collector_cost` has that side). A single slow statement is also written to the store's own log: on a managed or compose store, and on a bring-your-own store whose roles `provision-roles.sql` created, `viewer` and `mcp` statements that run past a third of their `statement_timeout` (5 seconds at the default 15) are logged, bind parameters excluded, and surface in `get_store_log` under `slow_statement` with comments stripped and every literal masked. **The managed store loads `pg_stat_statements` by itself** (the conf's v13 block adds it to `shared_preload_libraries`, merged with anything you added there or in an included file, and turns `pg_stat_statements.track_utility` off so role DDL, which carries passwords, is never recorded); the preload takes effect on the store's next service-owned start, and every start logs a warning if a later edit or an `ALTER SYSTEM` override drops it. **On a store you run yourself**, follow step 1b of `tools/provision-roles.sql`: add `pg_stat_statements` to `shared_preload_libraries` and set `pg_stat_statements.track_utility = off` (both in `postgresql.conf`; with `ALTER SYSTEM`, one quoted literal per library, `'timescaledb', 'pg_stat_statements'`, because a single literal holding the list is stored as one library name and the server will not start), restart PostgreSQL, and run `CREATE EXTENSION pg_stat_statements;` as a superuser in the `darling` database. If the service's login is not a superuser, other roles' statement text reads `<insufficient privilege>`: granting it `pg_read_all_stats` shows that text but also lets the login read every session's query text on the cluster, and a bring-your-own store's web and MCP hosts run as it when they have no logins of their own, so leave it out on a shared cluster. Only normalized DML shows its text; a utility statement (recorded only while `track_utility` is on, with its literals as typed) and a statement that opens with a comment keep their timings and read as withheld. The service builds its reader functions at its next start, and then within the hour. Until then the tool answers `precondition` and names what is missing and how to fix it. The store also keeps its own history of these figures: once an hour the service records each statement's change in calls, time, rows and blocks since the last snapshot into `collect.store_statement_history`, the 100 statements that spent the most time, kept 90 days (#5097). Deltas survive a service restart, a store restart, a `pg_stat_statements_reset()` and the nightly upgrade, and each snapshot's `collect.store_statement_captures` row says how many statements were active, how many entries the extension evicted, and whether the hour was a fresh baseline. `get_store_query_history` (MCP, or `/api/read/get_store_query_history` on the web host) reads it: without `query_id` the top statements over `hours_back` by time spent, with the mean of the newest quarter of their hours against the rest; with `query_id` that statement's hourly series, oldest first, with the first-seen, entry-restarted and reset flags.

**"Store object convergence: 14 steps, 0 changed (none), 0 failed (none), 214 ms"** (information, hourly and once at startup with an `at startup` prefix) — the convergence pass's own receipt (#3817). Every pass writes one, converged or not, for the reason the job-health line does: an absent line used to mean either *every store object is in place* or *the pass never ran and nobody noticed*. **steps** is how many ensures ran; **changed** names the ones that actually altered something (a converged store changes nothing, every hour, and says so); **failed** names any ensure that threw — each of which also logs its own warning, and each of which is retried on the next pass, which is the whole point of the cadence. A store that has just gained a collector or an aggregate shows it here once and then goes quiet. The elapsed is the pass's own clock including connection acquisition: on a converged store it is well under a second, and a pass that takes minutes is either building something for the first time or is on a store too slow to answer its own catalog — the per-object lines above it say which.

**"Store job health: 12 jobs read (compression 6, refresh 3, retention 3), 0 dead, 0 stuck, 1 held, 0 re-armed, 0 alerted"** (information, hourly) — the background-job self-heal's own receipt (#3816). Every pass writes one, healthy or not, because "no alert" used to mean either *nothing is wrong* or *the connection open failed, was swallowed at Debug, and nothing has been checked for days*. What the numbers mean:

- **dead** — `next_start = -infinity` while not running: the scheduler has abandoned the job. Below TimescaleDB 2.26.4 the service re-arms it once (`alter_job(next_start => now())`) and pages; from 2.26.4 on that state is a crash backoff the scheduler clears itself, so the first sighting is logged and left alone and a second consecutive sighting pages without re-arming — re-arming there *resets* the backoff instead of shortening it (#3591, upstream #9360).
- **stuck** — running for longer than its own bound: a run that hung and never finished.
- **held** — `scheduled = false`, which for a retention policy is the [rollup-coverage gate](../docs/retention-hold-runbook.md) working as designed. **Held is counted and never acted on.** A held policy is not a dead one, and arming one by hand drops history no rollup has materialized.
- **re-armed / alerted** — what this pass did about the above.

Each family pages under its own name so a mute or a notification route on one is not silently a mute on the others: `Compression Job Stuck` (critical — the archival tier stops draining and the disk fills), `Refresh Job Stuck` (critical — a rollup stops materializing, every reader of that view gets silently stale answers, and the coverage gate then holds that tier's retention), `Retention Job Stuck` (warning — the store keeps history it was told to drop, which is disk and nothing else). `Store Job Failing` (information) is the fourth: a job whose failure count grew since the previous hourly sample and whose last run reports `Failed` — alive and being retried, but failing, which no other surface reported (#2136's cadence check reads only *successful* runs, so it gets quieter as a job gets sicker).

**"out of background workers" / "failed to start a background worker" in the postmaster log, or the store keeps growing despite compression** — bring-your-own stores only: the cluster has fewer worker slots than the store has policies, so compression and retention jobs are being skipped. An occasional one is benign (the job retries on its next schedule); persistent ones mean the store is effectively uncompressed. Size the two settings and restart the server — see [Background workers](#background-workers-sizing-an-unmanaged-store-and-what-happens-if-you-dont), and multiply them if the cluster hosts more than one store. `timescaledb_information.job_stats` tells you whether jobs are actually succeeding.

**"Why are there 40+ postgres.exe processes?"** — the count is three populations, and only one is client connections: (1) PostgreSQL's own system processes (postmaster, checkpointer, WAL/background writers, autovacuum, stats); (2) **TimescaleDB background workers** — the managed conf sizes `timescaledb.max_background_workers` from host RAM (74 on a 4 GB host, up to 177 on 12 GB or more), and every RUNNING compression/retention policy job is its own process, so the count legitimately surges during checkpoint/compression waves and falls back when they finish; (3) client backends — the service's pools are capped at 24, the co-located viewer's at 10. Decompose it live with: `SELECT backend_type, count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY backend_type ORDER BY 2 DESC;` — and remember Windows charges the shared buffer segment to every attached process's working set, so per-process memory numbers cannot be summed.

**query_store bursts every ~15 minutes** — two or three near-empty cycles, then one large one, is Query Store's own behavior, not a collector bug: the engine buffers in memory and flushes to its persisted tables on `DATA_FLUSH_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (default 900s), so the collector genuinely sees nothing new between flushes. Narrowing the collection interval will not smooth it. The per-database log lines show which database drove a burst.

**MCP client cannot connect** — MCP defaults to off. Enable it live from the Viewer's Settings (the checkbox writes the control plane; the service starts the endpoint within seconds, no restart), or set `mcp.enabled: true` in `darling.json` for a file-seeded install. If the log says `Port 5152 is already in use — MCP server not started`, change `mcp.port`. The MCP server binds to `localhost` only unless you opt into a LAN endpoint (see [Opt-in Network Endpoints (LAN)](#opt-in-network-endpoints-lan)); a remote client that gets 401 is missing or mismatching the required bearer token, and one that is refused before any response is outside the configured `allowFrom` CIDR.

**Recommendations tab says no findings** — analysis runs every 30 minutes per server but only once the store holds at least 24 hours of collected data for that server; a fresh install simply has not earned findings yet. (The exception is **same-statement pileup**, which needs only the current snapshot plus 45 minutes of that statement's own history, so it can appear on a young install while every other finding is still waiting out the data-span gate.)

**The Viewer will not connect** — the failure window carries a **Configuration this viewer used** block naming the `darling.json` it read (and which rule picked it: an explicit command-line path, `DARLING_CONFIG`, beside the viewer, or the service root), plus the host, port, username, database, SSL mode, search path and certificate path it parsed. Read it before changing anything: the two faults it separates are *the viewer read a different file than you edited* and *it read your file and a value in it is wrong*. **Copy details** puts the whole block on the clipboard for a bug report, and the same lines are in `%APPDATA%\PerformanceMonitorDarling\logs\darling-viewer_yyyyMMdd.log`. It never contains a password.

**"Root Certificate ... exists: NO"** — with `SSL Mode=VerifyFull`, a **relative** `Root Certificate` path resolves against **the folder holding the `darling.json` the viewer read** — not the working directory, so how the viewer was launched no longer changes the answer. The diagnostics block prints that folder and the absolute path it actually opened; either put `server.crt` beside the config or make the `Root Certificate` value an absolute path. If you no longer have the certificate, re-run `--export-viewer-config` on the store host and copy the folder again (see [Connect a Remote Viewer](#connect-a-remote-viewer)) — it regenerates if the bind IP changes, so an old copy stops matching.

## Managed Bundled PostgreSQL

This section covered the store that the service runs for itself in managed mode. It moved to [Managed Bundled PostgreSQL](../docs/darling/managed-postgresql.md).

## Security & Least-Privilege Roles

The store is split into two schemas so that no consumer connects with more privilege than it needs:

- **`collect`** — the collector hypertables (one per collector) plus the service-written, user-read metadata (`servers`, `collection_log`, `analysis_findings`, the `v_*` views). Read-only to everyone but the service.
- **`config`** — exactly the tables a human operator changes through the Viewer or MCP: `config_mute_rules`, `config_alert_log` (alert dismissals), `config_edge_trigger_watermarks`, and `analysis_muted`.

Table names are unchanged — only their schema moved — and the shared SQL keeps using the bare, unqualified names, resolved through `search_path = collect, config, public` (set as the database default and carried on the managed connection strings). This is deliberate: Darling's SQL is byte-identical to Lite's DuckDB SQL, and re-qualifying it would fork that twin.

**The roles.** The service still owns the store as the `darling` superuser (it does the DDL — migrations, hypertable conversion, retention). On top of that, **managed mode provisions three least-privilege login roles**, and so does the service on the compose distribution's own store; any other bring-your-own store gets the same three from a shipped script (see below):

| Role | Privileges | Used by |
|---|---|---|
| `darling` | superuser / owner | the service (collection, migration, provisioning) |
| `admin` | SELECT on both schemas — **including** the secret columns, which the Settings window reads — plus INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on `config` only. No statement timeout | the Viewer, by default (`connectAs: "admin"`) |
| `viewer` | SELECT on all of `collect`, and on `config` **minus the secret columns** of `config_monitored_servers` / `config_command` / `config_notification` (carved fail-closed, below) + the web dashboard's narrow single-table writes: INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on `config.custom_views` (the composer's saved views), on `config.custom_alert_rules` (the user-authored alert rules), on `config.database_state_expected` (the per-database override editor) and on `config.config_mute_rules` (the dedicated mute-rule endpoints, [#3450](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3450)) — plus UPDATE on exactly the two `config_service` reload-beacon columns the mute table's bump trigger writes as the caller. Runs under `statement_timeout = 15s` | a locked-down Viewer (`connectAs: "viewer"`), and the web dashboard (on a bring-your-own store, when `postgres.webConnectionString` names it) |
| `mcp` | `viewer`'s exact read surface + INSERT on `collect.analysis_findings` + INSERT/DELETE on `config.analysis_muted` (the mute and unmute tools) + INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on `config.custom_views` (the custom-view tools) + INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on `config.database_state_expected` (`set_database_state_expected`, #6397) + UPDATE of the three close columns on `config.investigation_requests` and INSERT of the five decided-note columns and UPDATE of the four decision columns on `config.investigation_notes` (the four investigation admin tools, #6400) + INSERT of the practice request's columns on `config.investigation_requests` and of the copied alert columns on `config.investigation_request_alerts`, INSERT/UPDATE of the feedback columns on `config.investigation_feedback` and INSERT/UPDATE of the profile columns plus DELETE on `config.agent_environment_profiles` (the three tools that start a practice request, record feedback and save a profile, #6400) + the alert-tuning writes (INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on `config.config_mute_rules`, UPDATE on `config.config_alert_settings`, UPDATE on the single non-secret `email_cooldown_minutes` column of `config.config_notification`, and the `config_service` reload-beacon columns) + the server-onboarding writes (INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on `config.config_monitored_servers` — the credential column stays SELECT-carved, so it can WRITE a password blob but never READ one back) | the store identity the MCP host connects as — loopback and network alike — on a managed store (`DarlingMcpHostService` builds its pool from the stored `mcp`-role credential, never from the owner's) and on the compose store; on any other bring-your-own store when `postgres.mcpConnectionString` names it |

`admin` cannot `DROP`, alter schema, touch `collect` data, or create objects — it can only do what the Viewer's mute-rule / alert-dismiss surfaces need. The `mcp` role is narrower still: it reads exactly what `viewer` reads (the secret config columns are carved out identically) and its writes are a small, enumerated set — the two analysis-table INSERTs (`analyze_server` + `mute_analysis_finding`), the single-table `config.custom_views` CRUD (the custom-view tools), the alert-tuning writes (`config.config_mute_rules` CRUD + a single-row `config.config_alert_settings` UPDATE + a single-COLUMN `config.config_notification` UPDATE for the delivery cooldown, plus the two `config_service` beacon columns so a settings write's self-bump trigger can fire), and the server-onboarding writes (`config.config_monitored_servers` CRUD for `add_servers` / `remove_server` — its `config_monitored_servers` write fires the SAME `config_service` beacon trigger, already covered by that column grant) — so a token-holder on the network MCP endpoint can never reach the `config`-table service-credential pivot, the secret columns, or a service flag like `paused`. Even on `config_monitored_servers`, which it may write, the `encrypted_password` column stays in the fail-closed secret carve, so `mcp` can WRITE a credential blob (onboarding) but can never READ one back. `config_notification` is the sharpest case of the same shape: it holds the SMTP password blob, the Teams/Slack/generic webhook URLs and the PagerDuty routing key, so the write there is COLUMN-level on the one non-secret cooldown column — `mcp` cannot write even a non-secret sibling like `smtp_host`, let alone a credential. `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES` means new collector tables auto-inherit SELECT for `admin`/`viewer`, so the model never drifts as collectors are added (every `mcp` write is an explicit single-table/single-column grant, deliberately not schema-wide).

**Managed mode** provisions all of this automatically on every start (idempotent and self-healing), generating a per-role DPAPI-LocalMachine credential — `pg-admin-credential.dpapi`, `pg-viewer-credential.dpapi`, and `pg-mcp-credential.dpapi` beside the data directory, same posture as the owner's `pg-credential.dpapi`. The passwords never travel in a statement: provisioning sends each role's SCRAM-SHA-256 verifier, which PostgreSQL stores as-is (what psql's `\password` does), and only for a role whose stored verifier no longer matches its credential file, so a steady-state start sends none (#3910). Every start also switches off `SUPERUSER`, `CREATEROLE`, `CREATEDB`, `REPLICATION` and `BYPASSRLS` on the three roles and revokes any role membership someone granted one of them since ([#3914](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3914)): the batch grants none, and either would outrank the grants above (a `pg_read_all_data` membership reads the carved secret columns). Nothing to configure beyond `connectAs`.

**The compose store** gets the same roles from the same batch ([#3914](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3914)), because it is the service's own: the service recognizes it by logging in as the store's bootstrap superuser (the role `initdb` created, `POSTGRES_USER` in the compose file), inside a container. The passwords are generated the same way and kept as plain owner-only files (`pg-admin-credential`, `pg-viewer-credential`, `pg-mcp-credential`, the `file:` secret shape) in the `darling-credentials` volume at `/var/lib/darling/credentials` — the container boundary is the isolation there, as it is for `secrets/`. Three differences, each deliberate: the roles carry the comment `darling-compose` rather than `darling-managed`, so the service never takes over a role `provision-roles.sql` made (it re-keys the roles it owns from its credential files every start, and would otherwise reset an operator's password); the owner and database are whatever the store's login and database are called; and the database-level `REVOKE ALL … FROM PUBLIC` is left out, because people read the compose store directly with roles of their own and that statement would take their `CONNECT` away. When the store is not recognized (a login that is not the bootstrap superuser, or a cluster that also holds a database besides the store's own and `postgres`, which makes it an operator's own cluster), or a same-named role is not the service's, nothing is created. On such a start, and on one whose provisioning fails or never runs because the collector stopped first, the web dashboard and MCP keep connecting as `viewer` and `mcp` with the credentials an earlier start wrote, if the files pass the trust checks; a role login the store refuses keeps that surface down rather than falling back. Only a surface with no usable credential connects as the owner. Each surface's startup warning says which of these it is and why. The trust checks: every start sets the credentials directory back to owner-only (0700) before reading it, and a directory that is a symbolic link, still reachable by other users afterwards, or was writable by them before has none of its files read (every role gets a new password that start). Each file must be a regular file with no group or other permissions. The file's owner cannot be checked from managed code; the named volume the Dockerfile seeds as `root:root` 0700 is what keeps anyone else from creating one, so a bind mount another host user can write to is outside what the checks can see.

**Credential file protection.** DPAPI LocalMachine scope is deliberate (the service writes the credential, a *different* interactive user's Viewer reads it), which means the machine-bound blob is decryptable by anything that can *read* the file. So the credential files are locked down with an NTFS ACL that strips the inherited world-read `%ProgramData%` would give them:

| File(s) | Readable by |
|---|---|
| `pg-credential.dpapi` (superuser), `pg-mcp-credential.dpapi` (the network MCP role) + the transient init pwfile | SYSTEM, Administrators, the service account. **Not** the operator group |
| `pg-admin-credential.dpapi`, `pg-viewer-credential.dpapi` | the above **+ members of the local group `PerformanceMonitor Darling Operators`** (the Viewer and the CLI, run without elevation) |
| `log-hash-key.dpapi` (the store's log-hash key, [#4004](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/4004)) | SYSTEM, Administrators, the service account. **Not** the operator group |

`pg-mcp-credential.dpapi` sits with the superuser (non-interactive) rather than with the Viewer's credentials because only the in-service MCP host reads it — never an interactive Viewer.

**The log-hash key** ([#4004](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/4004)). `pg_log_events` stores two identities per event: `raw_line_hash`, which every read dedupes on, and `statement_fingerprint`, which groups one statement shape. Both are HMAC-SHA-256 under a 32-byte key the service generates and keeps **outside** the store, so a reader of the store cannot test guesses at the literals a log line hid (an unkeyed hash let #3996's review recover a four-digit PIN in 6 ms). Neither hash is returned by any tool or viewer. The key is loaded once at service start and lives beside each distribution's other secrets: `log-hash-key.dpapi` next to `pg-credential.dpapi` on a managed store (DPAPI, the ACL above); `log-hash-key` in the `darling-credentials` volume on compose (0600, under the same directory and file checks as the role passwords); and `darling-keys/log-hash-key` (`.dpapi` on Windows) beside `darling.json` on bring-your-own. It is generated when no file exists. The service does not replace an existing key, because a new one gives every stored log event a new identity: a key file it cannot trust or read makes it refuse to collect log events, with the file and the reason in the service log, until you fix the file's access or delete it (the way to rotate). The one exception is a key directory other users could write to, such as a bind-mounted credentials directory left at `0777`: any file in it could have been planted, so the moment the service finds it open (whichever start, and whichever part of the service looks first) it sets it owner-only, removes the key and the role passwords there, and logs an error; a new key and new passwords are generated, and the first `pg_log_events` run after that says so on its Collection Log row. A directory with something in it at one of those names is left alone (the service never deletes someone else's tree, and nothing reads a directory as a credential), but everything else is still removed and the directory stays owner-only; that start uses nothing in the directory, and its error names each path to remove before you restart. A directory that keeps being opened (a Kubernetes `fsGroup` re-applied on every mount) rotates the key on every start, so keep it owner-only. Run one service per credentials volume (on Kubernetes, one replica with `strategy: Recreate`, never a rolling update): the check is not locked across processes, so a second service starting beside the first could read the directory between the first one's chmod and its removal. Keep it with the store's backups of its other credentials. Rows stored before #4004 keep their unkeyed hashes until the 30-day retention drops them, so the first cycle after the upgrade stores each entry still in the tail's overlap window once more, and a statement shape's fingerprint changes once.

**The password key** ([#5366](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/5366)). A SQL login password saved in the store is sealed to a key pair the service owns. This works on every platform, so you can save a password on Linux and in a container as well as on Windows. The store publishes the public half, and every place that saves a password seals it to that half: the Viewer, the web dashboard, the MCP tools and `--add-server`. Only the service holds the private half, so only the service can open a saved password. A sealed password opens only for the connection it was saved with. If you change a server's host, port, database, login or encryption settings, enter its password again.

- **Where the key is kept.** In the compose deployment it is the `password-key` file in the `darling-credentials` volume, with the other service credentials. On a Linux or Windows install where you bring your own PostgreSQL it is in a `darling-keys` directory beside `darling.json`. With the managed PostgreSQL on Windows it is `password-key.dpapi`, beside the other credential files. It is never in the store or its volume.
- **First start and the fingerprint.** The first start makes the key. Each start logs a `Password key` line with the key's fingerprint, as four groups of four letters and digits. The first time a Viewer saves a password to a store it shows the same fingerprint. Check that the two match. If the key later changes, the Viewer stops saving passwords. It asks you to compare the new fingerprint with the service log, then choose Trust the new key.
- **Backups.** Back up the credentials volume (or the `darling-keys` directory) after the first start and again after every reset. Keep that backup apart from the store's backups. A store restored without its key cannot open its saved passwords, and the service reports the key as missing. On Windows the key is protected with DPAPI for the machine, so its backup restores only on the same machine.
- **Resetting the key.** [`--reset-password-key`](#make-a-new-password-key---reset-password-key) retires the key at the next start and makes a new one. Every saved password must then be entered again. If the key may have been copied, reset it. Then change the SQL logins' passwords. Delete the retired key file (`password-key.retired`, or `password-key.dpapi.retired` on Windows) once you no longer need it.
- **Updating and rolling back.** Update every Viewer that saves passwords to the store. An older Viewer does not know the key and saves passwords in the old format, which the service does not use for a new row. Passwords saved on Windows before the upgrade keep working on the same machine. If you roll the service back to an older version, it cannot open the passwords saved since the upgrade. Enter them again.

**Who can read `darling.json` and the admin and viewer credentials.** SYSTEM, Administrators, the service account, and members of the local group `PerformanceMonitor Darling Operators`. No one else can, including a user who is logged on at the console. Other services, sandboxed or SSRF socket code and scheduled tasks are shut out too, and so is the group from the superuser and MCP credentials.

`install-darling.ps1` and `upgrade-darling.ps1` create the group, empty, when it is missing. Adding a member is your decision, and the answer to the installer's question is No unless you type `y`. At the end of the install, `install-darling.ps1` asks `Add <account> to 'PerformanceMonitor Darling Operators'? [y/N]`, naming the account signed in to the session. When that is not the account that ran the script (you elevated with another administrator account), the prompt says so. For a scripted install, `-AddOperator DOMAIN\user` adds that account without asking. `-NoOperatorPrompt` never asks. A host that cannot take an answer (input redirected, or PowerShell started with `-NonInteractive`) counts as No. On a domain controller no group is created and nothing is asked. `upgrade-darling.ps1` never adds a member.

After a Yes, the account also gets a direct grant of the same access the group has. The Viewer and the CLI then work in the session that is already open, with no sign-out. The grant is read on `darling.json`, read on the store's `pg-admin-credential.dpapi` and `pg-viewer-credential.dpapi`, and traverse on the store folder. The service rewrites those permissions at each start, which drops the direct grant. From then on the group covers the account only after the person has signed out and back in once. A reboot does that. A service restart or an upgrade does not, so a person still in the same session then has to sign out and back in.

`uninstall-darling.ps1` removes the grant from those four paths. To remove it sooner, run `icacls <path> /remove *<the account's SID>` on each path. On a fresh install, the service writes the credential files at its first start. The installer waits up to three minutes for them, so the grant can include them.

For everyone else, and after a No, add each person who runs the Viewer or the CLI without elevation. Use Computer Management > Local Users and Groups, or `net localgroup "PerformanceMonitor Darling Operators" DOMAIN\user /add`. A person you add that way has to sign out and back in before the membership takes effect.

Members can read `darling.json` and the store's admin and viewer credentials, which decrypt every stored password. Add only people who are trusted with those passwords.

A hand install (`sc create`, no installer) has no group until you create it. Until then the service grants only SYSTEM, Administrators and itself, and it logs a warning that names the group. Create the group, add the people, and restart the service so it grants the group. Anyone who is not a member sees a message that names the group when the Viewer or a CLI verb cannot read the file.

The installer also creates the store folder before the service starts (`%ProgramData%\PerformanceMonitorDarling` unless `postgres.dataDirectory` says otherwise). Administrators own it, and it gets the same locked ACL the service applies on every start. An ordinary user can otherwise create and own that folder first.

The service also refuses to trust a credential file that isn't owned by SYSTEM/Administrators/itself, and regenerates an untrusted role credential.

**A read-only (`viewer`) Viewer degrades gracefully.** It probes its own privileges on connect (`has_table_privilege`), so the mute-rule Add/Edit/Toggle/Delete/Purge buttons and the alert Dismiss / Dismiss All buttons are hidden or disabled, and any write that still slips through returns a clear "read-only connection" message instead of an error.

**Bring-your-own PostgreSQL.** The schema split runs everywhere (it's a migration — the service applies it on startup and best-effort sets the database `search_path`; if your collection login can't `ALTER DATABASE`, run that one statement yourself as the owner). The service provisions roles only on a managed store and on the compose store, so for any other BYO store you create them yourself, once, with the shipped script:

```
psql -X -h <host> -U <owner> -d darling -f Darling/tools/provision-roles.sql
```

Use **psql 10 or later**. The script saves and restores your `ON_ERROR_STOP` around its collision check, and only psql 10 and later read an unset one as off; in psql 9.6 the run stops at its first error, so an owner without superuser gets no grants at all. Edit the three password placeholders (and the database/owner names if yours differ) first. The `-X` skips your own `psqlrc`: an `\set AUTOCOMMIT off` in it would leave steps 3 to 5 (the write grants, the default privileges and the public hardening) uncommitted while psql still exits `0`. The roles, the passwords, the read grants, the secret-column carve and the password key table revoke commit at the script's own `COMMIT`. An `ON_ERROR_STOP` in your `psqlrc` would change where the run stops. **It creates the same three login roles, `admin`, `viewer` and `mcp`, with exactly the managed grants** (a test holds the script's grants, default privileges and role settings to the managed batch's). If a role with one of those names already exists without Darling's `darling-managed` marker (a comment on the role), the script stops at that check with an error, before it re-keys or grants anything on that role: rename or drop the other role, then run it again.

The guard also refuses a marked role that another login administers. The marker is a public string, so it does not show who made the role. From PostgreSQL 16, a role with `CREATEROLE` holds `ADMIN OPTION` on every role it creates. That option lets the holder reset the password of the role and grant the role to other roles. The script refuses a marked role when another login holds `ADMIN OPTION` on it. That login is any role that is not a superuser, not the database owner and not the role running the script. A plain membership, such as `GRANT viewer TO some_analyst`, does not count. To continue, run the script as that login, which the check leaves out. If more than one login holds the option, run the script as one of them after the others lose it. Or, as a superuser, revoke that option or drop the role. A login that created the role cannot revoke the option itself, because PostgreSQL records that grant as made by the bootstrap superuser. Then run the script again.

The guard creates the three roles without a password. The `ALTER ROLE` lines after the guard set the passwords. A run that stops at the guard therefore leaves no password in the server log. The server writes a failed statement to its log with the whole statement text. A run that succeeds also writes all three passwords to the log when `log_statement` is `ddl`, `mod` or `all`, or when `log_min_duration_statement` is `0` or more. Step 0 turns both settings off for the session of the script, and the two sampling settings `log_min_duration_sample` and `log_transaction_sample_rate` as well. It also sets `log_min_error_statement` to `panic`, so that a failed statement is not written to the log with its text, and it turns off `debug_print_parse`, `debug_print_rewritten` and `debug_print_plan`. It can do that for a superuser, and for a role that was granted `SET` on the setting (PostgreSQL 15 and later). The role and grant statements of the run are then not in the log either. When a role that may not change `log_statement` or `log_min_duration_statement` finds either one set so that the passwords are logged, the script prints a WARNING right after step 0 and goes on.

`pg_stat_monitor` is handled like `pg_stat_statements`: step 0 sets `pg_stat_monitor.pgsm_track_utility = off` for the session (any role may), and the guard stops the run if it is still on. `pgaudit` is different. Its `role` class writes `ALTER ROLE` statements to the log, with the statement text while `pgaudit.log_statement` is on, which is its default. Only a superuser can change a `pgaudit` setting, so step 0 sets `pgaudit.log_statement = off` for a superuser (or a role granted `SET` on it) and for any other role the `pgaudit` entries of the run keep their statement text. Use a `SCRAM-SHA-256` verifier in place of each password literal there.

Every run also takes back what an adopted role picked up since. It switches off `CREATEROLE`, `CREATEDB`, `REPLICATION` and `BYPASSRLS`, and it revokes every membership in another role. Managed mode does the same. The attribute statements and the membership statements run as two blocks with `ON_ERROR_STOP` on: a privilege refusal prints a WARNING and the run goes on, and any other error stops the run, so a lock timeout or a concurrent role change cannot leave a role with an attribute or membership while psql exits `0`.

The table grants, the secret-column carve and the revoke on the password key tables (step 2d) run as one transaction. If a statement in that block fails, psql prints the error and none of the block commits. Fix the cause, then run the script again. Step 3g repeats the key table revoke after the write grants of step 3, so the key tables are closed to `viewer` and `mcp` when the transaction commits and stay closed.

Any other error is printed and the run carries on with the rest of the script. This is psql's default and is deliberate. Read psql's output for errors, and run the lines that failed as a superuser.

An owner that is not a superuser can run the script, with these limits:

- The owner needs `CREATEROLE`. From PostgreSQL 16 it also needs `ADMIN OPTION` on each of the three roles, to set a password or take back an attribute. An owner holds that option only for a role it created itself. For a role that a superuser made, a superuser must first run `GRANT <role> TO <owner> WITH ADMIN OPTION`. The guard checks this before it sends a password. If the owner cannot change one of the three roles, the run stops at the guard with exit code 3. The message names the roles and the `GRANT` statements to run. The same stop happens when one of the roles is a superuser. Before PostgreSQL 16, it also happens when one of the roles has `REPLICATION`. Only a superuser can change such a role. The guard also stops the run, before a password is sent, when one of the three roles is a member of a role that would outrank the grants (the owner, a superuser or `BYPASSRLS` role, `pg_read_all_data`, `pg_write_all_data`, `pg_read_server_files`, `pg_write_server_files` or `pg_execute_server_program`) and the owner cannot revoke that membership. From PostgreSQL 16 that needs `ADMIN OPTION` on the role the membership is in, and no role has that on itself. The message names each membership. A superuser is never stopped by these checks.
- The owner cannot set `temp_file_limit` or the two slow-statement logging settings, and those lines fail.
- The owner cannot take `pg_stat_statements` from PUBLIC when a superuser created the extension (step 1b), because only the owner of the extension or a superuser may. The script and the service then leave the grants as they are, and each prints a warning that names the owner of the extension and the statements to run. Run them as that owner, in the store's database (with your schema if the extension is not in `public`, and your store owner for `darling`; drop the last four lines when there is no `pg_stat_statements_info` view):

  ```sql
  REVOKE SELECT ON public.pg_stat_statements FROM PUBLIC;
  REVOKE EXECUTE ON FUNCTION public.pg_stat_statements(boolean) FROM PUBLIC;
  GRANT SELECT ON public.pg_stat_statements TO pg_read_all_stats, darling;
  GRANT EXECUTE ON FUNCTION public.pg_stat_statements(boolean) TO pg_read_all_stats, darling;
  REVOKE SELECT ON public.pg_stat_statements_info FROM PUBLIC;
  REVOKE EXECUTE ON FUNCTION public.pg_stat_statements_info() FROM PUBLIC;
  GRANT SELECT ON public.pg_stat_statements_info TO pg_read_all_stats, darling;
  GRANT EXECUTE ON FUNCTION public.pg_stat_statements_info() TO pg_read_all_stats, darling;
  ```

  A superuser who runs the script does this itself, and grants the view to the store owner too, so the service's statement statistics keep working for an owner that is not a superuser.
- The owner cannot switch off `SUPERUSER`. From PostgreSQL 16, it can switch off `REPLICATION`, `BYPASSRLS` and `CREATEDB` only if it holds that attribute itself. From 16, it can also revoke a role membership only with `ADMIN OPTION` on the role that the membership is in. Before PostgreSQL 16, only a superuser can switch off `REPLICATION` or `BYPASSRLS`. The script prints a WARNING for each attribute or membership that it leaves in place. From PostgreSQL 16, the owner also has to be able to act as the role that granted each membership of the three roles (the grantor in `pg_auth_members`), because a revoke removes only the membership recorded with the grantor it names. The script names that grantor in each revoke. The guard stops the run when the owner cannot act as the grantor of a membership that would outrank the grants, and a run that still finds such a membership after the revokes stops with an error before any grant.
- If `pg_stat_statements` is loaded with utility tracking on, the script's first statement, `SET pg_stat_statements.track_utility = off`, fails for this owner. The run then stops at the guard, before it touches a role or a password. The stop matters because utility tracking records the password statements verbatim, where every `pg_read_all_stats` member can read them. To continue, run `GRANT SET ON PARAMETER pg_stat_statements.track_utility TO <owner>` as a superuser (PostgreSQL 15 and later), or turn utility tracking off for the cluster. Then run the script again.
- Two settings put the passwords in the server log even when the run succeeds: `log_statement` at `ddl`, `mod` or `all`, and `log_min_duration_statement` at `0` or more. Step 0 turns those settings off for the session. It cannot do that for this owner unless a superuser ran `GRANT SET ON PARAMETER <setting> TO <owner>` (PostgreSQL 15 and later); where it could not, the script prints a WARNING after step 0. A failed `ALTER ROLE ... PASSWORD` line, for example one that a password-check module rejects, is also written to the log with its password, unless step 0 could set `log_min_error_statement` to `panic` for the session. To keep the passwords out of the log, put a SCRAM-SHA-256 verifier in each `ALTER ROLE` line in place of the literal. You can also set the passwords with psql's `\password`.
- Three more cases write the same lines: `log_transaction_sample_rate` above `0`, `log_min_duration_sample` at `0` or more together with `log_statement_sample_rate` above `0`, and `pgaudit.log` covering the `role` class (`role` or `all`, and not `-role`) while `pgaudit.log_statement` is `on`. The script prints the same WARNING after step 0 when it sees any of them.

Then point the web dashboard and the MCP server at them with the two settings in the [`postgres` block](#postgres) — the service's own login stays the owner, for collection:

```json
"postgres": {
  "connectionString": "file:/etc/darling/store_owner",
  "webConnectionString": "file:/etc/darling/store_viewer",
  "mcpConnectionString": "file:/etc/darling/store_mcp"
}
```

(literal connection strings work too; the references keep the passwords out of `darling.json`). **Left unset, a surface connects as the owner** — the collection login — and none of the role-level protections applies to it: not the secret-column carve, not the narrow write grants, not the `statement_timeout` backstop. The service logs a warning at startup naming exactly that. A read-only Darling Viewer points its `connectionString` at the `viewer` role the same way. **That script is the authoritative grant list for a BYO store** — it is what actually runs, the table above is its summary, and an `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES` in it means a store gaining collectors later needs no re-grant for reading them. Re-run it after a schema upgrade to cover new tables and the single-table writes a newer migration adds.

## Opt-in Network Endpoints (LAN)

By default all three network surfaces bind **loopback only** — the store to `127.0.0.1`, the MCP server and the web dashboard to `localhost` — exactly as they always have. Three optional, independent opt-ins let a remote viewer, MCP client, or browser on your **trusted LAN** reach them. This is a home-lab / trusted-subnet feature: **never expose any of these endpoints to the internet.** The store endpoint is **managed-mode only**, and the MCP and web endpoints are managed-mode or container only (a container's port mapping is its exposure boundary, [#1804](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/1804)); elsewhere in bring-your-own mode your own PostgreSQL / reverse proxy governs exposure and the config is ignored with a warning. All three are **fail-closed** — any invalid or incomplete field degrades that endpoint back to loopback and logs a critical line rather than exposing it. Removing the config on the next restart closes the box again.

### Guided setup (`--configure-network`)

The fastest path is the interactive wizard — run it on the **service host**:

```
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --configure-network
```

It shows the current exposure (read from the service's own resolvers), then walks you through the **store**, **MCP**, the **web dashboard**, any comma combination (e.g. `1,3`), or all three at once (or a **disable** that removes all exposure). Every answer is validated **by delegation to the exact checks the running service fail-closes on**, so the wizard can never write a config the service would refuse — it re-prompts with the resolver's own reason. It generates the MCP bearer / web access tokens for you (DPAPI-protected; each plaintext is printed once, so save it then), edits `darling.json` **in place preserving every comment** behind a timestamped `darling.json.bak-<timestamp>` backup, replaces only the keys it asks about when a `network` block already exists (every other key in that block, such as the `tls` and `hostName` of the MCP block and the `tls` and `oidc` of the web block, is kept as written and named on a `Kept ... from the existing darling.json.` line), prints the scoped firewall command(s), the `--export-viewer-config` handoff, and the web dashboard's browser login URL (`http://<listen>:<port>/?token=...`, or `https://` when `web.network.tls` is set), and offers to restart the service to apply. `install-darling.ps1 -Network` runs it automatically right after the install reaches Running. The manual field reference below documents exactly what it writes.

For the MCP and web endpoints, the `allowFrom` prompt takes one range or a comma list. The wizard writes the list as one comma string. When an MCP or web endpoint is exposed, the summary adds a `TLS:` line under it. The line says whether HTTPS is on, off, or misconfigured, and it names the `mcp.network.tls` or `web.network.tls` setting. The wizard does not ask for a certificate. The `tls` blocks are file settings, and a restart applies them.

### Firewall rules (`--configure-firewall`)

The service runs as `NT SERVICE\PerformanceMonitor Darling`, an unprivileged virtual account that **cannot create Windows Firewall rules** — and should not be able to. So the rules are managed from the elevated install instead: `install-darling.ps1` runs `--configure-firewall` for you (before the first start, and again after `-Network`), and `uninstall-darling.ps1` removes them. Run it by hand after any edit to a `network` block:

```
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --configure-firewall
```

Run **elevated**. It reconciles all three scoped rules — store, MCP, web dashboard — against `darling.json` in one pass: it opens the port for every surface that really is exposed and removes the rule for every surface that is not, so it also cleans up after an exposure you turned back off. It is idempotent (safe on every upgrade).

The **port** each rule is named for comes from the control plane, not from the file. `mcp.port` / `web.port` in `darling.json` are only a first-run seed; `config.config_service.mcp_port` / `web_port` is what the endpoint actually binds, so a rule named from the file after someone moved the port in the Viewer's Settings would open a port nothing is listening on and leave the served port shut. The verb therefore reads those two columns before it plans. That read is **best effort** — the verb still has to run at install time, before the store exists, where `darling.json`'s port is the value the store will be seeded *with* — and it is never silent: when the store cannot be reached it names the port it used, why it could not do better, and the one case in which that port is wrong (a port that has since been changed in the Viewer), so re-running it once the service is up moves the rule. `--enable-mcp` / `--enable-web` have no such window: they take the port back from the store write they already perform, so they cannot open a port on a guessed number at all.

Because the port is part of the rule's **name**, changing a port does not update a rule — it makes a different one and leaves the old one standing as an inbound allow on a port nothing serves. Every removal here therefore sweeps `PerformanceMonitor Darling <surface> (port *)` rather than one exact name, so the stale rule goes with it. The wildcard is derived from a rule name this product built; a name that does not parse degrades to the exact-name removal, so a sweep can never reach a rule that is not ours.

"Really exposed" is decided by the same resolvers the running service fail-closes on, not by reading `listen` at face value. A `network` block the service would degrade to loopback — an unparseable `listen`, a missing `allowFrom`, an `allowFrom` list with one invalid entry, an address family that disagrees, a missing token, BYO mode — gets **no open port**, and the verb tells you why.

The running service never touches these rules. It **checks** them on start and logs what it finds: nothing at all for the normal loopback-only install, one INFO line when an exposed endpoint's rule is present, and one WARN naming the exact command when an exposed endpoint's rule is missing or when a loopback-only endpoint still has a stale rule open. It states each verdict once, not once per retry.

### Headless enable/disable + firewall (`--enable-mcp` / `--enable-web`)

On a box with no Viewer, two things are otherwise awkward: the `enabled` flags in the `mcp` / `web` blocks below are only a **first-run seed** — after the first run the store (`config.config_service.mcp_enabled` / `web_enabled`) is authoritative and is normally flipped only from the Viewer's Settings — and the service account (`NT SERVICE\PerformanceMonitor Darling`) **cannot open the firewall itself**. Four verbs, run on the **service host**, close both in one elevated action:

```
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --enable-mcp
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --disable-mcp
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --enable-web
PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service.exe --disable-web
```

Each flips only its endpoint's **live store flag** with a targeted `config_service` write; the service **hot-reloads within one collection sweep — no restart.** If that endpoint's `network` block opts into LAN exposure (a non-loopback `listen`), the verb also reconciles that endpoint's **scoped, idempotent-by-name firewall rule**: **run elevated**, it opens (or, on `--disable-*`, removes) the rule; **run non-elevated**, the store toggle still succeeds and it prints the exact elevated firewall command to run by hand (a loopback-only endpoint needs no rule and says so). Managed-mode only, Windows only. So the headless bring-up is: write the `network` block (the wizard above or the manual reference below), then `--enable-mcp` / `--enable-web` from an **elevated** shell.

After `--enable-web` the verb prints the address to open, for example `http://localhost:5153/`. After `--enable-mcp` it prints the `claude mcp add` command, the same one the Viewer's Settings copies, with `<token>` in place of the token. When `darling.json` sets a token, the verb also names the command that shows it: `--print-web-token` or `--print-mcp-token`. The verb never prints the token itself.

### Verify it's actually reachable (and the two failures that look like bugs)

Enabling an endpoint is **not** the same as reaching it, and both common failures leave the store flag reading `true`, so "it says enabled" is not proof. After `--enable-mcp` / `--enable-web`, verify on the **service host**:

1. **The listener is on the LAN address, not loopback.** `Get-NetTCPConnection -State Listen | Where-Object LocalPort -eq 5152` (or `5153` for web) must show the box's LAN IP, e.g. `10.0.0.5:5152` — **not** only `::1` / `127.0.0.1`. *Enabled but still loopback-bound* is the single most common failure: the store flag is on, but the service loaded `darling.json` **before** the `network` block existed. The block is read **once at service start** — the enable toggle stops/starts the endpoint with the already-loaded config and does **not** reload the file. **Restart the service** (`Restart-Service 'PerformanceMonitor Darling'`) so it re-reads the block, then re-check the listener; after the restart run `--configure-firewall` **elevated** if the firewall rule is missing (the service account cannot create it, so the service only tells you it is missing).
2. **The scoped firewall rule exists and covers the client.** `Get-NetFirewallRule -DisplayName 'PerformanceMonitor Darling MCP (port 5152)'` (or `... Web (port 5153)`) should be `Enabled=True, Action=Allow`, scoped to the `network.allowFrom` ranges. If it is absent, the service's own start-up log already says so and names the command; `--configure-firewall` elevated is the one-step fix. After you edit `allowFrom`, run `--configure-firewall` again. The service checks that the rule exists. It does not check which ranges the rule covers. Reading rules needs no elevation, so this check works from any shell.

Then from the **client** host:

3. **Connect to the box's LAN IP, never `localhost`.** Use `http://<box-LAN-IP>:5152/`, or `https://<hostName>:5152/` when `mcp.network.tls` is set (the name must be the one on the certificate). `localhost` / `127.0.0.1` only resolves *on the box itself*, so an off-box MCP client pointed at localhost fails silently — this is the number-one "MCP won't connect" cause. Send `Authorization: Bearer <token>` (the `network.token`), and do a **fresh** `initialize` + `tools/list` rather than trusting a cached tool list from a previous version.

**After a reinstall:** the installer replaces binaries but does **not** touch `darling.json` (the zip ships only `darling.sample.json`) or the store, so the `network` block and both live flags survive the upgrade — and the reinstall restarts the service, which re-reads the block. If MCP stops connecting afterward it is almost always failure 3 (the client pointed at `localhost`) or a missing firewall rule, **not** lost config: run `--configure-firewall` **elevated** to re-open the rule if check 2 comes up empty (the installer already does this, so an in-place upgrade normally leaves the rules correct). A stale loopback bind is unlikely after a restart unless the block itself is invalid, in which case the endpoint fail-closes to loopback and logs a critical line saying why — fix the block and restart again. A full `--configure-network` re-run is only needed if the `network` block itself is gone.

### Store endpoint (viewer over the LAN)

Add a `network` block to `postgres` (managed mode):

```json
"postgres": {
  "managed": true,
  "port": 5641,
  "network": {
    "listen": "192.168.1.205",
    "allowFrom": "192.168.1.0/24",
    "role": "viewer"
  }
}
```

On every start the service reconciles this against the live cluster: it adds the bind IP to `listen_addresses`, generates a self-signed TLS certificate (`server.crt` / `server.key` beside the data directory, with both an IP SAN for `listen` and a DNS SAN for the machine hostname), writes a marked `hostssl darling <role> <allowFrom> scram-sha-256` rule into `pg_hba.conf` for each admitted role and reloads, and **checks** (never creates — see [Firewall rules](#firewall-rules---configure-firewall)) that the store's scoped firewall rule matches.

- **`allowFrom`**: one range in CIDR form, with each IPv4 address written as four plain decimal numbers (no leading zeros) and no IPv6 zone index. The store takes ONE range and never a list, because the value feeds the `pg_hba.conf` rule that the service reconciles. Host bits are masked, so `192.168.1.5/24` means `192.168.1.0/24`.
- **`role`** — the pg_hba login role(s) the rule names: `"viewer"` (default, **read-only** — the secure default, covering a laptop reading every dashboard, chart, and finding), `"admin"` (full remote **writes**; the service logs a warning because `admin` holds the `config_command` / `config_monitored_servers` / `config_notification` service-credential pivot), `"sources"` (the external alert source logins, through the `darling_alert_sources` group: the line reads `hostssl darling +darling_alert_sources <allowFrom> scram-sha-256`; see [Alert sources on another machine](#alert-sources-on-another-machine)), or **several at once** — `"admin,viewer"` or `"viewer,sources"`, separated by a comma, a `+`, or whitespace. Blank still means `"viewer"` alone, and one unknown element rejects the whole value. Both writes one `hostssl` line per role inside the managed block, so an `admin` seat and any number of read-only ones reach the same store. Never the superuser, and never `all`: every generated line still names exactly one role and one CIDR, so narrowing `allowFrom` later tightens all of them together — unlike a second line hand-added outside the markers, which the reconciler preserves verbatim and therefore leaves on the old, wider CIDR. A value naming anything else is rejected **whole**, including a typo in one half of a pair: the store degrades to loopback with the reason logged rather than opening with the half that parsed. Absent or blank still means `viewer` alone. This is **distinct from `postgres.connectAs`** (the *local* VM viewer's loopback role, default `admin`): `network.role` is the *remote* role and defaults to `viewer`, so the two have opposite defaults — the local seat is writable, the remote seat is read-only, unless you say otherwise.
- **TLS is verify-full, not `require`.** Because Darling generates the cert, the client can pin it, so the connection string below uses `SSL Mode=VerifyFull` — which actually defends against an on-path MITM (`require` verifies nothing). The store's network pg_hba line is `hostssl`, so a non-TLS network client is refused.
- **The firewall is defense-in-depth, not the boundary** — pg_hba + TLS are. `--configure-firewall` (elevated) creates the store's scoped rule for you along with the other two; the equivalent by hand is:

  ```
  New-NetFirewallRule -DisplayName "PerformanceMonitor Darling store (port 5641)" -Direction Inbound -Action Allow -Protocol TCP -LocalPort 5641 -RemoteAddress 192.168.1.0/24
  ```

**That is the service side. The viewer side is [Connect a Remote Viewer](#connect-a-remote-viewer)** — `--export-viewer-config` on this host writes the viewer machine's whole configuration folder (config, certificate, and a plain-text field reference), and that section covers copying it over, the certificate's placement and rotation, and the manual `--print-viewer-connection` fallback.

### MCP endpoint (assistant over the LAN)

Add a `network` block to `mcp` (managed mode, or the compose container; `mcp.enabled` must be `true`):

```json
"mcp": {
  "enabled": true,
  "port": 5152,
  "network": {
    "listen": "192.168.1.205",
    "allowFrom": "192.168.1.0/24",
    "encryptedToken": "<output of --encrypt-password>"
  }
}
```

When `listen` is a network address **and** a token is present **and** `allowFrom` is a valid CIDR list, the MCP host binds that interface behind two gates: an **in-app CIDR check** on the remote address (checked first; loopback is always allowed, so local clients keep working) and a **required bearer token** (constant-time, no loopback exemption). An address outside `allowFrom` gets a 403 whatever token it sends. Any missing precondition keeps MCP loopback-only. A token configured with no network `listen` also keeps MCP loopback-only and gates the loopback listener: every local client sends `Authorization: Bearer <token>`, and a token that cannot be decrypted stops the MCP server from starting rather than serving it open. With no token configured, an enabled MCP server does not start unless `mcp.allowTokenlessLoopback` is true, which serves local clients with no token and with the write and investigation tools off. A POST to MCP whose Content-Type is not `application/json` is refused with 415. Prefer `encryptedToken` (a DPAPI blob from `--encrypt-password`); a plaintext `token` works for dev but is warned. `env:NAME` and `file:PATH` references are for the compose and Linux setups. A Windows service uses `encryptedToken`. Set the same scoped firewall rule for the MCP port:

**Lost the token?** `--print-mcp-token` (elevated) reprints it from `darling.json` — `--print-web-token` does the same for the dashboard. Both write the live token to **STDOUT** with every warning on STDERR, so `... --print-mcp-token | clip` captures the value and still shows the warning. This discloses nothing new: the blob is DPAPI at `LocalMachine` scope with an entropy constant published in this repository, and `darling.json` is readable by members of the local group `PerformanceMonitor Darling Operators` by design (see [Security & Least-Privilege Roles](#security--least-privilege-roles)), so any member of that group could already decrypt it. The elevation requirement makes reprinting a deliberate act; the token's actual protection is the file's ACL. If the token has **leaked** rather than been mislaid, reprinting is not the fix — `--configure-network` generates a new one, and every client configured against the old one must be updated.

```
New-NetFirewallRule -DisplayName "Darling MCP" -Direction Inbound -Action Allow -Protocol TCP -LocalPort 5152 -RemoteAddress 192.168.1.0/24
```

The token must have at least 16 characters, at least 6 of them different (`--configure-network` generates 32 random ones). A shorter or more repetitive token keeps MCP loopback-only. On the loopback bind such a token still gates every request, but the MCP write and investigation tools stay off with it, and the web dashboard serves reads only with one. The service log says why. The log says the token is shorter than the minimum, or uses too few different characters; it does not give its length. To make a strong token yourself, run this in PowerShell, which uses the system's cryptographic random generator and prints 64 hex characters: `$b = New-Object byte[] 32; [System.Security.Cryptography.RandomNumberGenerator]::Create().GetBytes($b); -join ($b | ForEach-Object { $_.ToString('x2') })`.

One address can send three wrong bearer tokens without delay. The fourth starts a wait of 2 seconds, and each further wrong token doubles the wait, up to 5 minutes. During the wait the host answers every request from that address with `429` and a `Retry-After` header. It compares no token during the wait, so a correct token is refused too. A request with no bearer token does not count as a wrong one. The same wait applies on the loopback bind, where every local client is one address. Each token is counted before it is compared, so a burst of parallel requests is counted one by one. A request that is held up only by requests from the same address that are still being compared (a client that sends several requests at once) waits for them, at most 5 seconds, instead of getting `429`; if they were right tokens it goes through, and if they were wrong it is held without a comparison. At most four wrong tokens are compared per address before its wait starts, however many requests arrive at once. A correct token gives back only its own attempt. IPv6 addresses are counted by their /64, and every loopback address (`127.0.0.0/8`, `::1`) is one address. One total budget of 60 attempts an hour holds across all addresses that have not presented a correct token during the last hour; an address that did is not held by it, though its own wait still applies. When the total budget holds, the `429` says so: the wrong tokens came from other addresses, and restarting the service clears the hold. The MCP host reads no forwarded headers, so behind a proxy every client is one address. The wait is kept in memory: restarting the service clears it.

`allowFrom` takes one range or a list. Write a list as one string with commas, for example `"10.8.0.0/16,192.168.1.0/24"`. Each entry must be in CIDR form (use `/32` for one address), with each IPv4 address written as four plain decimal numbers (no leading zeros) and no IPv6 zone index, and must match the address family of `listen`. A `::` listen takes IPv6 entries only. IPv4 clients can still reach a `::` listener, but the service never admits them there, apart from loopback, which is always allowed. For IPv4 clients, listen on `0.0.0.0` or a specific IPv4 address. The service masks host bits, so `192.168.1.5/24` means `192.168.1.0/24`. One bad entry keeps MCP loopback-only.

The wizard accepts a list and writes one comma string. `--configure-firewall` opens the port for exactly those ranges. `web.network.allowFrom` follows the same rules. `postgres.network.allowFrom` still takes one range.

A JSON array of strings also loads, but only on this version or later. An older version refuses to start on an array. It reads a comma string as an invalid range, so that listener stays loopback-only. For that reason the wizard never writes an array.

MCP can serve HTTPS itself ([#5288](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/5288)). Without it, the bearer token and every tool result cross the segment in clear text. An exposed MCP server with no `tls` block logs a warning about that at every start. The `tls` block has the same settings as `web.network.tls`. Add it to `mcp.network`:

```jsonc
"network": {
  "listen": "192.168.1.205",
  "allowFrom": "192.168.1.0/24",
  "encryptedToken": "<output of --encrypt-password>",
  "hostName": "darling.example.com",
  "tls": {
    "pfxPath": "C:\\ProgramData\\PerformanceMonitorDarling\\certs\\mcp.pfx",
    "encryptedPfxPassword": "<output of --encrypt-password>"
    // ...or a PEM pair instead: "certPath" + "keyPath"
  }
}
```

Give one form, a PKCS#12 bundle or a PEM pair, and never both. The password can sit in `encryptedPfxPassword` (DPAPI), in `pfxPassword` as a `file:` or `env:` reference, or nowhere if the bundle has no password. With a specific `listen` IP, only the network listener serves HTTPS. The two loopback listeners stay plain HTTP, and they still require the token. With `0.0.0.0` or `::`, the one listener serves HTTPS to everyone, loopback included. There is no redirect from HTTP and no second port, so a plain-HTTP client fails the handshake.

The service reads the certificate file when MCP starts. After you renew the certificate, restart the service. The product consumes a certificate and does not manage a PKI: there is no ACME and no self-signed fallback. Loading fails closed. A certificate that is missing, unreadable, ambiguous, expired, or not yet valid keeps MCP loopback-only and logs a Critical line. It never falls back to plain HTTP. The token is still required on that loopback listener. Only SYSTEM, Administrators and the service account should be able to read the key file (the PEM key, or a PKCS#12 bundle), for `web.network.tls` and `mcp.network.tls` alike, and on Windows the service logs a warning at start that names the file when ordinary users can read it.

The self-alert `MCP TLS Certificate Expiring` warns for the last 30 days before the certificate expires, and `MCP TLS Certificate Renewed` resolves it. It is separate from `Web TLS Certificate Expiring`, so one certificate shared by both endpoints raises two alerts.

Set `mcp.network.hostName` to the DNS name that clients use, for example `darling.example.com`. Write the name only, with no scheme, port, path, or wildcard. While MCP is exposed on the LAN, the `Host` header must name the listen address, `localhost`, or this name, plus loopback addresses, and any IP address when `listen` is `0.0.0.0` or `::`. The name is admitted only while MCP is exposed. The service refuses every other name with a 400. A value that is not a valid DNS name is ignored, with one warning at start. Clients then connect to `https://darling.example.com:5152/`, and the certificate must name that host.

**What a token-holder can — and cannot — do.** Start with the boundary: **no MCP tool runs SQL an AI client wrote against your monitored servers.** No such tool exists, and a stored custom view cannot become one either — a composed query names only `collect.*` collector tables in the monitoring store. The only live contact with a monitored SQL Server is `analyze_server`'s plan fetch and `add_servers`' one-time connection probe, and both run the product's own fixed, read-only queries under the same least-privilege monitoring login the collectors use — the ceiling on what they can see is the ceiling you granted that login, and it has no write grants to hit. Everything else answers from the monitoring store.

What the token does gate is the monitor's own configuration and collected data: the entire read surface, `analyze_server`, the Custom Views tools (create / modify / delete the saved dashboards and notebooks in `config.custom_views`), the alert-tuning tools (`update_alert_settings` / `create_mute_rule` / `update_mute_rule` / `delete_mute_rule` / `set_mute_rule_enabled`), `update_notification_channels` (which sets the SMTP password, the webhook URLs and the keys, sealed and never shown back), and the server-onboarding tools (`add_servers` / `remove_server`), which edit the monitored-server registry in `config.config_monitored_servers` — including storing a SQL-auth credential for a server they add. On a managed store and on the compose store, the store-side identity is still the least-privilege `mcp` role: read, the two analysis-table INSERTs, INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on the single `config.custom_views` table (the same narrow write the web composer's `viewer` role has), the narrow alert-config writes (`config.config_mute_rules` CRUD + a single-row `config.config_alert_settings` UPDATE + a single-column `config.config_notification` UPDATE for the delivery cooldown, EXECUTE on the `config.set_notification_channels` function, plus the `config_service` reload-beacon columns), and the single-table `config.config_monitored_servers` CRUD. So a token-holder can read everything collected, trigger analysis, author custom views, tune alerting, and onboard/offboard servers — and can never reach the `config_command` service-credential pivot or a service flag like `paused`, and can never READ a carved secret column back (the SMTP password, the webhook URLs and keys, and the monitored-server `encrypted_password` blob). It can WRITE some of them without reading them: onboarding stores a server's password and `update_notification_channels` stores the SMTP password, webhook URLs and keys, each sealed and never shown back. Custom-view JSON and alert config carry no secrets. Guard the token like the keys to your monitoring configuration — that is what it opens; your SQL Servers are not behind it. **On any other bring-your-own store the MCP server's store identity is whatever `postgres.mcpConnectionString` names** — the `mcp` role `provision-roles.sql` creates holds exactly the grants above — **and with that setting unset it is the store OWNER**: every limit in this paragraph is then the tools' own behaviour and none of it is enforced by the store, so a token-holder's reach is bounded only by what the tools do, not by what the login could ([#3914](https://github.com/erikdarlingdata/PerformanceMonitor/issues/3914)). The service warns at every start while that is the case.

**`add_servers` and `update_notification_channels` carry credentials in their requests.** A SQL-auth `password` (`add_servers`), or the SMTP password, webhook URLs and keys (`update_notification_channels`), ride the request JSON; the service encrypts them at rest (the server password with DPAPI, the channel values sealed) and never returns them, but on the wire it is only as protected as the endpoint — the same plaintext HTTP the token rides unless `mcp.network.tls` is set. On a segment you do not fully trust, set `mcp.network.tls` or front the MCP port with a TLS reverse proxy, and prefer Windows/integrated auth for onboarded servers where you can — then no per-server secret crosses the wire at all. With writes on, use TLS or loopback for the MCP endpoint, because the request carries these values.

If your segment is not fully trusted, serve MCP over HTTPS with `mcp.network.tls` (see above). You can also put a TLS-terminating reverse proxy in front of the MCP port and point clients at that. The in-app CIDR bounds *who can route to* the port, but it does not protect the wire. Without TLS the bearer token travels in clear text on the segment. An active on-path attacker (ARP spoof, rogue DHCP, compromised switch) can capture and replay it. The store endpoint needs neither: it has verify-full TLS built in.
